# Stage 3 detailed curriculum: 3.2 Stochastic calculus (optional), 3.3 Interview preparation, and the Stage 2 and Stage 3 exit tests

Blueprint work file, Stage 2 Phase 1. Draft 1, 6 October 2026.

**Source of truth.** `docs/curriculum.md` for stages, topics, hours and exit tests. This file expands item 3.2 Stochastic calculus (optional, "hours not counted") and item 3.3 Interview preparation (250 h) into courses, levels, lessons, homework, topic exams, reading and verification; rebuilds the 3.3 exit and the whole Stage 2 and Stage 3 exit tests in the app; proposes a completion rule for 3.2, which has no curriculum exit; and writes one lesson in full.

> **Revised by the `docs/PLAN.md` review, 7 October 2026.** These lines win over the rest of this file where they differ; content agents are briefed from them, and CI enforces them on the lesson files.
>
> | Where | Was | Now |
> |---|---|---|
> | Runtime tags (`micropython-ok`, `ulab-ok`, `web`), MicroPython CI runs, the `zqrandom` port | Drills tagged for MicroPython in the Artifact and web-only otherwise | **Void.** Pyodide 314.0.7 runs on both targets (measured on the owner's iPhone, `spikes/README.md`). Every drill declares `needs`: read `micropython-ok` as `needs: []`, `ulab-ok` as `needs: [numpy]`, and `web` as the packages the drill actually imports (NumPy, pandas, SciPy, statsmodels, scikit-learn); where it runs is decided at run time from `needs` against the build's load groups (`docs/PLAN.md` 5.7). CI runs every drill on CPython 3.12 and Pyodide only. Seeded randomness uses `random.Random(seed)` or the `zq_rng` drill helper (`docs/WIDGETS.md` 5.1.1), not a port |
> | Handed-off screens | A Parsons fallback built from the reference solution | **No Parsons fallback** on a handed-off screen: it shows the web-app link and accepts the result code. A Parsons ordering would credit ordering, not writing, and would be a second graded artefact per drill to author and verify. The small-screen Parsons mode of `code-cell` (`docs/WIDGETS.md` 5.1.1) is unaffected |
> | Sample lesson headers | `introduces` lists of 3 or 4 skills | At most 2 skills a lesson, at level grain; finer distinctions become step tags with no card (`docs/ASSESSMENT.md` 4.1) |
> | Decision 8, 3.4 | Hold-back applies to 3.3's lessons and homework; "recognised and re-solved" counts as cold, because the Stage 1 weekly bank served Mosteller warm | The weekly bank serves only `original` problems before 3.3 (`docs/ASSESSMENT.md` 10.3), so no Mosteller, Zhou, Crack or Joshi problem, nor an imitation of one, is met before 3.3. A recognised problem is logged **seen** and leaves both the numerator and the denominator; it never counts as cold |
> | Decision 13 | The Zhou gate's 20 recreated problems | Unchanged; imitations of Zhou chapters 4 and 5 serve only this held-out cold set, never the weekly bank |
> | 3.3 weekly problems | Cold from 3.3 | All three cold in Stage 3; one of three cold from 1.5 and two from Stage 2 already (`docs/ASSESSMENT.md` 10.4) |

**Built on.** `docs/design-research.md` sections 5, 6, 8, 9 and 10 (lesson anatomy, interaction catalogue, state machine, gamification, curriculum architecture, teaching style, critiques); `docs/blueprint/work/widgets-calc-linalg.md` (its section 7, the stochastic calculus composites, and its 3.2.1 to 3.2.4 working outline, which this file replaces as the 3.2 lesson outline); `docs/blueprint/work/widgets-finance-games.md` (its section 8, the interview composites, and its 3.3.1 to 3.3.5 working outline, which this file replaces as the 3.3 lesson outline; decisions 5, 6, 9, 11 and 12); `widgets-prob-stats-ts.md`, `widgets-ml-research.md`, `widgets-programming.md` and `widgets-stage0.md` for the shared inputs and the widgets this file borrows; `CONTENT-FORMAT.md` revision 2 (ids, screen types, checks, tutor fields, drills, verification); `ASSESSMENT.md` draft 2 (the ladder, homework rules, paper kinds including `cold-set`, exit parts and their schema, the Daily 10, the weekly probability problems, the book-log, mock interviews, the project record); `AI-TUTOR.md` (Bertie's availability and the interviewer mode); `canon-gap.md` revision 2 (proposal P16, which gives 3.2 its 150 hours, proposal P2 on continuous-time processes, and the Stage 3 resource canon); the sibling curriculum files in `docs/curriculum/`, whose lessons this file cites by number and does not repeat; `spikes/README.md` (Pyodide with NumPy and pandas runs inside the Artifact on the owner's iPhone); the approved prototype's course map (`prototype/js/data.js`: 3.2 "Brownian Motion and Itô"; 3.3 "Probability Puzzles", "Market-Making Games", "Mental Maths Under Pressure"); the owner's lesson-creator, maths-teaching, code-craft and project-forge skills.

**Tags.** [our choice] marks a design decision. [check] marks a resource detail (a chapter, section, lecture or problem number, a title, an edition, a URL path, a firm's test format or date) that I recall but could not confirm from this environment; confirm it before the content cites it. [recalled] marks a fact from memory that I am confident of but did not verify here. [searched] marks a fact confirmed by a web search on 6 October 2026 (direct page fetches were blocked by the egress proxy, so confirmations rest on search-result text). [unverified] marks a platform behaviour or a firm's practice not yet measured. [mode to add] marks a widget mode the catalogues do not list (open item 4).

**Answers.** Every number in sections 2 (hooks that state a number), 3, 4 and 7 was recomputed on 6 October 2026 with Python 3.11, NumPy 2.4.6, SciPy 1.17.1 and SymPy 1.14: exact `fractions` and `itertools` enumeration for every tree, card, dice and counting answer; SymPy linear solves for every chain; SciPy `quad` for densities, killed densities and maxima of Brownian motion with drift; closed forms checked against a second route (trees against expectations, formulas against seeded Monte Carlo with exact Brownian-bridge maxima, Poisson-mixture prices against simulation, posteriors against enumeration). The verification log at the end lists what was run and what changed.

**Currency in prose.** A dollar sign opens inline maths here, so prose writes "USD 100"; pounds use £. Prices in quotes and option chains are bare numbers.

**At a glance.**

| Item | Hours | Courses | Levels | Lessons | Lesson hours | Homework sets | Topic exam |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 3.2 Stochastic calculus (optional) | 150, outside the 3,100 (canon-gap P16's line) | 5 | 20 | 104 | 26.0 (17.3%) | 20 | `exm.stoch.finals`: 180 min, 8 multi-part questions, 100 marks, 40 long-form; pass 70% |
| 3.3 Interview preparation | 250 | 5 | 28 | 154 | 38.5 (15.4%) | 28 | `exm.iv.paper`: 120 min, 15 questions, 100 marks; pass 70%. Exit gate `exm.iv.zhou-cold` (kind `cold-set`): 20 recreated green-book-style problems, 120 min in two sittings; pass 80% |
| **Total** | 400 (250 counted) | 10 | 48 | 258 | 64.5 | 48 | 3 papers |

**Exits.** 3.2 has no curriculum exit; section 4.2 proposes `3.2.done` (the finals paper at 70% plus a three-pricer capstone drill) for the owner. 3.3 keeps the curriculum's three checkboxes as `3.3.zhou`, `3.3.zetamac` and `3.3.mocks` (`ASSESSMENT.md` 2.9), and section 4.4 proposes a fourth part, `3.3.books`, for the map line's "the interview books solved cold". Section 4.5 rebuilds the Stage 2 exit and section 4.6 the Stage 3 exit.

#### 1.0.2 What 3.2 and 3.3 inherit, and do not repeat

| Topic | Taught in | 3.2 or 3.3 does |
|---|---|---|
| One-step replication, risk-neutral $q$, backward induction, delta through the tree, CRR calibration, American puts, the one-array pricer | 1.7 lessons 64 to 70 | AT recaps them on one screen each and proves them: the no-arbitrage theorem (1), the wealth equation for path-dependent claims (2), martingale pricing (9), the Snell envelope (17) |
| The lognormal model, the formula as the tree's limit, two digital claims, the at-the-money shortcut, the Greeks, a day's P&L, delta hedging one path, implied volatility, the smile | 1.7 lessons 71 to 80 | IC3 derives the BSM equation and the hedging P&L formula that 1.7 lessons 77 and 78 observed; RN1 derives the formula as an expectation and reads $N(d_1)$ under the stock measure; EX3 explains the smile that 1.7 lesson 80 measured; MG5 drills all of it at interview speed |
| Reflection counts, the ballot theorem, Catalan numbers, recurrence, the scaled walk and arcsine law; martingales, optional stopping, ruin by martingales, Wald, the pattern martingale | 1.5 lessons 135 to 145 | BR3 to BR5 take them to continuous time; PZ4 uses the overlap rule at speed |
| Normal moments, MGFs, the CLT, conditional expectation and Adam's and Eve's laws, the bivariate normal | 1.5 levels V5, V6, J3, J4, J6 | BR2 generalises conditional expectation to σ-algebras; PZ5 uses bivariate normal facts |
| Kelly preview, dice stopping, the secretary problem, two envelopes, streaks | 1.5 lessons 151 to 155 | MG2 does Kelly in full; PZ2 lesson 12 does stopping with a cost per roll |
| Glosten-Milgrom, the dealer simulator, the order book, execution | 1.7 lessons 29 to 45 | MG3 turns adverse selection into the interview market-making game |
| The volatility surface, three pricers compared, Monte Carlo with confidence intervals, Breeden-Litzenberger, the PDE pricer mode | 2.3 lessons 137 to 179 (course OA) | RN3 derives what OA computed (Feynman-Kac, finite-difference stability, variance reduction); EX3 models the surface OA built |
| Taylor series, partial derivatives, first-order ODEs, the Gaussian integral | 1.3 lessons 82 to 85 (first-order ODEs), 99 to 105 (Taylor), 109 to 111 (partial derivatives) and 133 (the Gaussian integral) | IC2 uses second-order Taylor; RN3 solves the Vasicek bond ODEs; QM6 drills recall |
| Cholesky, eigenvalues, positive definiteness | 1.4 | BR3 correlates Brownian motions; QM6 drills recall; PZ5 bounds correlations |
| Estimators, regression, multiple testing, the statsmodels table | 1.6 | TR1 at interview speed |
| Time-ordered validation, leakage, regularisation, trees, boosting, PCA | 2.1 | TR2 at interview speed; TR3 adds the ML-lab depth 2.1 leaves out |
| Complexity, hashing, heaps, graphs, DP; C++ | 1.8, 1.9 | TR4 and TR5 rehearse live coding and C++ questions |
| The three projects and the replication | 2.3, 3.1 | IV2 turns them into walk-throughs and research discussions |
| Mental arithmetic to Zetamac 25 | 0.5, the Daily 10 | QM1 and QM3 take it to 50 and the 80-in-8 format |
| The weekly probability problems (warm in Stages 1 and 2) | `ASSESSMENT.md` 10 | In Stage 3 they switch to cold mode and draw on Crack, Zhou and the Jane Street guide; they stay outside the item's hours |

#### 1.0.3 Conventions for every lesson

**Ids** follow `CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 4.1. 3.2 courses: `crs.tree-pricing`, `crs.brownian`, `crs.ito`, `crs.risk-neutral`, `crs.exotics-rates`. 3.3 courses: `crs.puzzles`, `crs.mental-pressure`, `crs.mm-games`, `crs.technical`, `crs.interview`. Levels `lvl.<course>.<slug>`, lessons `lsn.<course>.<slug>`, homework `set.<course>.<level-slug>`, exams `exm.stoch.finals`, `exm.iv.paper`, `exm.iv.zhou-cold`, project `prj.stoch.lab`. Libraries use the topic namespaces `stoch` (3.2) and `iv` (3.3): `skl.stoch.*`, `mc.stoch.*`, `gen.stoch.*`, `drl.stoch.*`, and the same with `iv`. The `#` column is the lesson's number within its item; display numbers come from position and are never stored.

**Every lesson** runs the design research 8.3 sequence: a hook on a canvas; free manipulation; the name for what the learner just did; a ladder of 2 to 4 screens adding one constraint each; the trap; the rule stated as a description of the learner's own steps, with at least one derived line the learner completes; a transfer screen; the summary card; then a quiz of 4 items at 1 mark each (Mastered at 3 of 4, `ASSESSMENT.md` decision 26). From the owner's skills, every lesson also carries the "why this exists" frame, one common trap, at least one edge case, a worked example with a decision point where the naive route fails, "derive, don't state", and a motivated leap wherever a creative step appears (`derive` screens carry `leap: naive, fails, pointsTo`).

**3.2 adds two rules** [our choice]: **every continuous-time result is shown first on a discrete or simulated picture** (a tree, a refined partition, a fan of paths) before its formula, so concreteness fades from picture to notation; and **every simulation sits beside an exact answer** and is never graded on its estimate (`stage1-probability.md` decision 8).

**3.3 adds three** [our choice]: **every lesson is timed** (a soft clock on each task screen, shown in caption type, so speed is practised from the first lesson but never graded inside lessons); **every lesson has an aloud screen** where the learner explains one step in words, typed or spoken through `voice-capture`, marked against a three-point communication check (states the plan, names the tool, checks the answer); and **every hook is original** (decision 8).

**Bertie** is available on lesson screens after the first Check, after each quiz item is graded, on homework after submission and on project milestones; never during `exm.stoch.finals`, `exm.iv.paper`, `exm.iv.zhou-cold`, cold-mode weekly problems or a mock's answer phase, where he is the interviewer (`AI-TUTOR.md` 2.2 and 2.12; finance catalogue 2.9.1). His 3.2 guidance: never state a derivation's next line; prefer "what would this be on a tree?", "which terms survive as $\Delta t \to 0$?" and "is this a martingale, and under which measure?" to statements. His 3.3 guidance: behave as a good interviewer behaves, with hints that are questions ("what would you do with a smaller deck?") and never a method name before the learner's first attempt; in debriefs, quote the learner's transcript as evidence for every mark.

**Widgets.** 3.2 uses the calculus catalogue's section 7 composites (`path-simulator`, `quadratic-variation`, `reflection-principle`, `ito-sum`, `ito-lemma-lab`, `gbm-lab`, `measure-change`, `binomial-tree` (now owned by the finance catalogue), `filtration-tree`, `hedging-sim`, `heat-diffusion`, `sde-stepper`), canon-gap P2's proposed `brownian-path` and `ctmc-clock` where they exist (otherwise `path-simulator`), the finance catalogue's `bs-explorer`, `greeks-lab`, `vol-surface`, `pricer-compare`, `payoff-builder` and `yield-curve-lab`, and the shared inputs (`derivation-steps`, `numeric-input`, `expression-input`, `choice`, `matrix`, `value-table`, `plot-2d`, `plot-3d`, `slider`, `sim-runner`, `code-cell`, `free-response`). 3.3 uses the finance catalogue's section 8 composites (`puzzle-workbench`, `bet-lab`, `kelly-lab`, `mm-game`, `calibration-lab`, `mock-interview`) and primitives (`quote-entry`, `voice-capture`, `scratch-pad`, `price-ladder`), Stage 0's `speed-drill`, `strategy-trainer`, `fermi-estimator`, `multiplier-chain` and `card-sort`, the probability catalogue's `first-step-solver`, `markov-chain`, `lattice-walk`, `geo-prob`, `order-statistics`, `gaussian-2d`, `indicator-board`, `counting-slots`, `outcome-grid`, `trial-simulator`, `chance-object` and `game-lab`, the ML catalogue's `overfit-lab`, `time-cv-lab`, `regression-lab`, `ml-workbench`, `viva` and `results-note`, and the programming catalogue's `problem-workbench`, `code-cell`, `code-surface`, `bench-lab` and `setup-checklist`. Every lesson also uses the general inputs; they are listed only where they carry the idea.

**Quiz item types** (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 3.4), the letters of the sibling files: N numeric (exact fractions and closed forms accepted), X expression, C single choice, M multi-select, O order the steps, S sort or match, I interval, P point or curve on a canvas, R region selection, A matrix or vector entry, T predict the output of code, K code with tests, G reach a target state in a simulator (a quote in `mm-game`, a stake in `bet-lab`). 3.3 adds **D**, a timed deck item in `speed-drill` graded on its score against a target [our choice; `speed-drill` already reports a score]. Long-form items appear in homework and exams, never in quizzes. Single and multiple choice stay at or below 40% of each course's quiz items (design research 10); section 1.0.4 gives the counts.

**Level review.** Every level ends with a Level review of 8 items at difficulty 2 to 4, interleaved, with up to 30% from earlier levels of the same course (`ASSESSMENT.md` 2.4); in 3.3 the review runs in cold mode with a visible clock. The homework set follows (section 3).

**Columns.** Objective is one measurable sentence. Hook is the puzzle the lesson opens with, answer in brackets where it is a number. Trap is the misconception the trap screen is built around; Edge is the fringe case the lesson must show.

**Notation, 3.2** (Shreve's, with the sibling files' conventions where they differ, each difference shown on screen as a chip): $\Omega$, $\mathcal F$, $\mathcal F_n$ and $\mathcal F(t)$; $P$ the real measure and $\widetilde P$ the risk-neutral measure, $\widetilde E$ its expectation; in trees $\tilde p$ and $\tilde q$ (Shreve's; 1.7 wrote $q$ for $\tilde p$, and the chip says so), $u$, $d$, $r$ per period; $W(t)$ or $W_t$ Brownian motion, $\widetilde W$ under $\widetilde P$; $[X, X](t)$ quadratic variation; $\Delta(t)$ a position, $\Gamma(t)$ an integrand in the martingale representation (never the option Greek gamma, which is written "gamma" in words in 3.2); $\alpha$ the stock's mean rate of return, $\sigma$ volatility, $\theta$ the market price of risk (Shreve's $\Theta$); $D(t) = e^{-\int_0^t R(s)\,ds}$ the discount process; $N(\cdot)$ the standard normal CDF (1.7's convention); $Z$ a Radon-Nikodym derivative and $\zeta$ a state price density.

**Notation, 3.3**: the sibling conventions of the topic each item comes from (1.5's for probability, 1.7's for options, 1.6's for statistics), restated on the item card, because interview problems arrive without notation and choosing it is part of the answer (lesson 1).

### 1.1 Item 3.2 Stochastic calculus (optional; 150 h when taken)

#### 1.1.1 Purpose in a quant or ML career

- **It is the language of derivatives desks.** Every continuous-time price, hedge and risk number on a derivatives desk is written in it: the Black-Scholes-Merton equation, Greeks as partial derivatives of a PDE solution, risk-neutral expectations, changes of numéraire for rates and FX, local and stochastic volatility. The curriculum is right that "derivatives pricing and desk quant roles" need it and that trading and statistical research interviews rarely test it in depth.
- **It explains what 1.7 and 2.3 computed.** Why the drift of the share does not enter an option price; why a delta-hedged option earns $\frac12\Gamma S^2(\sigma_{\text{realised}}^2 - \sigma_{\text{implied}}^2)$ per unit of time; why trees, PDEs and Monte Carlo agree; why the smile exists. A learner who has only the formulas cannot say when they fail.
- **Interviews for desk quant and structuring roles ask for derivations** [recalled from interview reports]: Itô's formula for a given function, the BSM equation by replication, a price under a change of measure, the probability that Brownian motion with drift hits a barrier, the value of a perpetual American put.
- **ML labs use the same mathematics.** Diffusion models are SDEs run backwards; score matching and the Fokker-Planck equation are Feynman-Kac's cousins; Langevin dynamics is an SDE sampler; Girsanov's theorem is importance sampling for paths. The measure-theoretic level (BR1, BR2) is the floor for reading that literature [recalled].
- **It sharpens judgement about models.** Quadratic variation says volatility is observable and drift is not (lesson 43): the reason a quant trusts a volatility estimate from a month of data and distrusts a return forecast from ten years.

#### 1.1.2 Learning outcomes

At the end of 3.2 the learner can:

| # | Outcome | Measured by |
|---|---|---|
| SC-O1 | State and prove the no-arbitrage condition of the binomial model, replicate any path-dependent claim with the wealth equation, and bound prices in an incomplete one-period model | 85% on difficulty-3 items; long-form at 7 of 10 (homework AT1); `exm.stoch.finals` Q1 |
| SC-O2 | Compute conditional expectations on coin-toss space, test the martingale and Markov properties, and prove the risk-neutral pricing formula from the wealth equation | 85% on difficulty-3 items; long-form at 7 of 10 (homework AT2) |
| SC-O3 | Change measure with a Radon-Nikodym derivative and its process, price with state prices, and solve log-utility investment with them | 85% on difficulty-3 items; Q1(c) |
| SC-O4 | Price American claims by the Snell envelope, find optimal stopping times, and prove that an American call on a non-dividend share is not exercised early when rates are not negative | Long-form at 7 of 10 (homework AT4); Q2 |
| SC-O5 | Use first-passage generating functions and short-rate trees: perpetual American puts on a walk, zero-coupon bonds, caplets, forward measures, forwards against futures | 85% on difficulty-3 items; Q2(c) |

#### 1.1.3 Prerequisites

- **1.3 Calculus**, especially Taylor's theorem to second order with remainder, partial derivatives and the chain rule in two variables, improper integrals and the Gaussian integral, first-order linear ODEs.
- **1.4 Linear algebra**: Cholesky factors and positive definite matrices (correlated Brownian motions).
- **1.5 Probability**, all of it, and especially levels V5 to V7 (normal, lognormal, MGFs), J4 (conditional expectation), J6 (the central limit theorem), Z3 (random walks and the Brownian preview) and Z4 (martingales and optional stopping).
- **1.7 Finance basics**, course OP (trees, the formula, Greeks, implied volatility, the smile), and RB (discounting, zero-coupon bonds).
- **2.3 Projects**, course OA (the three pricers and the volatility surface), recommended, not required.
- **Recommended, not required:** canon-gap P2 (continuous-time Markov chains and Brownian motion in 1.5), P3 (numerical methods), P16's real analysis and measure-theoretic probability.

**Slot.** After Stage 2, beside 3.1, for a learner who opts into the derivatives add-on; before 3.3 on that route, so that PZ6 can be tested out of. **Feeds.** 3.3 (PZ6, MG5, Zhou chapters 5 and 6), the desk-quant interview rounds, and any later work on diffusion models.

### 1.2 Item 3.3 Interview preparation (250 h)

#### 1.2.1 Purpose in a quant or ML career

- **It converts knowledge into the format that is hired.** By Stage 3 the learner knows the probability, statistics, finance and code. Interviews test something narrower and harder: solving a fresh problem cold, aloud, under a clock, with a stranger marking the reasoning as it happens (curriculum 3.3: "interviewers mark the reasoning as it happens, not only the answer").
- **Each track has its rounds.** Traders: probability, mental arithmetic, market-making and betting games. Researchers: probability, statistics, regression, machine learning and a data take-home. Developers: algorithms, C++ and systems (curriculum, the track table). 3.3 rehearses all of them and weights them by track.
- **The live formats are not in the books.** The curriculum says so and lists them: Zetamac at 50, the 80-in-8 format, market-making aloud with a partner, betting and Kelly sizing, Jane Street's guide, mock interviews. Courses QM, MG and IV are built on that list.
- **Calibration is the job.** A market maker's width is an interval with a price on it; a researcher's claim needs an error bar; a trader's size is a Kelly fraction shrunk for doubt about the edge. 3.3 grades these decisions against exact answers, never against luck (decision 20).
- **ML labs interview the same way.** Research engineering and research scientist interviews at ML labs ask probability and statistics cold, derivations of gradients and losses, the reasons behind attention's scaling and initialisation rules, and live coding [recalled from public interview reports]. Level TR3 carries that end.

#### 1.2.3 Prerequisites

- **Stage 2 complete** (the curriculum's "no exit test, no next stage"), and **1.5 Probability** above all: 3.3 assumes every pattern of 1.5 and rehearses it on new problems.

- **0.5 and the Daily 10** for QM1 and QM3; the learner's 7-day Zetamac median at the start of 3.3 decides whether the speed reserve is used (decision 16).
- **3.2** is optional; a 3.2 graduate tests out of PZ6.

**Feeds.** Applications and interviews; the Stage 3 exit; the weekly probability problems in cold mode, which continue after the path ends.

---

## 2. Courses

Ten courses, 48 levels, 258 lessons. Each level is 4 to 7 lessons, then a Level review (8 items) and a homework set (section 3).

### 2.1 Item 3.2 Stochastic calculus

#### Course AT: Arbitrage on a Tree (`crs.tree-pricing`, 5 levels, 24 lessons)

Shreve volume I in five levels. 1.7 priced options on trees by recipe; this course rebuilds the binomial model as a probability space, proves that the recipe is forced by no arbitrage, and develops on finite trees every idea volume II needs in continuous time: conditional expectation as averaging over what is not yet known, martingales, Markov state variables, changes of measure and state prices, stopping times and the Snell envelope, first passage, and random interest rates. Every proof here is complete, because on a finite tree nothing can go wrong with limits. Spine: Shreve I chapters 1 to 6; Baxter and Rennie chapter 2 as a parallel.

**Level AT1: Replication on many periods** (`lvl.tree-pricing.replication`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `lsn.tree-pricing.arbitrage-theorem` No arbitrage, exactly | State an arbitrage as a portfolio with zero cost, no chance of loss and some chance of gain, and prove that the one-period model admits none exactly when $0 < d < 1 + r < u$ (one screen recaps 1.7 lessons 64 and 65) | A share at 100 moves to 110 or 95 in one period; the period's interest rate is 12%. Is there a free lunch? (Yes: short the share and lend 100; one period later you hold 112 and owe at most 110, a gain of at least 2 in both states) | `binomial-tree` (arbitrage), `position-editor`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: "an arbitrage is any trade with a positive expected profit" (it must have no chance of loss). Edge: with $1 + r = d$ exactly, buying the share with borrowed money never loses and sometimes gains, so the inequalities must be strict | C, N, O, M |
| 2 | `lsn.tree-pricing.wealth-equation` The wealth equation and path-dependent claims | Write $X_{n+1} = \Delta_nS_{n+1} + (1 + r)(X_n - \Delta_nS_n)$, solve it backwards for $\Delta_n = \frac{V_{n+1}(\omega_1\dots\omega_nH) - V_{n+1}(\omega_1\dots\omega_nT)}{S_{n+1}(\omega_1\dots\omega_nH) - S_{n+1}(\omega_1\dots\omega_nT)}$, and replicate a claim that depends on the whole path | Three periods, $S_0 = 4$, $u = 2$, $d = \frac12$, $r = \frac14$. A lookback pays the path's maximum minus the final price, $\max_{n \le 3}S_n - S_3$. Its price, and the shares held at the start? ($\frac{172}{125} = 1.376$; $\Delta_0 = \frac{13}{75} \approx 0.173$) | `binomial-tree` (path-dependent) [mode to add], `value-table` | Trap: pricing the lookback on the recombining tree of $S_3$ alone, which merges paths with different maxima. Edge: on paths where the final price is the maximum the payoff is 0, yet the claim is still worth something at those paths' earlier nodes | N, N, C, O |
| 3 | `lsn.tree-pricing.state-variables` What a price depends on: state variables and the cost of computing | Find the smallest state that makes a claim's price a function of the current node (the share price for a European claim; the pair of share price and running maximum for a lookback), and count the nodes each choice needs | A 100-step lookback tree has $2^{100}$ paths. How many distinct (share price, running maximum) states can occur at step 100 when $ud = 1$? (2,601, against 101 share prices) | `binomial-tree` (path-dependent, state view), `value-table` | Trap: "a path-dependent claim always needs the whole path" (a sufficient statistic of the path is enough). Edge: an Asian claim's running sum takes almost as many values as there are paths, so its tree does not shrink; Monte Carlo or a grid on the average is used instead (EX1) | N, C, S, N |
| 4 | `lsn.tree-pricing.incomplete-trinomial` When replication fails: a third state | Show that a one-period model with three outcomes cannot replicate a call, find the interval of prices consistent with no arbitrage from the set of risk-neutral probability vectors, and read its upper end as the cost of super-replication | A share at 100 moves to 120, 100 or 90; zero interest; a call struck at 100. Which prices exclude arbitrage? (Every price strictly between 0 and $\frac{20}3$) | `binomial-tree` (trinomial) [mode to add], `region-select`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: one price, found by averaging the three outcomes with some probabilities. Edge: the upper end, 6.67, equals the binomial price on the 120 and 90 tree, the cheapest portfolio that never pays less than the call | I, N, C, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 1's theorem is why a desk can quote a price without a view; lesson 3 is how a production lookback pricer is sized; lesson 4 is the first meeting with incomplete markets, met again with stochastic volatility (lesson 80) and jumps (lesson 103).

Then the Level review and homework `set.tree-pricing.replication`.

**Level AT2: Probability on coin-toss space** (`lvl.tree-pricing.coin-space`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 5 | `lsn.tree-pricing.coin-space` Two measures on one space | Describe the $N$-period model as $\Omega = \{H, T\}^N$ with $P(\omega) = p^{\#H(\omega)}q^{\#T(\omega)}$ and $\widetilde P(\omega) = \tilde p^{\#H(\omega)}\tilde q^{\#T(\omega)}$, and compute expectations of $S_n$ under each | In the lesson 2 tree the real chance of an up move is $\frac23$. What is the expected price after three periods under the real measure, and under the risk-neutral one? (13.5 and $\frac{125}{16} = 7.8125$) | `filtration-tree` (two measures), `value-table` | Trap: "the risk-neutral measure is a forecast" (it is the measure under which discounted prices are fair, not a belief). Edge: $P$ and $\widetilde P$ give positive mass to the same outcomes, which is all pricing needs from $P$ | N, N, C, M |
| 6 | `lsn.tree-pricing.information` Information as partitions | Identify the partition of $\Omega$ revealed after $n$ tosses, decide which events and random variables are known at time $n$ (adapted), and list the σ-algebra $\mathcal F_n$ for small $N$ | Three tosses. Known at time 2: "the first toss was H"? "$S_2 = 4$"? "the maximum over all three periods is 8"? (Yes, yes, no) | `filtration-tree` (atoms), `region-select` | Trap: "an event is known at time 2 if it can be decided after the third toss" (it must be decidable from the first two). Edge: $\mathcal F_0$ holds only $\emptyset$ and $\Omega$; $\mathcal F_3$ holds all $2^8 = 256$ events | M, S, N, C |
| 7 | `lsn.tree-pricing.conditional-expectation` Conditional expectation as averaging over the unseen | Compute $E_n[X]$ by averaging over the tosses after time $n$, and use linearity, taking out what is known, the tower property, independence and Jensen | In the same tree, after a first H, what is $\widetilde E_1[S_3]$? After a first T? (12.5; 3.125) | `filtration-tree` (conditional), `derivation-steps` | Trap: $E_n[X]$ is a number (it is a random variable, one value per atom of $\mathcal F_n$). Edge: $E_0[X] = E[X]$ and $E_N[X] = X$, the two ends of the tower | N, N, O, X |
| 8 | `lsn.tree-pricing.martingale-pricing` Martingales and the pricing formula | Prove that under $\widetilde P$ the discounted share price and the discounted value of every self-financing portfolio are martingales, and derive $V_n = \widetilde E_n\left[\frac{V_N}{(1 + r)^{N - n}}\right]$ | Which are martingales: $S_n$ under $\widetilde P$; $\frac{S_n}{(1 + r)^n}$ under $\widetilde P$; $\frac{S_n}{(1 + r)^n}$ under $P$ with $p = \frac23$? (Only the second) | `filtration-tree` (martingale), `binomial-tree` | Trap: "the share price is a martingale under the risk-neutral measure" (its discounted price is). Edge: the result holds for any $\Delta_n$ chosen with the information of time $n$, which is why every hedge's value can be priced the same way | M, C, O, N |
| 9 | `lsn.tree-pricing.markov` Markov processes and the pricing function | Test whether a process is Markov, show that the price of a claim $g(S_N)$ is $v_n(S_n)$ for a function computed backwards, and show that the running maximum alone is not Markov while the pair $(S_n, M_n)$ is | Two three-toss histories end with the same running maximum, 8: HTT (price 2) and HTH (price 8). Is the distribution of the next maximum the same for both? (No: from price 8 it can rise to 16; from price 2 it stays 8) | `filtration-tree` (Markov test), `value-table` | Trap: "a process whose future depends only on the present value is Markov" stated for the maximum, without checking. Edge: the share price is Markov under both $P$ and $\widetilde P$, because the tosses are independent under both | C, N, S, X |

Transfer screens: lesson 5 is why a pricing model's drift is not a trading signal; lesson 8 is the identity every pricing library is tested against (discounted prices of traded assets are martingales); lesson 9 sizes a pricer's state.

Then the Level review and homework `set.tree-pricing.coin-space`.

**Level AT3: State prices and changing measure** (`lvl.tree-pricing.state-prices`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 10 | `lsn.tree-pricing.radon-nikodym` The Radon-Nikodym derivative | Define $Z(\omega) = \frac{\widetilde P(\omega)}{P(\omega)}$, show $E[Z] = 1$ and $\widetilde E[X] = E[ZX]$, and state when two measures are equivalent | With $p = \frac23$ and $\tilde p = \frac12$, three tosses: $Z(\text{HHH})$ and $Z(\text{TTT})$? ($\frac{27}{64}$ and $\frac{27}8$) | `filtration-tree` (radon-nikodym) [mode to add], `value-table` | Trap: $Z = \frac{P}{\widetilde P}$, upside down. Edge: if $P(\omega) > 0$ but $\widetilde P(\omega) = 0$, $Z$ is 0 there and $\widetilde P$ is not equivalent to $P$; pricing needs equivalence | N, N, C, X |
| 11 | `lsn.tree-pricing.z-process` The Radon-Nikodym process and Bayes' formula | Compute $Z_n = E_n[Z]$, show it is a $P$-martingale, and derive $\widetilde E_n[Y] = \frac{1}{Z_n}E_n[Z_NY]$ | In the same tree, $Z_1$ after H and after T? ($\frac34$ and $\frac32$) | `filtration-tree` (radon-nikodym), `derivation-steps` | Trap: $\widetilde E_n[Y] = E_n[Z_NY]$, forgetting to divide by $Z_n$. Edge: $Z_n$ is the likelihood ratio of the first $n$ tosses, the same object as a sequential test's statistic (1.6) | N, O, X, C |
| 12 | `lsn.tree-pricing.state-prices` State prices | Define the state price density $\zeta = \frac{Z}{(1 + r)^N}$, price any claim as $E[\zeta V]$, and price Arrow-Debreu securities | What is today's price of a security that pays 1 if the three tosses are HHH and nothing otherwise? ($\frac{8}{125} = 0.064$) | `filtration-tree` (state prices), `value-table` | Trap: the price is the real probability of HHH discounted ($\frac{8}{27} \times \frac{64}{125} \approx 0.152$). Edge: the state price per unit of real probability is highest in the worst state (TTT): insurance against bad states costs most | N, N, C, S |
| 13 | `lsn.tree-pricing.optimal-investment` Optimal investment with state prices | Maximise $E[\ln X_N]$ subject to $E[\zeta X_N] = X_0$ by a Lagrange multiplier, get $X_N = \frac{X_0}{\zeta}$, and recover the replicating holding, the growth-optimal (Kelly) portfolio | One period, $X_0 = 1$, $p = \frac23$, $\tilde p = \frac12$, $r = \frac14$, $S_0 = 4$. Log-optimal wealth after H and after T, and the share of wealth held in the share? ($\frac53$ and $\frac56$; $\frac59$) | `filtration-tree` (state prices), `lagrange-explorer`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: "the best portfolio puts everything in the asset with the higher expected return". Edge: with power utility the optimal wealth is $\zeta^{-1/(1 - \gamma)}$ up to a constant; log utility is the $\gamma \to 0$ case | N, X, O, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 12 is how a desk reads the market's price of insurance; lesson 13 is Kelly (MG2) derived from prices rather than odds.

Then the Level review and homework `set.tree-pricing.state-prices`.

**Level AT4: American claims and stopping times** (`lvl.tree-pricing.american`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 14 | `lsn.tree-pricing.stopping-times` Stopping times | Decide whether a random time is a stopping time by checking that $\{\tau = n\} \in \mathcal F_n$ for every $n$ | Which are stopping times: "the first time the price reaches 8"; "the time the path reaches its maximum"; "one period before the first fall"? (Only the first) | `filtration-tree` (stopping), `region-select` | Trap: "a time defined by the path is a stopping time". Edge: a constant time and the minimum of two stopping times are stopping times | M, S, C, N |
| 15 | `lsn.tree-pricing.american-algorithm` The American algorithm | Price an American claim by $V_n = \max\left\{g(S_n), \frac{\tilde pV_{n+1}(H) + \tilde qV_{n+1}(T)}{1 + r}\right\}$, and hedge it with a portfolio that may consume | Two periods, $S_0 = 4$, $u = 2$, $d = \frac12$, $r = \frac14$, an American put struck at 5. Its price, against the European put's? ($\frac{34}{25} = 1.36$ against $\frac{24}{25} = 0.96$) | `binomial-tree` (american), `value-table` | Trap: exercising wherever the put is in the money. Edge: at the node where exercise is optimal the hedger's portfolio holds more than the continuation value; the surplus may be consumed | N, N, C, O |
| 16 | `lsn.tree-pricing.snell-envelope` The Snell envelope | Show that the discounted American price is the smallest $\widetilde P$-supermartingale above the discounted payoff, and that it is a martingale up to the optimal exercise time | In lesson 15's tree, is the discounted American put price a martingale under $\widetilde P$? (No: at the down node it is 3, while the discounted expectation of next period's values is 2) | `filtration-tree` (supermartingale), `binomial-tree` (american) | Trap: "every price process is a martingale under $\widetilde P$" (only the prices of self-financing portfolios with no consumption). Edge: if early exercise is never optimal, the envelope is a martingale and equals the European price | C, N, M, O |
| 17 | `lsn.tree-pricing.optimal-exercise` Optimal exercise and its value | Compute $\tau^* = \min\{n : V_n = g(S_n)\}$, value it as $\widetilde E\left[\frac{g(S_{\tau^*})}{(1 + r)^{\tau^*}}\right]$, and compare with suboptimal rules | Three periods in the same tree, strike 5: the American put's price, and the nodes where it is exercised? ($\frac{174}{125} = 1.392$; exercised only after a first T, at price 2, so the price-1 node after TT is never reached alive) | `binomial-tree` (american, exercise map), `region-select` | Trap: one exercise boundary in price for every time to expiry. Edge: the rule "never exercise early" is worth the European price, 0.864, a stopping time like any other | N, R, C, N |
| 18 | `lsn.tree-pricing.american-call` American calls are not exercised early | Prove that with $r \ge 0$ and no dividends an American call is worth its European twin, by Jensen's inequality on the convex payoff and the submartingale property, and find when early exercise does pay | Should you ever exercise an American call on a share that pays no dividend, with positive interest rates? (Never: selling the call is always worth at least as much) | `binomial-tree` (american, call), `derivation-steps` | Trap: "exercise when the call is deep in the money". Edge: with negative interest rates, or just before a large dividend, early exercise can be optimal; with $r \le 0$ and no dividend, an American put is never exercised early | C, O, M, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 15 is the American pricer in every options library; lesson 17's exercise map is what a trader holding American puts watches; lesson 18 is an interview question in every derivatives round.

Then the Level review and homework `set.tree-pricing.american`.

**Level AT5: Random walks and random interest rates** (`lvl.tree-pricing.rates`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 19 | `lsn.tree-pricing.first-passage-gf` First passage by an exponential martingale | Use the martingale $\left(\frac{2}{e^\sigma + e^{-\sigma}}\right)^ne^{\sigma M_n}$ and optional stopping to get $E[\alpha^{\tau_1}] = \frac{1 - \sqrt{1 - \alpha^2}}{\alpha}$ for a symmetric walk, and read $P(\tau_1 < \infty) = 1$ from $\alpha \to 1$ | A symmetric walk starts at 0. What is $E\left[\left(\frac12\right)^{\tau_1}\right]$, where $\tau_1$ is the first time it reaches 1? ($2 - \sqrt3 \approx 0.268$) | `lattice-walk` (first passage), `derivation-steps` | Trap: optional stopping applied without checking that the stopped martingale is bounded. Edge: $E[\tau_1] = \infty$ (the derivative at $\alpha = 1$ is infinite), the 1.5 lesson 139 result recovered | N, X, O, C |
| 20 | `lsn.tree-pricing.perpetual-put-walk` A perpetual American put on a walk | Price a perpetual American put on $S_n = 4 \cdot 2^{M_n}$ by choosing the exercise level $L$ that maximises $(K - L)\,E\left[(1 + r)^{-\tau_L}\right]$, using lesson 19's generating function | Strike 4, the share at 4 moves to 8 or 2 each period, $r = \frac14$, $\tilde p = \frac12$. When do you exercise, and what is the put worth now? (At price 2; worth 1) | `binomial-tree` (perpetual) [mode to add], `value-table`, `plot-2d` | Trap: exercise as soon as the put is in the money (at 4 it is worth 0 if exercised). Edge: the value function $v(S) = \frac4S$ above the boundary and $4 - S$ below it meet at 2, the discrete form of smooth pasting (lesson 93) | N, N, C, P |
| 21 | `lsn.tree-pricing.short-rate-tree` A binomial model for interest rates | Price zero-coupon bonds $B_{n,m} = \widetilde E_n\left[\frac{D_m}{D_n}\right]$ when the one-period rate $R_n$ is random, with $D_n = \prod_{k < n}(1 + R_k)^{-1}$ | The rate is 5% now and next period 10% or 2%, each with risk-neutral chance $\frac12$. A zero-coupon bond pays 1 in two periods. Its price, against discounting at the average rates 5% and 6%? (0.8998 against 0.8985) | `binomial-tree` (short rate) [mode to add], `yield-curve-lab`, `value-table` | Trap: discounting at the average future rate. Edge: the bond is worth more than the average-rate price because $\frac1{1 + R}$ is convex (Jensen): the convexity that makes long bonds' yields bend | N, N, C, X |
| 22 | `lsn.tree-pricing.fixed-income-claims` Caplets, floorlets and swaps on the tree | Price a caplet, a floorlet and a swap on a short-rate tree, and check cap-floor parity | In the same tree, a caplet pays $(R_1 - 5\%)^+$ at time 2 per unit notional. Price? ($\frac{0.5 \times 0.05}{1.05 \times 1.10} \approx 0.02165$) | `binomial-tree` (short rate), `value-table` | Trap: paying the caplet at time 1, when the rate is set (it is paid at the end of the period it covers). Edge: cap minus floor at the same strike is a payer swap, so their prices differ by the swap's value | N, N, A, C |
| 23 | `lsn.tree-pricing.forward-measure` Forward measures | Define the $T$-forward measure by $Z = \frac{D_T}{B_{0,T}}$, show that forward prices are its martingales, and compute a forward price as $\frac{S_0}{B_{0,T}}$ | An asset worth 100 today, delivered in two periods in lesson 21's tree. Forward price? ($\frac{100}{0.8998} \approx 111.14$) | `filtration-tree` (forward measure), `binomial-tree` (short rate) | Trap: forward price $= S_0(1 + R_0)^2$, as with a constant rate. Edge: under the forward measure, the expected future short rate equals today's forward rate, which is false under $\widetilde P$ | N, X, C, M |
| 24 | `lsn.tree-pricing.futures-forwards` Futures against forwards | Show that futures prices are $\widetilde P$-martingales, $\mathrm{Fut}_{n,m} = \widetilde E_n[S_m]$, while forward prices are forward-measure martingales, and sign the gap from the correlation of the asset with interest rates | An asset tends to rise when rates rise. Is its futures price above or below its forward price? (Above: the long's margin gains arrive when rates are high and can be reinvested at those rates) | `futures-mtm`, `binomial-tree` (short rate), `value-table` | Trap: "futures and forwards are the same contract with different paperwork". Edge: with deterministic rates the two prices are equal, which is why 1.7 could treat them alike | C, N, M, O |

Transfer screens: lesson 21 is a rates desk's first model; lesson 22's caplet is the most traded rate option; lesson 24 is the convexity adjustment that interest-rate futures need.

Then the Level review and homework `set.tree-pricing.rates`.

#### Course BR: Measure, Information and Brownian Motion (`crs.brownian`, 5 levels, 27 lessons)

Shreve volume II chapters 1 to 3. Continuous time needs infinitely many coin tosses, so the course first builds the measure-theoretic language at exactly the depth Shreve uses (σ-algebras, almost sure statements, the Lebesgue integral, the convergence theorems, densities as changes of measure, conditional expectation by partial averaging), then builds Brownian motion from scaled walks, proves that its quadratic variation is $T$, and solves its first-passage, maximum and exit problems. Baxter and Rennie chapter 3 is the first read; MIT 18.S096 lectures 5 and 17 are the lecture parallels.

**Level BR1: Probability on infinite spaces** (`lvl.brownian.infinite-spaces`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 25 | `lsn.brownian.why-sigma-algebras` Why σ-algebras | Explain why probabilities on $\{H, T\}^\infty$ or on $[0, 1]$ cannot be built by adding the masses of single outcomes, and state the σ-algebra axioms | Toss a fair coin for ever. What is the probability of the sequence HTHTHT...? Of every other single sequence? (0 for every one, yet the probabilities of all events still add up to 1) | `filtration-tree` (infinite), `chance-object` (coins) | Trap: "an outcome of probability 0 cannot happen". Edge: not every subset of $[0, 1]$ can be given a length consistently, so a σ-algebra lists the events that can; finance never meets the others | C, N, M, S |
| 26 | `lsn.brownian.almost-surely` Countable additivity and "almost surely" | Use countable additivity to compute probabilities of countable unions, and read "almost surely" as "outside an event of probability 0" | A number is drawn uniformly from $[0, 1]$. Chance it is rational? (0: the rationals are a countable union of single points) | `plot-2d` (interval measure), `numeric-input` | Trap: "there are infinitely many rationals, so the chance is positive". Edge: the strong law holds almost surely, yet the sequence of all heads is a possible outcome on which it fails | N, C, M, N |
| 27 | `lsn.brownian.random-variables` Random variables and their distributions | Treat a random variable as a function on $\Omega$, its distribution as a measure on $\mathbb R$, and tell "same distribution" from "same random variable" | $X$ is uniform on $[0, 1]$ and $Y = 1 - X$. Same distribution? Same random variable? $P(X = Y)$? (Yes; no; 0) | `plot-2d` (two maps), `dist-explorer` | Trap: "two random variables with the same distribution are equal". Edge: pricing needs only distributions for European claims but the joint law of the path for path-dependent ones | C, N, M, S |
| 28 | `lsn.brownian.lebesgue-integral` Expectation as a Lebesgue integral | Define $E[X]$ by approximating $X$ from below with simple random variables (partitioning the range, not the domain), and compute expectations Riemann integration cannot | $X$ is uniform on $[0, 1]$. What is $E[\mathbb 1\{X \text{ is rational}\}]$? (0; the Riemann integral of that indicator does not exist) | `plot-2d` (range partition), `value-table` | Trap: "an expectation is always a Riemann integral of a density". Edge: a random variable with $E[X^+] = E[X^-] = \infty$ has no expectation at all (the Cauchy distribution, 1.5) | N, O, C, N |
| 29 | `lsn.brownian.convergence-theorems` When limits and expectations commute | State the monotone and dominated convergence theorems, apply them, and break the exchange of limit and expectation with $X_n = n\,\mathbb 1\{U < \frac1n\}$ | $U$ is uniform on $(0, 1)$ and $X_n = n$ when $U < \frac1n$, else 0. Where does $X_n$ go, and where does $E[X_n]$ go? ($X_n \to 0$ almost surely while $E[X_n] = 1$ for every $n$) | `plot-2d` (sequence of functions), `derivation-steps` | Trap: $\lim E[X_n] = E[\lim X_n]$ always. Edge: no integrable bound exists here, because $\sup_nX_n$ behaves like $\frac1U$, whose expectation is infinite | N, C, X, M |
| 30 | `lsn.brownian.density-change` Changing measure with a density | Define $\widetilde P(A) = E[Z\,\mathbb 1_A]$ for a positive $Z$ with $E[Z] = 1$, show that $Z = e^{-\theta X - \frac12\theta^2}$ shifts a standard normal $X$ to mean $-\theta$, and use it for importance sampling | $X$ is standard normal under $P$. Reweight every outcome by $Z = e^{-X - \frac12}$. Under the new measure, what is the mean of $X$? ($-1$; $X$ is $\mathcal N(-1, 1)$ under $\widetilde P$) | `measure-change` (importance-sampling) [mode to add], `dist-explorer` | Trap: changing measure changes the variable (it changes only the probabilities of its values). Edge: estimating $P(X > 4) \approx 3.17 \times 10^{-5}$ by sampling under a shifted measure cuts the variance about 7,000-fold (homework BR1) | N, C, X, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 26 is what "the model holds almost surely" means in a pricing paper; lesson 29 is the step every "let the time step go to zero" argument in a hedge needs; lesson 30 is importance sampling for deep out-of-the-money options and rare losses.

Then the Level review and homework `set.brownian.infinite-spaces`.

**Level BR2: Information and conditioning** (`lvl.brownian.conditioning`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 31 | `lsn.brownian.generated-sigma-algebra` The information in a random variable | Describe $\sigma(X)$ as the events decided by observing $X$, define filtrations and adapted processes, and decide membership for given events | You observe only $X^2$, where $X$ is standard normal. Is the event $\{X > 1\}$ known to you? Is $\{\lvert X\rvert > 1\}$? (No; yes) | `region-select` (preimages), `filtration-tree` | Trap: "observing $X^2$ tells you $X$". Edge: $\sigma(X) = \sigma(e^X)$ because the map is one to one; $\sigma(X^2)$ is strictly smaller | M, S, C, N |
| 32 | `lsn.brownian.independence-lemma` Independence and the independence lemma | State independence of σ-algebras, and compute $E[f(X, Y) \mid \mathcal G] = g(X)$ with $g(x) = E[f(x, Y)]$ when $X$ is known and $Y$ is independent of $\mathcal G$ | $X$ is known to be 3 and $Y$ is standard normal, independent of everything known. What is $E[(X + Y)^2 \mid \mathcal G]$? (10) | `derivation-steps`, `value-table` | Trap: averaging over $X$ too. Edge: the lemma fails if $Y$ depends on the known information; it is the engine of every Markov-property proof (lessons 35, 45, 85) | N, X, C, O |
| 33 | `lsn.brownian.partial-averaging` Conditional expectation by partial averaging | Define $E[X \mid \mathcal G]$ as the $\mathcal G$-measurable variable with the same averages as $X$ on every event of $\mathcal G$, and derive its properties | $X$ and $Y$ are independent standard normals. What is $E[X \mid X + Y = 3]$? (1.5) | `gaussian-2d` (conditioning line), `derivation-steps` | Trap: "condition on $X + Y = 3$ by setting $Y = 0$". Edge: conditioning on an event of probability 0 needs this definition; the naive ratio is $\frac00$ | N, X, M, C |
| 34 | `lsn.brownian.bayes-change` Conditional expectations under a change of measure | Prove $\widetilde E[Y \mid \mathcal F(s)] = \frac{1}{Z(s)}E[YZ(t) \mid \mathcal F(s)]$ for $\mathcal F(t)$-measurable $Y$ and apply it with $Z(t) = e^{-\theta W(t) - \frac12\theta^2t}$ | $W$ is Brownian motion under $P$; $\widetilde P$ is defined by $Z(2) = e^{-W(2) - 1}$. What is $\widetilde E[W(2) \mid \mathcal F(1)]$? ($W(1) - 1$) | `measure-change` (paths), `derivation-steps` | Trap: dividing by $Z(t)$ instead of $Z(s)$. Edge: with $s = 0$ the formula is lesson 30's $\widetilde E[Y] = E[YZ]$ | X, N, O, C |
| 35 | `lsn.brownian.markov-continuous` Martingales and Markov processes in continuous time | State the martingale and Markov properties with filtrations, and show that the running maximum of Brownian motion is not Markov while the pair (position, running maximum) is | You know a path on $[0, 1]$. Does $E[f(M(2)) \mid \mathcal F(1)]$, where $M$ is the running maximum, depend on $W(1)$ alone? (No: on $W(1)$ and $M(1)$) | `path-simulator` (running max), `filtration-tree` | Trap: "every function of a Markov process is Markov". Edge: $\lvert W\rvert$ is Markov although it is a function of $W$, because Brownian motion is symmetric | C, X, M, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 32 is how a desk computes the value of a claim given today's prices; lesson 33 is Kalman filtering's core step for Gaussian signals; lesson 34 is the step inside every Girsanov proof (lesson 72).

Then the Level review and homework `set.brownian.conditioning`.

**Level BR3: Building Brownian motion** (`lvl.brownian.construction`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 36 | `lsn.brownian.scaled-walk` Scaled random walks | Define $W^{(n)}(t) = \frac1{\sqrt n}M_{nt}$, find its increments' means and variances, and show its limit at a fixed time is $\mathcal N(0, t)$ by the MGF (recap of 1.5 lesson 140 on one screen) | In $W^{(100)}$, what values can $W^{(100)}(0.25)$ take, and what is its variance? (Odd multiples of 0.1 from $-2.5$ to 2.5; variance 0.25) | `path-simulator` (scaled), `dist-explorer` | Trap: scaling space by $\frac1n$ (the limit is a flat line). Edge: the limit keeps independent increments because the walk's steps are independent, the property the definition will demand | N, N, C, X |
| 37 | `lsn.brownian.definition` Brownian motion defined | State the definition (start at 0, continuous paths, independent increments, $W(t) - W(s) \sim \mathcal N(0, t - s)$), derive $\mathrm{Cov}(W(s), W(t)) = \min(s, t)$, and compute with the multivariate normal | $\mathrm{Cov}(W(2), W(5))$? $\mathrm{Var}(W(5) - 2W(2))$? (2; 5) | `path-simulator` (fan), `covariance-lab`, `matrix` | Trap: treating $W(2)$ and $W(5)$ as independent (giving 13). Edge: the vector $(W(t_1), \dots, W(t_k))$ is jointly normal with covariance matrix $\min(t_i, t_j)$, which is positive definite (1.4) | N, N, A, C |
| 38 | `lsn.brownian.invariances` What else is a Brownian motion | Show that $-W(t)$, $\frac1{\sqrt c}W(ct)$, $W(s + t) - W(s)$ and $tW(\frac1t)$ are Brownian motions, and find what fails in look-alikes | $Z$ is standard normal and $X(t) = \sqrt tZ$, so $X(t) \sim \mathcal N(0, t)$ for every $t$. Is $X$ a Brownian motion? (No: $\mathrm{Cov}(X(1), X(4)) = 2$, not 1) | `path-simulator` (transforms), `covariance-lab` | Trap: "the right marginal distributions make a Brownian motion". Edge: time inversion is a Brownian motion, which turns statements near time 0 into statements near infinity | C, N, M, S |
| 39 | `lsn.brownian.bridge` Building paths by halving: the Brownian bridge | Derive the conditional law of $W(\frac{s + t}2)$ given $W(s)$ and $W(t)$, build a path by midpoint refinement, and use the bridge's crossing probability to correct barrier simulations | $W(0) = 0$ and $W(1) = 1$. What is the distribution of $W(\frac12)$? ($\mathcal N(\frac12, \frac14)$) | `path-simulator` (bridge) [mode to add], `derivation-steps` | Trap: a conditional variance of $\frac12$, forgetting the end point's information. Edge: between grid points a path crosses a barrier $b$ with chance $e^{-2(b - x)(b - y)/(\sigma^2\Delta t)}$; ignoring it overprices knock-out options (lesson 90) | X, N, C, N |
| 40 | `lsn.brownian.correlated` Correlated Brownian motions | Build $W_2 = \rho W_1 + \sqrt{1 - \rho^2}B$ from independent motions, generalise with a Cholesky factor, and compute variances of combinations | Two shares are driven by Brownian motions with correlation 0.6. What is $\mathrm{Var}(W_1(1) + W_2(1))$? (3.2) | `path-simulator` (2D), `quadratic-form`, `matrix` | Trap: correlating the paths by sharing a fraction of the same draws without the square root, which gives the wrong variance. Edge: with $\rho = 1$ the second motion is the first, and with $\rho = -1$ it is the mirror image | N, A, X, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 37 is the covariance every Monte Carlo pricer samples; lesson 39 is the barrier correction in production pricers; lesson 40 is how a basket's paths are generated.

Then the Level review and homework `set.brownian.construction`.

**Level BR4: Roughness: variation and martingales** (`lvl.brownian.variation`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 41 | `lsn.brownian.first-variation` Infinitely long paths | Compute $E\sum_j\lvert\Delta W_j\rvert = \sqrt{\frac{2nT}{\pi}}$ for $n$ equal steps on $[0, T]$, and conclude that Brownian paths have infinite length on every interval | Split $[0, 1]$ into 100 equal steps. Expected total distance a Brownian path travels? (About 7.98, and it grows like $\sqrt n$) | `quadratic-variation` (first variation), `path-simulator` (zoom) | Trap: "a continuous path has finite length". Edge: infinite first variation is why $\int f\,dW$ cannot be a Riemann-Stieltjes integral (lesson 52) | N, C, X, M |
| 42 | `lsn.brownian.quadratic-variation` Quadratic variation: why $dW\,dW = dt$ (section 7, in full) | Show that $\sum_j(\Delta W_j)^2$ has mean $T$ and variance $\frac{2T^2}n$ for $n$ equal steps, conclude $[W, W](T) = T$, and use the table $dW\,dW = dt$, $dW\,dt = 0$, $dt\,dt = 0$ | For the smooth path $f(t) = 3t$ on $[0, 1]$ with 4 steps, $\sum(\Delta f)^2$? With 1,000 steps? ($\frac94$; 0.009: it vanishes). Now predict the same sum for a Brownian path (about 1, for every fine partition) | `quadratic-variation`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: the sum of squared increments vanishes, as for smooth paths. Edge: drift does not change quadratic variation; $\sigma W$ has $\sigma^2T$; $W(2t)$ has $2T$ | N, N, M, S |
| 43 | `lsn.brownian.realised-variance` Volatility is observable, drift is not | Show that $\sum(\Delta\ln S)^2 \to \sigma^2T$ whatever the drift, and compare the standard errors of estimated volatility and estimated drift from the same data | A share has volatility 20% a year. With one year of daily data, what are the standard errors of the estimated drift and of the estimated volatility? (About 20% for the drift, useless; about 0.9% for the volatility) | `quadratic-variation` (realised), `gbm-lab` | Trap: "more frequent data estimates the drift better" (only a longer span does). Edge: microstructure noise in tick data biases realised variance upwards at the highest frequencies, which is why 5-minute returns are common [recalled] | N, N, C, M |
| 44 | `lsn.brownian.martingales` Martingales of Brownian motion | Prove that $W(t)$, $W(t)^2 - t$ and $e^{\sigma W(t) - \frac12\sigma^2t}$ are martingales with the independence lemma, and test candidates | Which are martingales: $W(t)^2$; $W(t)^2 - t$; $e^{W(t)}$; $e^{W(t) - t/2}$; $W(t)^3$? ($W(t)^2 - t$ and $e^{W(t) - t/2}$) | `path-simulator` (average), `derivation-steps` | Trap: "zero expected value makes a martingale" ($W(t)^3$ has mean 0 but $E[W(t)^3 \mid \mathcal F(s)] = W(s)^3 + 3(t - s)W(s)$). Edge: $W(t)^3 - 3tW(t)$ is a martingale (lesson 58) | M, X, N, C |
| 45 | `lsn.brownian.markov-property` The Markov property and the transition density | Show $E[f(W(t)) \mid \mathcal F(s)] = g(W(s))$ with $g(x) = \int f(y)\,p(t - s, x, y)\,dy$, and check that $p$ solves the heat equation | Given $W(1) = 2$, what is $E[W(3)^2 \mid \mathcal F(1)]$? (6) | `path-simulator` (from a point), `heat-diffusion` (kernel) | Trap: forgetting the variance term (answering 4). Edge: $p(\tau, x, y)$ satisfies $p_\tau = \frac12p_{yy}$, the first meeting with the PDE side of the subject (lesson 86) | N, X, C, O |

Transfer screens: lesson 42 is the reason Itô calculus differs from ordinary calculus; lesson 43 is why risk models trust volatility estimates and distrust return forecasts; lesson 44's exponential martingale is the density of every change of measure.

Then the Level review and homework `set.brownian.variation`.

**Level BR5: Hitting times and the maximum** (`lvl.brownian.hitting`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 46 | `lsn.brownian.first-passage` First passage by optional stopping | Use the exponential martingale and optional stopping to get $E[e^{-\alpha\tau_m}] = e^{-m\sqrt{2\alpha}}$, and deduce that $\tau_m$ is finite almost surely with infinite mean | Will Brownian motion ever reach 1? Expected time? $E[e^{-\tau_1}]$? (Yes, almost surely; infinite; $e^{-\sqrt2} \approx 0.243$) | `reflection-principle` (hitting), `derivation-steps` | Trap: "it surely arrives, so the expected time is finite". Edge: the argument needs $\sigma > 0$ in the martingale and the bound $e^{\sigma W(t \wedge \tau_m)} \le e^{\sigma m}$ | N, C, X, N |
| 47 | `lsn.brownian.reflection` The reflection principle | Derive $P(\tau_m \le t) = 2P(W(t) \ge m)$ by reflecting the path after its first touch, and get the density of $\tau_m$ | Chance that Brownian motion rises to 1 at some time before time 1? ($2(1 - N(1)) \approx 0.317$) | `reflection-principle` | Trap: $P(W(1) \ge 1) \approx 0.159$, forgetting the paths that touch and fall back. Edge: by the same reflection, $P(W(1) \le 0,\ \max_{t \le 1}W(t) \ge 1) = P(W(1) \ge 2) \approx 0.023$ | N, N, C, X |
| 48 | `lsn.brownian.joint-maximum` The joint law of the maximum and the endpoint | Derive the joint density of $(M(t), W(t))$, show $M(t)$ has the law of $\lvert W(t)\rvert$, and compute expectations of the maximum | Expected maximum of Brownian motion over $[0, 1]$? ($\sqrt{\frac2\pi} \approx 0.798$) | `reflection-principle` (joint), `plot-3d` | Trap: $E[M(1)] = E[W(1)] = 0$. Edge: the density vanishes on the line $w = m$ only at $m = 0$; the support is $w \le m$, $m \ge 0$ | N, X, C, N |
| 49 | `lsn.brownian.exit-interval` Leaving an interval, with and without drift | Compute $P(\text{hit } a \text{ before } -b)$ and the expected exit time for $W$ ($\frac b{a + b}$ and $ab$) and for $X(t) = \mu t + \sigma W(t)$ through the martingales $e^{-2\mu X(t)/\sigma^2}$ and $X(t) - \mu t$ | $X(t) = 0.1t + W(t)$ starts at 0. Chance it hits 1 before $-1$? Expected exit time? (0.550; 0.997) | `reflection-principle` (drift) [mode to add], `first-step-solver` (continuous analogue) | Trap: using $e^{X(t)}$ as the martingale for drifted motion. Edge: as $\mu \to 0$ the drifted answers tend to $\frac12$ and 1, the driftless ones | N, N, X, C |
| 50 | `lsn.brownian.maximum-drift` The maximum of Brownian motion with drift | Show that for $\mu < 0$, $P(\sup_tX(t) \ge m) = e^{2\mu m/\sigma^2}$, and read it as a probability of ruin or of a drawdown | A strategy's P&L drifts at $-0.5$ a year with volatility 1. Chance it ever stands 1 above its start? ($e^{-1} \approx 0.368$) | `reflection-principle` (drift), `path-simulator` | Trap: "with negative drift it never rises above its start". Edge: with $\mu \ge 0$ the probability is 1 | N, C, N, M |
| 51 | `lsn.brownian.zeros` Zeros and the arcsine law | Show that Brownian motion has no zero in $(s, t)$ with chance $\frac2\pi\arcsin\sqrt{\frac st}$, and that its zero set near 0 is infinite | Chance that Brownian motion has no zero between times 1 and 2? ($\frac12$) | `path-simulator` (zero set), `reflection-principle` | Trap: "a path that crossed zero at time 1 will cross again soon, so almost surely". Edge: started at 0, it returns to 0 infinitely often in every interval $(0, \varepsilon)$ | N, C, M, X |

Transfer screens: lesson 47 is the closed form behind barrier and one-touch prices (lesson 90); lesson 49 sizes the chance a market maker's inventory hits a limit; lesson 50 is the chance a strategy with a known Sharpe ratio ever reaches a drawdown limit.

Then the Level review and homework `set.brownian.hitting`.

#### Course IC: Itô Calculus (`crs.ito`, 4 levels, 20 lessons)

Shreve volume II chapter 4: the prototype's "Brownian Motion and Itô" course, renamed for what it now holds. The Itô integral as the gain from trading, built from left-point sums; the isometry; Itô's formula from a second-order Taylor expansion and quadratic variation; Itô processes, GBM and Ornstein-Uhlenbeck; the Black-Scholes-Merton equation derived by replication; many dimensions and simulation. MIT 18.S096 lecture 18 (Itô calculus) is the lecture parallel [searched].

**Level IC1: The Itô integral** (`lvl.ito.integral`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 52 | `lsn.ito.why-new-integral` Why ordinary integration fails | Compute left-point and right-point sums of $\int_0^TW\,dW$ on refining partitions, find that they converge to different limits, $\frac12(W(T)^2 - T)$ and $\frac12(W(T)^2 + T)$, and name quadratic variation as the gap | On one path with 10,000 steps over $[0, 1]$, the left sum is 0.21 and the right sum is 1.21. What is their difference close to, and why? (1, the sum of squared increments) | `ito-sum` (left, right) | Trap: "the evaluation point does not matter in the limit", true for smooth integrators. Edge: for $\int f(t)\,dW$ with smooth deterministic $f$ the choice does not matter, because $\sum\Delta f\,\Delta W \to 0$ | N, C, X, M |
| 53 | `lsn.ito.simple-integrands` Simple integrands are trading strategies | Define $I(t) = \sum_j\Delta(t_j)[W(t_{j+1}) - W(t_j)]$ for adapted step functions, read it as the gain from holding $\Delta(t_j)$ shares, and show it is a martingale | A trader who could see each next increment would hold $\mathrm{sign}(\Delta W_j)$. Expected gain over $[0, 1]$ with 100 steps? (About 7.98, growing without limit as steps shrink) | `ito-sum` (strategy), `path-simulator` | Trap: an integrand that looks at $W(t_{j+1})$ (peeking is not allowed: adaptedness is the no-insider rule). Edge: an adapted strategy's expected gain is exactly 0, however clever | N, C, M, O |
| 54 | `lsn.ito.isometry` The Itô isometry | Prove $E[I(t)^2] = E\int_0^t\Delta(u)^2\,du$ for simple integrands and use it for variances of integrals | What is $\mathrm{Var}\left(\int_0^1W(t)\,dW(t)\right)$? ($\frac12$; check: $\frac12(W(1)^2 - 1)$ has variance $\frac12$) | `ito-sum` (many paths), `derivation-steps` | Trap: $\mathrm{Var} = \int_0^1E[W(t)]^2\,dt = 0$. Edge: cross terms vanish because each increment is independent of everything known when it starts | N, O, X, C |
| 55 | `lsn.ito.gaussian-integrals` Integrals of deterministic functions | Show that $\int_0^Tf(t)\,dW(t) \sim \mathcal N\left(0, \int_0^Tf(t)^2\,dt\right)$, and rewrite $\int_0^TW(t)\,dt$ as $\int_0^T(T - t)\,dW(t)$ | Distributions of $\int_0^1t\,dW(t)$ and of $\int_0^1W(t)\,dt$? Their covariance? (Both $\mathcal N(0, \frac13)$; covariance $\frac16$) | `ito-sum` (deterministic), `covariance-lab` | Trap: $\mathrm{Var}\int_0^1W\,dt = \int_0^1t\,dt = \frac12$, forgetting the correlation of the path with itself. Edge: the average of a Brownian path is Gaussian, which is why a geometric Asian option has a closed form (lesson 92) | X, N, N, C |
| 56 | `lsn.ito.stratonovich` Midpoints, Stratonovich and the choice finance makes | Show that the sum with the average of the endpoint values gives exactly $\frac12W(T)^2$ on every partition, relate Stratonovich and Itô integrals, and explain why finance uses Itô's | On any partition, $\sum\frac12(W_j + W_{j+1})(W_{j+1} - W_j)$ equals what? ($\frac12W(T)^2$ exactly: it telescopes) | `ito-sum` (average), `derivation-steps` | Trap: "Stratonovich is the correct integral because the chain rule works". Edge: a trader cannot hold the average of today's and tomorrow's price, so gains from trading are Itô integrals | X, C, O, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 53 is a P&L as an integral; lesson 55 is the variance of an average price, the input of Asian option pricing; lesson 56 is why physics and finance write the same SDE differently.

Then the Level review and homework `set.ito.integral`.

**Level IC2: Itô's formula** (`lvl.ito.formula`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 57 | `lsn.ito.formula-w` Itô's formula for $f(W(t))$ | Derive $df(W) = f'(W)\,dW + \frac12f''(W)\,dt$ from Taylor's theorem and quadratic variation, and use it for moments | Does $E[W(t)^2]$ grow, and how fast? What is $E[W(t)^4]$? ($t$; $3t^2$, from $d(W^4) = 4W^3\,dW + 6W^2\,dt$) | `ito-lemma-lab`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: $d(W^2) = 2W\,dW$, the ordinary chain rule. Edge: for convex $f$ the $dt$ term is positive: $f(W)$ drifts upwards on average although $W$ does not | X, N, C, O |
| 58 | `lsn.ito.formula-tw` Itô's formula for $f(t, W(t))$ and martingale tests | Use $df = (f_t + \frac12f_{xx})\,dt + f_x\,dW$, and find functions that make $f(t, W(t))$ a martingale by solving $f_t + \frac12f_{xx} = 0$ | Find $a(t)$ with $a(0) = 0$ such that $e^{W(t) + a(t)}$ is a martingale. ($a(t) = -\frac t2$) | `ito-lemma-lab` (martingale), `derivation-steps` | Trap: $a(t) = -t$. Edge: $W(t)^3 - 3tW(t)$ and the Hermite polynomials in $(t, W)$ are martingales; each solves the backward heat equation | X, X, M, C |
| 59 | `lsn.ito.processes` Itô processes | Define $X(t) = X(0) + \int\Delta\,dW + \int\Theta\,du$, compute $[X, X](t) = \int\Delta^2\,du$, and apply Itô's formula to $f(t, X(t))$ with $dX\,dX = \Delta^2\,dt$ | $X(t) = \int_0^ts\,dW(s) + t^2$. What is $dX\,dX$, and $[X, X](1)$? ($t^2\,dt$; $\frac13$) | `ito-lemma-lab` (process), `value-table` | Trap: including the drift in the quadratic variation. Edge: $\int\Theta\,du$ has zero quadratic variation, so drift never enters $[X, X]$ | X, N, C, S |
| 60 | `lsn.ito.gbm` Geometric Brownian motion | Solve $dS = \alpha S\,dt + \sigma S\,dW$ by Itô's formula for $\ln S$, and separate the mean growth $\alpha$ from the median growth $\alpha - \frac12\sigma^2$ (recap of 1.7 lesson 71 on one screen) | $\alpha = 10\%$ and $\sigma = 40\%$ a year. Expected growth rate of $\ln S$? (2% a year, not 10%) | `gbm-lab`, `ito-lemma-lab` | Trap: $d\ln S = \alpha\,dt + \sigma\,dW$. Edge: with $\sigma^2 > 2\alpha$ almost every path tends to 0 while $E[S(t)]$ grows | N, X, C, M |
| 61 | `lsn.ito.product-rule` The product rule and mean reversion | Use $d(XY) = X\,dY + Y\,dX + dX\,dY$, solve the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck equation $dR = (\alpha - \beta R)\,dt + \sigma\,dW$ with the integrating factor $e^{\beta t}$, and read its mean, variance and half-life | $\beta = 0.5$, $\sigma = 1$, $\alpha = 0$, $R(0) = 0$. Long-run variance and half-life? (1; $\frac{\ln 2}{0.5} \approx 1.39$) | `sde-stepper` (Ornstein-Uhlenbeck), `derivation-steps` | Trap: forgetting $dX\,dY$, or the variance $\sigma^2t$ of Brownian motion for a mean-reverting process. Edge: the discretised process at a fixed step is an AR(1) with coefficient $e^{-\beta\Delta t}$ (2.2) | N, N, X, C |
| 62 | `lsn.ito.levy` Lévy's characterisation | State that a continuous martingale with $[M, M](t) = t$ is a Brownian motion, and use it to recognise Brownian motions built as integrals | Is $\int_0^t\mathrm{sign}(W(s))\,dW(s)$ a Brownian motion? Is $\int_0^tW(s)\,dW(s)$? (Yes; no, its quadratic variation is $\int_0^tW^2\,ds$) | `ito-sum` (quadratic variation), `path-simulator` | Trap: "any martingale with variance $t$ is a Brownian motion" (the quadratic variation must be $t$ path by path, and paths continuous). Edge: a compensated Poisson process has variance $\lambda t$ but jumps, so it is not one (lesson 103) | C, M, X, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 60 is the volatility drag that makes funds holding a fixed multiple of an index lose value in sideways markets; lesson 61 is the Vasicek short rate and the pairs-trading spread of 3.1; lesson 62 is how the risk-neutral Brownian motion of lesson 72 is recognised.

Then the Level review and homework `set.ito.formula`.

**Level IC3: Black-Scholes-Merton by replication** (`lvl.ito.bsm`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 63 | `lsn.ito.portfolio-dynamics` A self-financing portfolio in continuous time | Write $dX = \Delta\,dS + r(X - \Delta S)\,dt$ and show $d(e^{-rt}X) = \Delta\,d(e^{-rt}S)$ | A portfolio holds one share bought entirely with borrowed money. Is it self-financing, and what is its discounted value's differential? (Yes; $d(e^{-rt}S)$, since $X = 0$ at the start and the loan grows at $r$) | `hedging-sim` (wealth), `derivation-steps` | Trap: adding $S\,d\Delta$ to $dX$ (rebalancing costs nothing in a self-financing portfolio; that term is financed). Edge: the discrete-time wealth equation of lesson 2 becomes this one as the period shrinks | X, C, O, N |
| 64 | `lsn.ito.bsm-equation` The Black-Scholes-Merton equation | Match the $dW$ and $dt$ terms of $d(e^{-rt}X)$ and $d(e^{-rt}c(t, S))$ to get $\Delta = c_x$ and $c_t + rxc_x + \frac12\sigma^2x^2c_{xx} = rc$ with $c(T, x) = (x - K)^+$ | Why does the share's mean return $\alpha$ not appear in the equation? (It enters both sides through $\Delta\,dS$ and cancels once $\Delta = c_x$) | `derivation-steps`, `hedging-sim` | Trap: setting the $dt$ terms equal before choosing $\Delta$ to kill the $dW$ terms. Edge: the boundary conditions $c(t, 0) = 0$ and $c(t, x) \approx x - Ke^{-r(T - t)}$ for large $x$ close the problem | O, C, X, M |
| 65 | `lsn.ito.verify-bs` Checking the formula against the equation | Verify that the Black-Scholes price solves the equation, and use the identity $\Theta + rS\Delta + \frac12\sigma^2S^2\Gamma = rV$ to compute one Greek from the others | An at-the-money one-year call with $S = K = 100$, $r = 5\%$, $\sigma = 20\%$ has price 10.4506, delta 0.6368 and gamma 0.01876. Its theta per year? ($-6.414$) | `bs-explorer`, `greeks-lab` | Trap: theta as the price's change per day without the year conversion, or with the wrong sign. Edge: deep in the money, a European put's theta can be positive, which the identity explains (the $rV$ term dominates) | N, N, C, X |
| 66 | `lsn.ito.hedging-pnl` Hedging with the wrong volatility | Derive that a delta hedge at implied volatility $\sigma_i$ earns $\frac12\Gamma S^2(\sigma_r^2 - \sigma_i^2)\,dt$ when the realised volatility is $\sigma_r$ (1.7 lesson 78 observed it) | A book is long gamma 0.02 per share at $S = 100$; implied volatility 20%, realised 30%. Expected hedged P&L per trading day per share, with 252 trading days a year? (About 0.0198: $\frac12 \times 0.02 \times 100^2 \times (0.09 - 0.04) = 5$ a year) | `hedging-sim` (wrong vol), `pnl-attribution` | Trap: "a delta-hedged option has no exposure to volatility". Edge: hedging at the realised volatility instead makes the P&L deterministic, equal to the price difference at the two volatilities (homework IC3) | N, C, X, M |
| 67 | `lsn.ito.linear-pde` Linear equations, portfolios and power claims | Use linearity to show that forwards, shares and put-call parity satisfy the equation, and price a claim on $S(T)^2$ by guessing a solution | A claim pays $S(T)^2$ at $T = 1$, with $S(0) = 100$, $r = 5\%$, $\sigma = 20\%$. Price? ($10^4e^{0.05 + 0.04} \approx 10{,}941.7$) | `derivation-steps`, `heat-diffusion` | Trap: $S(0)^2e^{rT}$, as if the square were a traded share. Edge: a claim on $S(T)^p$ is worth $S(0)^pe^{(p - 1)(r + \frac12p\sigma^2)T}$, a power-law check on any pricer | N, X, C, O |

Transfer screens: lesson 64 is the equation every desk's PDE pricer solves; lesson 66 is the P&L of volatility trading (2.3 OA7, MG5); lesson 67's power claim is a test case for the Stochastic Lab.

Then the Level review and homework `set.ito.bsm`.

**Level IC4: Many dimensions and simulation** (`lvl.ito.multidim`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 68 | `lsn.ito.multivariable` Multivariable Itô | Apply Itô's formula to $f(t, X, Y)$ with $dW_1\,dW_2 = \rho\,dt$, and compute covariations | $W_1$ and $W_2$ have correlation $\rho$. What is $d(W_1W_2)$, and $E[W_1(t)W_2(t)]$? ($W_1\,dW_2 + W_2\,dW_1 + \rho\,dt$; $\rho t$) | `ito-lemma-lab` (two), `matrix` | Trap: $dW_1\,dW_2 = 0$ for correlated motions. Edge: independent motions have zero covariation, so their product is a martingale | X, N, C, A |
| 69 | `lsn.ito.ratio-basket` Ratios and baskets of two shares | Derive the volatility of $\frac{S_1}{S_2}$ as $\sqrt{\sigma_1^2 + \sigma_2^2 - 2\rho\sigma_1\sigma_2}$ and approximate a basket's volatility | $\sigma_1 = 30\%$, $\sigma_2 = 20\%$, $\rho = 0.5$. Volatility of the ratio? (26.5%) | `path-simulator` (ratio) [mode to add], `quadratic-form` | Trap: $\sigma_1 - \sigma_2 = 10\%$. Edge: with $\rho = 1$ and equal volatilities the ratio has zero volatility; a basket of lognormals is not lognormal, so its option needs an approximation or simulation | N, X, C, M |
| 70 | `lsn.ito.euler-maruyama` Simulating SDEs: Euler-Maruyama and its orders | Discretise an SDE by Euler-Maruyama, measure strong error (order $\frac12$) and weak error (order 1) against exact GBM on the same increments | GBM with $\alpha = 10\%$, $T = 1$: Euler's mean $(1 + 0.01)^{10}$ against $e^{0.1}$, then with 20 steps. How fast does the error shrink? (0.00055, then 0.00028: halving the step halves the weak error) | `sde-stepper` (orders), `code-cell` | Trap: one order for every error measure. Edge: Milstein's correction $\frac12\sigma^2S(\Delta W^2 - \Delta t)$ raises the strong order to 1 for GBM, because the scheme now includes the quadratic-variation term | N, N, C, K |
| 71 | `lsn.ito.covariation-data` Covariation from data | Estimate covariation $[X, Y]$ by summing products of increments, read correlation from it, and explain why correlation estimated from very short returns of asynchronously traded shares is biased towards zero (the Epps effect) | Two shares have correlation 0.6 in daily returns. Measured from 10-second returns it comes out near 0.2. Model failure or measurement? (Measurement: trades are not simultaneous, so many short-interval products miss each other's moves) [recalled] | `path-simulator` (two, asynchronous) [mode to add], `quadratic-variation` (covariation) | Trap: "more frequent data always estimates covariance better". Edge: refresh-time sampling, which samples both at the times both have traded, removes most of the bias [recalled] | C, N, M, X |

Transfer screens: lesson 69 is the volatility of a spread and of an exchange option (lesson 95); lesson 70 is the convergence test every Monte Carlo pricer needs; lesson 71 is why risk models use daily, not intraday, correlations.

Then the Level review and homework `set.ito.multidim`.

#### Course RN: Risk-Neutral Pricing and the PDE (`crs.risk-neutral`, 3 levels, 18 lessons)

Shreve volume II chapters 5 and 6. Girsanov's theorem turns the real measure into the risk-neutral one by changing drift, not volatility; prices become discounted expectations; the martingale representation theorem supplies the hedge; the fundamental theorems say when prices are consistent and unique.
