| 79 | `lsn.mm-games.kelly-odds` Kelly for any odds | Derive $f^* = p - \frac{1 - p}b$ by maximising $p\ln(1 + bf) + (1 - p)\ln(1 - f)$, and check it against 1.5 lesson 154's even-money case | A bet wins with chance 0.4 and pays 2 to 1. Kelly fraction? (10%) | `kelly-lab` (curve) | Trap: "a bet that loses more often than it wins should never be taken". Edge: with $p = 0.3$ and the same odds the edge is negative ($f^* < 0$): do not bet | N, X, P, C |
| 80 | `lsn.mm-games.overbetting` Over-betting and fractional Kelly | Show that twice Kelly grows at about zero and half Kelly keeps about three quarters of the growth, and read the drawdown trade-off | A 55% even-money bet: growth per bet at full Kelly (10%), half Kelly and twice Kelly? (0.50%, 0.38%, about $-0.01\%$) | `kelly-lab` (compare, fractional) | Trap: "Kelly is the safe choice" (full Kelly has a 50% chance of halving wealth at some point [recalled from Thorp's approximation]). Edge: the three-quarters rule is exact only for small edges; here it gives 74.9% | N, N, C, M |
| 81 | `lsn.mm-games.simultaneous` Several outcomes and simultaneous bets | Allocate Kelly stakes over several outcomes and over simultaneous independent bets by maximising expected log wealth | Two independent even-money bets, each winning with chance 0.6, settle at the same time. Kelly stake on each? (19.2% each, less than the 20% for one bet alone) | `kelly-lab` (simultaneous, multi), `mass-editor` | Trap: adding the individual Kelly fractions (`sum-individual-kellys`). Edge: if all the bets can lose together the total stake must stay below 100%, so with 10 such bets Kelly stakes about 10.0% on each and 99.9% in all | N, N, C, X |
| 82 | `lsn.mm-games.uncertain-edge` An uncertain edge | Size on the posterior mean of the edge, not on the raw estimate, and explain why selection makes estimated edges too large | You won 55 of 100 even-money bets. A sceptical prior, Beta(50, 50), centres on no edge. Posterior mean and Kelly fraction, against the raw ones? (0.525 and 5%, against 0.55 and 10%) | `kelly-lab` (uncertain), `dist-explorer` (Beta update) | Trap: betting the raw estimate. Edge: for a single bet, expected log growth is linear in $p$, so the posterior mean is exactly the right input; the shrinkage comes from the prior, not from a separate penalty | N, N, C, M |
| 83 | `lsn.mm-games.continuous-kelly` Continuous Kelly and the Sharpe ratio | Derive $f^* = \frac{\mu - r}{\sigma^2}$ from the log-wealth drift $r + f(\mu - r) - \frac12f^2\sigma^2$, and its growth $r + \frac12S^2$ | Excess return 6% a year, volatility 20%. Kelly position and the extra growth? (1.5 times wealth, borrowing the rest; 4.5% a year) | `kelly-lab` (continuous), `ito-lemma-lab` | Trap: $f^* = \frac{\mu - r}\sigma$, the Sharpe ratio itself. Edge: an error of 3 points in the estimated excess return halves the position or raises it by half (0.75 or 2.25 times wealth; 3.2 lesson 43: drift is hard to estimate) | N, X, C, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 80 is why funds size below Kelly; lesson 83 links position size to the Sharpe ratio a researcher reports.

Then the Level review and homework `set.mm-games.sizing`.

**Level MG3: Making markets** (`lvl.mm-games.making-markets`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 84 | `lsn.mm-games.quote-anatomy` A two-sided market | Read and write a quote ("7 at 8, 10 up": bid, offer, size), centre it on the expected value and state its width | Make a market on the sum of two dice, to be rolled after the trade. (Centred on 7, for example 6.5 at 7.5) | `mm-game` (anatomy, dice), `quote-entry` | Trap: centring on the most likely value without checking it is also the mean (here both are 7). Edge: for the maximum of two dice the mode is 6 and the mean 4.47; centre on the mean | G, N, C, S |
| 85 | `lsn.mm-games.width` Width from uncertainty | Set width from the quantity's standard deviation and from how much you lose to an informed counterparty, and narrow it as information arrives | Make a market on the number of heads in 10 tosses. How wide, and why wider than for the sum of two dice relative to the mean? (Centred on 5 with standard deviation 1.58, against 7 and 2.42 for two dice: the coin market should be the tighter of the two) | `mm-game` (width), `calibration-lab` | Trap: a fixed width for every quantity. Edge: a market one tick wide on a quantity the counterparty knows exactly loses on every trade | G, N, C, M |
| 86 | `lsn.mm-games.update-on-trade` What a trade tells you | Update the fair value after being lifted or hit by a counterparty who is informed with a known probability | One die, hidden. The counterparty is informed half the time (buys only if the value exceeds your offer) and otherwise buys or sells at random. You quote 3 at 4 and they buy at 4. New fair value? (4.3, up from 3.5) | `mm-game` (update), `odds-ledger` | Trap: ignoring the trade (`ignore-being-lifted`) or jumping to the top of the range. Edge: if the counterparty is never informed, the trade tells you nothing and the value stays 3.5 | N, G, C, X |
| 87 | `lsn.mm-games.inventory` Inventory and skew | Skew quotes against inventory with a reservation price $r = s - q\gamma\sigma^2(T - t)$ (Avellaneda and Stoikov, 3.1), and say which side you want filled | Mid 100, inventory $+5$, risk aversion 0.1, variance 4 per unit time, half a unit of time left. Reservation price? (99: quote around it so that you sell more often than you buy) | `mm-game` (inventory), `price-ladder` | Trap: skewing the wrong way (raising both quotes when long, `skew-wrong-way`). Edge: as the end approaches, $T - t \to 0$ and the skew vanishes, because there is less time for the price to move against you | N, G, C, M |
| 88 | `lsn.mm-games.sequential` Re-quoting as information arrives | Recompute the expected value and width after each reveal and move the market at once | A market on the total of three cards dealt from a deck (ace 1 to king 13). Before any reveal, and after the first card shows a king? (21; about 26.8) | `mm-game` (sequential), `chance-object` (cards) | Trap: adding 13 to the old mean of the other two (getting 27.0, forgetting the king is gone from the deck). Edge: after all three cards are revealed the width is zero; the value is known | N, G, N, C |
| 89 | `lsn.mm-games.fermi-markets` Markets on estimates | Make a market on a Fermi quantity with a calibrated 90% interval as its width, and update on the counterparty's trades | Make a market on the number of commercial flights in the air worldwide right now. (Graded on the interval's calibration and the update after being lifted) [check the fact bank's figure] | `mm-game` (fermi), `fermi-estimator`, `calibration-lab` | Trap: an interval that is too narrow (`overconfident-narrow`). Edge: on a log scale, a market of 5,000 at 20,000 is centred near 10,000 | G, I, C, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 86 is Glosten-Milgrom (1.7 lesson 43) as an interview game; lesson 87 is the inventory term of every market-making model.

Then the Level review and homework `set.mm-games.making-markets`.

**Level MG4: Trading games** (`lvl.mm-games.trading-games`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 90 | `lsn.mm-games.linked-markets` Linked markets | Keep quotes on related quantities consistent, and find the arbitrage when they are not | Markets: $X$ 3.2 at 3.8, $Y$ 3.2 at 3.8, $X + Y$ 7.8 at 8.4. Arbitrage? (Yes: buy $X$ and $Y$ at 3.8 each, sell $X + Y$ at 7.8, a riskless 0.2) | `mm-game` (linked), `bet-lab` (arbitrage) | Trap: checking only the centres (3.5, 3.5, 8.1). Edge: consistency needs the sum's bid at most the sum of the offers and its offer at least the sum of the bids | G, N, C, M |
| 91 | `lsn.mm-games.stale-quotes` Stale quotes and several counterparties | Re-quote immediately after news, and measure what a stale quote costs against sharp counterparties | In lesson 88's game your offer is 23 when a king is revealed. What happens, and what does it cost per lot? (A sharp counterparty lifts it at once: about 3.8 per lot) | `mm-game` (multi-bot) | Trap: waiting to see whether others move. Edge: pulling the quote is also an answer; in the interview game you are expected to re-quote, not vanish | N, G, C, S |
| 92 | `lsn.mm-games.figgie` Inference in a trading game: Figgie | Infer the likely goal suit in Figgie from your hand by Bayes over the twelve deck compositions, and trade on it | A Figgie deck has 40 cards: one suit of 12, two of 10, one of 8; the goal suit is the same colour as the 12-card suit. Your 10 cards: 4 spades, 3 clubs, 2 hearts, 1 diamond. Most likely goal suit? (Clubs, about 38%: spades is most likely the 12-card suit, and the goal is its partner) [searched for the rules] | `mm-game` (figgie) [mode to add], `odds-ledger` | Trap: "I hold most spades, so spades is the goal". Edge: the goal suit has 8 or 10 cards, never 12, which is why holding many of the long suit is a signal about its partner | N, C, M, S |
| 93 | `lsn.mm-games.partner-aloud` Market-making aloud with a partner | Run the spoken protocol ("bid 7 for 10, at 8"), confirm trades, keep a blotter and swap roles | Your partner says "I'll buy 5 at your 8" when your market is 7 at 8 for 10. Your position and the next quote? (Short 5 at 8; move the market up, for example 7.5 at 8.5) | `mm-game` (partner), `voice-capture`, `value-table` (blotter) | Trap: ambiguous quote language ("8 for 10" heard as an offer). Edge: an interviewer who trades only at one side repeatedly is testing whether you update | G, S, C, O |
| 94 | `lsn.mm-games.game-debrief` Debriefing a game: edge against P&L | Score each trade's edge against the posterior at the time, separate it from luck, and write three things to change | You lost money but every trade had positive edge against the information at the time. Good or bad game? (Good: P&L is one draw; edge is the decision) | `mm-game` (review), `results-note` (short form) | Trap: judging by P&L. Edge: a game with positive P&L and negative edge on most trades is a warning, not a success | C, S, N, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 90 is index arbitrage in miniature; lesson 92 is reading a market's composition from your own holdings; lesson 94 is how a desk reviews a day.

Then the Level review and homework `set.mm-games.trading-games`.

**Level MG5: Options at interview speed** (`lvl.mm-games.options-speed`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 95 | `lsn.mm-games.quote-arbitrage` Arbitrage in option quotes | Find violations of parity, monotonicity, call-spread bounds and convexity (the butterfly) in a list of quotes, and write the trade | Calls struck at 90, 100 and 110 on one share and expiry trade at 14, 8.5 and 2. Arbitrage? (Yes: the butterfly $14 - 2 \times 8.5 + 2 = -1$ costs less than nothing; buy it and receive 1 for a payoff that is never negative) | `option-bounds`, `payoff-builder` | Trap: checking only that prices fall as strikes rise. Edge: with quotes 14, 8 and 3 the butterfly costs 1 and there is no arbitrage | N, C, M, G |
| 96 | `lsn.mm-games.greeks-intuition` Greeks without the formula | Predict deltas, gammas, vegas and thetas for options across strikes and expiries without computing, and check against `greeks-lab` | An at-the-money one-month call, zero rates, 20% volatility: delta? (Slightly above one half: about 0.51) | `greeks-lab`, `bs-explorer` | Trap: exactly 0.5 at the money. Edge: a digital's delta peaks at the money and grows without limit as expiry approaches, which is why digitals are hedged as call spreads | N, C, M, S |
| 97 | `lsn.mm-games.digitals-skew` Digitals, call spreads and the skew | Price a digital as minus the slope of the call price in strike, including the skew term $-\nu\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial K}$ | Share and strike 100, one year, zero rates, at-the-money volatility 20%, skew $-0.1$ volatility points per unit of strike. Digital call with and without the skew? (0.500 against 0.460) | `bs-explorer` (digital), `vol-surface` | Trap: pricing the digital with $N(d_2)$ at the at-the-money volatility. Edge: with a positive skew the digital is cheaper than $N(d_2)$ | N, N, C, X |
| 98 | `lsn.mm-games.gamma-scalping` Long gamma: earning realised against implied | Explain the P&L of a delta-hedged long straddle as $\frac12\Gamma S^2(\sigma_r^2 - \sigma_i^2)$ per unit of time (3.2 lesson 66), and what changes without hedging | You buy a straddle at 20% implied volatility and the share then moves with 25% realised volatility. Delta-hedged, do you expect to make or lose money? Unhedged? (Make; unhedged the P&L depends on where the share ends, not on the path's volatility) | `hedging-sim` (long gamma), `pnl-attribution` | Trap: "long options always lose to time decay". Edge: hedging at discrete times leaves noise around the expected P&L, not a bias | C, M, N, S |
| 99 | `lsn.mm-games.basket-vs-options` A basket of options against an option on a basket | Prove $\left(\sum_iw_iS_i - K\right)^+ \le \sum_iw_i(S_i - K_i)^+$ for strikes with $\sum w_iK_i = K$, and say what correlation does to the gap | Which is worth more: a call on an index, or the index-weighted sum of calls on its members at matching strikes? (The sum of calls; the gap is larger when correlation is lower) | `payoff-builder` (basket), `covariance-lab` | Trap: "they are the same, the index is just the weighted sum". Edge: with correlation 1 and equal volatilities the two are equal; the gap is what dispersion trades sell and buy [recalled] | C, M, X, N |
| 100 | `lsn.mm-games.what-if` What happens to the price if... | Sign the effect on calls and puts, American and European, of changes in volatility, rates, dividends, time and the share price, and give the reason in one line | An American call on a share that pays a large dividend tomorrow, deep in the money. Exercise today? (Possibly: if the dividend exceeds the call's time value plus the interest on the strike, exercise just before the ex-date is optimal) | `bs-explorer` (inputs), `binomial-tree` (dividend) | Trap: "never exercise an American call early", without its condition (no dividends, 3.2 lesson 18). Edge: with negative rates, early exercise of a call can pay even without dividends | C, M, S, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 95 is the first check an options market maker runs on a quote screen; lesson 97 is why digitals are priced off the skew; lesson 99 is the logic of dispersion and correlation trading.

Then the Level review and homework `set.mm-games.options-speed`.

#### Course TR: Technical Rounds: Statistics, Machine Learning and Code (`crs.technical`, 6 levels, 33 lessons)

The rounds the curriculum's track table names for researchers ("statistics, regression, machine learning, a data take-home") and developers ("algorithms, C++, systems design"), plus the ML-lab depth the owner asks for. 1.6, 2.1, 1.8 and 1.9 taught the material; this course rehearses the questions interviewers ask about it, at their speed, and adds the derivations an ML lab expects from memory: gradients by hand, signal propagation, optimiser stability, attention's scaling, entropy and KL divergence. Books worked alongside: Zhou chapter 7 (algorithms and numerical methods), and Joshi, Denson and Downes on the developer track; Stefanica, Radoičić and Wang otherwise (section 5.2).

**Level TR1: Statistics questions** (`lvl.technical.statistics`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 101 | `lsn.technical.estimators-speed` Estimators in interview form | Derive maximum likelihood and unbiased estimators for uniform, exponential and Bernoulli models in under three minutes, and compare their errors | Serial numbers 23, 57, 81 and 102 are seen, drawn without replacement from $1, \dots, N$. Estimate $N$. (126.5, the minimum-variance unbiased estimate $m\left(1 + \frac1k\right) - 1$; the maximum likelihood estimate is 102) | `puzzle-workbench`, `dist-explorer` | Trap: twice the sample mean, 131.5, which ignores the maximum. Edge: the maximum likelihood estimate is biased low, because the true maximum is never below the sample's | N, X, C, M |
| 102 | `lsn.technical.regression-puzzles` Regression puzzles | Answer the standard regression puzzles (the slopes of $y$ on $x$ and $x$ on $y$ multiply to $R^2$; duplicating data leaves coefficients and shrinks standard errors by $\sqrt2$; adding a regressor never lowers in-sample $R^2$) with one-line reasons | Regressing $y$ on $x$ gives slope 2 and $R^2 = 0.5$. Slope of $x$ on $y$? (0.25, not 0.5) | `regression-lab` (swap axes), `puzzle-workbench` | Trap: the reciprocal of the slope. Edge: the two fitted lines coincide only when $\lvert\rho\rvert = 1$ | N, C, M, S |
| 103 | `lsn.technical.omitted-variables` Omitted variables and confounding | Derive the omitted-variable bias $\beta_2\frac{\mathrm{Cov}(x_1, x_2)}{\mathrm{Var}(x_1)}$ and sign it in examples | True model $y = x_1 + 2x_2 + \varepsilon$; $x_1$ and $x_2$ have equal variances and correlation 0.5. Slope from regressing $y$ on $x_1$ alone? (2) | `regression-lab` (omitted), `covariance-lab` | Trap: "the slope on $x_1$ is still 1, just noisier". Edge: if the omitted variable is uncorrelated with $x_1$, the slope is unbiased and only its standard error grows | N, X, C, M |
| 104 | `lsn.technical.testing-traps` p-values and multiple testing at speed | Compute family-wise error rates for independent tests, apply Bonferroni, and say what a p-value is and is not | Twenty independent tests of true null hypotheses at the 5% level. Chance of at least one rejection? (0.642) | `multiple-testing`, `puzzle-workbench` | Trap: 5%, or "the p-value is the probability the null is true". Edge: for strategy searches the tests are correlated, so Bonferroni is conservative and the right correction is a resampling one (2.3 RM4) | N, N, C, S |
| 105 | `lsn.technical.sample-size` Sample sizes and the square-root law | Size an A/B test or a backtest from the effect size, the noise and the power, with $n \propto \frac{(z_\alpha + z_\beta)^2\sigma^2}{\delta^2}$ | Detect a rise in conversion from 10% to 11% at 5% significance (two-sided) with 80% power. Per-arm sample size? (About 14,750) | `sampling-lab`, `puzzle-workbench` | Trap: about 1,000, from $\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt n} = 1\%$ without the power term and the two arms. Edge: halving the effect quadruples the sample, which is why small edges need years of data | N, C, X, N |
| 106 | `lsn.technical.sharpe-law` Sharpe ratios, information ratios and breadth | Use the fundamental law $\mathrm{IR} \approx \mathrm{IC}\sqrt{\text{breadth}}$, the standard error of a Sharpe ratio $\approx\sqrt{\frac{1 + \frac12S^2}T}$, and the Sharpe ratio of combined strategies | An information coefficient of 0.02 on 500 shares rebalanced monthly. Information ratio? (About 1.55: breadth 6,000) | `sharpe-lab`, `signal-lab` | Trap: counting breadth as 500, the number of shares, without the rebalancing. Edge: breadth counts independent bets, so 500 correlated shares are fewer than 500 bets | N, N, C, X |

Transfer screens: lesson 104 is the first question a sceptical reader asks of a backtest; lesson 105 is how long a strategy must run before its Sharpe ratio means anything.

Then the Level review and homework `set.technical.statistics`.

**Level TR2: Machine-learning questions** (`lvl.technical.ml`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 107 | `lsn.technical.regularisation` Bias, variance and regularisation at interview speed | Give the closed forms of ridge and lasso for an orthonormal design (shrink by $\frac1{1 + \lambda}$; soft-threshold at $\lambda$) and explain why the lasso's corners give zeros | Orthonormal design, least-squares coefficient 3, penalty $\lambda = 2$ (lasso with $\frac12\lVert y - X\beta\rVert^2 + \lambda\lVert\beta\rVert_1$; ridge with $\frac12\lVert y - X\beta\rVert^2 + \frac\lambda2\lVert\beta\rVert^2$). The two estimates? (1 and 1) | `overfit-lab`, `regression-lab` (penalty paths) | Trap: "ridge sets small coefficients to zero". Edge: with coefficient 1.5 the lasso gives 0 and ridge 0.5: the lasso selects, ridge shrinks | N, N, C, X |
| 108 | `lsn.technical.leakage-questions` Leakage and validation questions | Diagnose leakage from symptoms (an AUC of 0.99, a feature built with future data, scaling fitted on all the data) and fix the protocol | A fraud model scores AUC 0.99 in cross-validation. What do you check first? (Leakage: a feature recorded after the outcome, or duplicate customers across folds) | `time-cv-lab`, `ml-workbench` (leakage audit) | Trap: celebrating. Edge: random folds on time series leak through autocorrelation even with no future feature; purging and embargo (2.3 RM5) fix it | C, S, M, O |
| 109 | `lsn.technical.ensembles` Trees, forests and boosting | Explain why bagging reduces variance by $\rho\sigma^2 + \frac{(1 - \rho)\sigma^2}B$ and boosting reduces bias, and when each fails | 100 trees, each with prediction variance 1 and pairwise correlation 0.3. Variance of their average? (0.307) | `ml-workbench` (ensembles), `covariance-lab` | Trap: $\frac1{100}$, ignoring correlation. Edge: random forests sample features at each split to lower $\rho$, which is the point of the method | N, C, X, S |
| 110 | `lsn.technical.logistic-loss` Logistic regression and cross-entropy | Derive $\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\left[-y\ln\sigma(z) - (1 - y)\ln(1 - \sigma(z))\right] = \sigma(z) - y$, and explain why squared error is a poor loss for probabilities | What is that derivative? ($\sigma(z) - y$) | `derivation-steps`, `plot-2d` | Trap: $(\sigma(z) - y)\sigma'(z)$, the squared-error gradient's form. Edge: on separable data the maximum-likelihood weights diverge; regularisation or early stopping keeps them finite | X, C, O, M |
| 111 | `lsn.technical.dimensionality` PCA and the curse of dimensionality | Compute how volume concentrates near the boundary in high dimensions, explain why nearest neighbours degrade, and read a PCA scree plot | In 100 dimensions, what fraction of the unit cube's volume lies inside the central cube of side 0.9? ($0.9^{100} \approx 2.7 \times 10^{-5}$) | `pca-lab`, `plot-2d` | Trap: "90% of each side, so most of the volume". Edge: the same effect makes random high-dimensional vectors nearly orthogonal, which embedding methods use | N, N, C, X |
| 112 | `lsn.technical.metrics` Metrics: precision, recall, AUC and calibration | Compute precision and recall from rates and base rates, read AUC as $P(\text{score of a random positive} > \text{score of a random negative})$, and tell discrimination from calibration | Scores for three positives 0.9, 0.6, 0.4 and three negatives 0.7, 0.3, 0.2. AUC? ($\frac79 \approx 0.78$) | `ml-workbench` (metrics), `card-sort` | Trap: accuracy as the measure for a rare class. Edge: a model can have a high AUC and badly calibrated probabilities; a monotone recalibration fixes calibration without changing AUC | N, N, C, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 108 is the first line of a model review; lesson 112's calibration is what a position sizer needs from a classifier's probabilities.

Then the Level review and homework `set.technical.ml`.

**Level TR3: ML-lab depth** (`lvl.technical.ml-lab`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 113 | `lsn.technical.backprop-hand` Backpropagation by hand | Compute every gradient of a two-layer network on one example by the chain rule, and check it by finite differences | $y = w_2\,\mathrm{relu}(w_1x)$ with $x = 2$, $w_1 = 0.5$, $w_2 = -1$, target 1, loss $\frac12(y - 1)^2$. $\frac{\partial L}{\partial w_1}$ and $\frac{\partial L}{\partial w_2}$? (4 and $-2$) | `derivation-steps`, `code-cell` (finite differences) | Trap: dropping the relu's derivative or the factor $x$. Edge: with $w_1 < 0$ the relu is off and both gradients are 0: a dead unit | N, N, X, K |
| 114 | `lsn.technical.initialisation` Initialisation and signal propagation | Derive $\mathrm{Var}(\text{output}) = n\,\mathrm{Var}(w)\,\mathrm{Var}(x)$ for a linear layer, and the $\frac1n$ and $\frac2n$ rules for tanh-like and relu layers | 512 inputs of variance 1, weights of variance 0.01. Output variance? After ten such layers? (5.12; about $1.2 \times 10^7$) | `plot-2d` (layer variances), `code-cell` | Trap: "small weights are always safe" (they shrink the signal instead). Edge: relu halves the second moment, which is where the factor 2 in $\frac2n$ comes from | N, N, C, X |
| 115 | `lsn.technical.optimisers` Optimisers and step sizes | Show that gradient descent on a quadratic converges exactly when $\eta < \frac2{\lambda_{\max}}$, and explain momentum and Adam's per-coordinate scaling | Gradient descent on $f(x) = 5x^2$. Largest step size that converges? (0.2) | `plot-2d` (iterates), `iteration-cobweb` | Trap: "a larger learning rate always learns faster". Edge: with curvatures 1 and 100, the best fixed step converges at rate $\frac{99}{101}$ per step: ill-conditioning, which preconditioning and Adam address | N, C, X, M |
| 116 | `lsn.technical.attention` Attention: scaling, cost and softmax | Explain why attention divides by $\sqrt d$ (a dot product of $d$ unit-variance terms has variance $d$), its $O(n^2d)$ cost, and the effect of softmax temperature | Query and key entries are independent with variance 1 in $d = 64$ dimensions. Variance of their dot product, and the scale applied? (64; divide by 8) | `derivation-steps`, `plot-2d` (softmax saturation) | Trap: dividing by $d$. Edge: without the scale, softmax saturates and its gradients vanish for large $d$ | N, N, C, M |
| 117 | `lsn.technical.information-theory` Entropy, cross-entropy and KL divergence | Compute entropy and KL divergence, prove $\mathrm{KL} \ge 0$ by Jensen, and show that minimising cross-entropy is maximum likelihood | Entropy of a fair die in bits? $\mathrm{KL}(\mathrm{Bern}(0.5) \,\Vert\, \mathrm{Bern}(0.25))$ in nats? (2.585; 0.144) | `derivation-steps`, `convexity-chord` | Trap: KL is symmetric (the reverse divergence is 0.131 nats). Edge: KL is infinite when the second distribution gives zero probability to an event the first does not, which is why smoothing is used | N, N, C, X |

Transfer screens: lesson 113 is the computation every deep-learning framework automates (1.3's automatic differentiation); lesson 117's KL is the term in every variational objective.

Then the Level review and homework `set.technical.ml-lab`.

**Level TR4: Coding rounds** (`lvl.technical.coding`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 118 | `lsn.technical.live-coding-method` The live-coding method | Run the method aloud: restate, ask about input sizes and edge cases, write examples, give a brute force, improve, test while writing | "Return the $k$ most frequent elements of a list." Three questions to ask before coding? (Ties, order of the output, and whether $k$ can exceed the number of distinct elements) | `problem-workbench` (timed), `voice-capture` | Trap: coding at once in silence. Edge: an $O(n\log k)$ heap or an $O(n)$ bucket answer both pass; saying which and why is part of the mark | O, S, C, K |
| 119 | `lsn.technical.reservoir` Reservoir sampling | Implement and prove reservoir sampling: replace the kept item with item $i$ with chance $\frac1i$ | A stream of unknown length; keep one uniformly random item with constant memory. Chance the $j$th item survives to the end of $n$? ($\frac1j\prod_{i > j}\left(1 - \frac1i\right) = \frac1n$) | `problem-workbench`, `trial-simulator` | Trap: storing the stream, or keeping item $i$ with chance $\frac12$. Edge: for $k$ items, item $i > k$ enters with chance $\frac ki$ and evicts a random kept one | K, X, C, N |
| 120 | `lsn.technical.shuffles` Shuffles: Fisher-Yates and the biased one | Show that swapping each position with a uniformly random position in the whole array is biased, by counting, and implement Fisher-Yates | For 3 elements, the naive shuffle makes $3^3 = 27$ equally likely swap sequences. Are the 6 orders equally likely? (No: three orders come up 5 times in 27 and three come up 4 times) | `problem-workbench`, `counting-slots` | Trap: "every element moves randomly, so the result is uniform". Edge: 27 is not a multiple of 6, so no assignment of 27 equally likely cases can be uniform: a counting proof in one line | N, C, K, M |
| 121 | `lsn.technical.streaming-stats` Streaming statistics | Keep a running median with two heaps and a running variance with Welford's update, and explain why the textbook one-pass variance formula fails | Prices near 10,000 with a spread of 0.01. Why does "mean of squares minus square of mean" give nonsense in floating point? (Catastrophic cancellation: two numbers near $10^8$ that agree in their first 12 digits, so at most 4 of double precision's 16 significant digits survive the subtraction, and rounding in the running sums can consume those) | `problem-workbench`, `code-cell` | Trap: sorting the window on every new tick. Edge: Welford's update gives the same result as the two-pass formula to rounding error | K, C, N, X |
| 122 | `lsn.technical.order-book-code` Coding an order book | Design and code the core of a price-time priority book: price levels in a sorted map, a queue per level, and a hash of order ids for cancels | Which structures give the best price in $O(1)$, inserts in $O(\log L)$ for $L$ price levels, and cancels by id in $O(1)$? (A sorted map or a heap of levels, a deque per level, and a dictionary from order id to its node) | `problem-workbench`, `order-book`, `code-surface` | Trap: one sorted list of all orders. Edge: a cancel must not leave an empty level in the map, or the best price is wrong | K, S, C, O |
| 123 | `lsn.technical.numerical-code` Numerical code in interviews | Code Newton's method for $\sqrt a$, bisection, a Monte Carlo estimate with its standard error, and the log-sum-exp trick; explain `0.1 + 0.2 != 0.3` | Newton's iterates for $\sqrt2$ from 1? (1.5, 1.41667, 1.414216: the number of correct digits roughly doubles each step) | `problem-workbench`, `code-cell`, `root-finder` | Trap: comparing floats with `==`. Edge: Newton's method fails from a start where the derivative is zero, and for $\sqrt a$ it converges from any positive start | T, N, K, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 121 is a market-data handler's statistics (2.3 project 4); lesson 122 is a developer interview's most common system question [recalled].

Then the Level review and homework `set.technical.coding`.

**Level TR5: C++ and systems questions** (`lvl.technical.cpp-systems`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 124 | `lsn.technical.cpp-object-model` Virtual functions and the object model | Explain dynamic dispatch through a vtable, why a base class with virtual functions needs a virtual destructor, and object slicing | You delete a derived object through a pointer to its base, and the base destructor is not virtual. What happens? (Undefined behaviour; in practice the derived part is not destroyed) | `code-surface`, `problem-workbench` (C++, web) | Trap: "the derived destructor runs anyway". Edge: passing a derived object by value to a function taking the base slices off the derived part | C, T, M, S |
| 125 | `lsn.technical.cpp-memory` RAII and smart pointers | Choose between `unique_ptr`, `shared_ptr` and `weak_ptr`, and explain RAII as tying a resource's lifetime to an object's | Two objects hold `shared_ptr`s to each other. When are they freed? (Never: the cycle keeps both counts above zero; make one link a `weak_ptr`) | `code-surface`, `node-link` (ownership graph) | Trap: `shared_ptr` everywhere "to be safe". Edge: `make_shared` puts the object and its count in one allocation | C, T, S, M |
| 126 | `lsn.technical.cpp-moves` Copies, moves and the rule of zero | Count copies and moves in common statements, and state the rules of zero, three and five | How many copies of the string happen in `v.push_back(std::string("abc"))`? (None: the temporary is moved; `emplace_back("abc")` constructs it in place) | `code-surface`, `problem-workbench` (C++) | Trap: "every push_back copies". Edge: a moved-from object is valid but unspecified; reading it is allowed, relying on its value is not | N, T, C, M |
| 127 | `lsn.technical.stl-complexity` Containers and their costs | State the complexity and invalidation rules of `vector`, `deque`, `map`, `unordered_map` and `list`, and derive the amortised cost of `vector` growth | A `vector` that doubles its capacity from 1 grows to hold 1,000,000 elements. Total element moves during reallocations? (1,048,575, under 2 per element: amortised $O(1)$) [recalled: implementations differ in growth factor] | `bench-lab`, `code-surface` | Trap: "push_back is $O(n)$ because it sometimes copies everything". Edge: a reallocation invalidates every iterator and reference into the vector | N, S, C, M |
| 128 | `lsn.technical.latency-basics` Latency, caches and threads | Explain cache lines, false sharing, branch prediction and the order of magnitude of memory latencies, and sketch a single-producer single-consumer queue | Two threads increment two different counters that sit in the same cache line. Why is it slow? (False sharing: each write invalidates the other core's copy of the line) | `bench-lab`, `latency-lab` | Trap: "different variables cannot interfere". Edge: padding each counter to its own cache line fixes it; `std::hardware_destructive_interference_size` names the size [recalled] | C, O, S, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 125 is the ownership model of every C++ trading system; lesson 128 is the first performance question for a low-latency developer.

Then the Level review and homework `set.technical.cpp-systems`.

**Level TR6: The data take-home** (`lvl.technical.take-home`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 129 | `lsn.technical.take-home-brief` Reading the brief | Extract what is being judged from a take-home brief, plan the time and write the questions to send back | A 72-hour take-home asks for a model and a short report on a price dataset. How do you split 8 working hours? (About 1 reading and checking data, 2 baseline and validation, 2 improving, 2 writing, 1 checking and packaging) [our estimate] | `card-sort`, `setup-checklist` | Trap: spending the time on a complex model. Edge: an unclear brief is a test too; one clarifying email is professional, five are not | O, S, C, N |
| 130 | `lsn.technical.take-home-hygiene` Data hygiene | Find look-ahead, survivorship, time-zone, corporate-action and duplicate errors in a dataset before modelling | A planted dataset holds five defects. Find them. (A future-dated feature, a delisted share missing, prices in two time zones, an unadjusted split, duplicated rows) | `ml-workbench` (bias audit), `results-note` | Trap: modelling first and checking after. Edge: survivorship is invisible in the data you have; ask how the universe was built | M, S, O, C |
| 131 | `lsn.technical.take-home-baseline` Baselines and honest validation | Fit a baseline first, validate with time-ordered splits, and judge a result against what is plausible for the problem | Your model's out-of-sample $R^2$ for next-day returns is 0.02. Good or bad? (Large for daily returns; check for leakage before claiming it, and report it with its uncertainty) | `time-cv-lab`, `regression-lab` | Trap: judging a return model by the $R^2$ standards of physical data. Edge: a negative out-of-sample $R^2$ means worse than predicting the mean, a common and honest result | C, N, M, S |
| 132 | `lsn.technical.take-home-report` The write-up | Write the report in the results-note format: the answer first, the method, the evidence with uncertainty, what failed, what next | Four openings for the same report; which leads with the answer? (The one that states the finding and its size in the first sentence) | `results-note`, `card-sort` | Trap: a chronological diary of what you tried. Edge: what failed earns credit; hiding it costs trust when the reader finds it | S, O, C, M |
| 133 | `lsn.technical.take-home-defence` Defending the take-home | Prepare for the follow-up interview: the three weakest points, the checks you ran, the extension you would do next | "Your backtest shows a Sharpe ratio of 2.5. Why should I believe it?" What do you say first? (The number of variants tried, the out-of-sample period, the costs assumed, and the leakage checks: the same list as 2.3's results note) | `viva`, `mock-interview` (technical) | Trap: defending the number instead of the method. Edge: "I would not trust it yet, and here is the test that would change my mind" is a strong answer | O, S, C, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 130 is the bias audit of 2.3 RM; lesson 133 is the interview version of every results note.

Then the Level review and homework `set.technical.take-home`.

#### Course IV: The Interview Itself (`crs.interview`, 4 levels, 21 lessons)

The curriculum's last instruction for 3.3: "mock interviews, spoken aloud: interviewers mark the reasoning as it happens, not only the answer." This course teaches what interviewers mark and how to give it to them, turns the learner's projects and replication into answers, covers the hiring process, and runs the mocks that close the exit. Its judgements are sorted, ordered and matched against the rubric, not chosen from lists, so that choice items stay under 40% (section 1.0.4).

**Level IV1: How interviews are marked** (`lvl.interview.marking`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 134 | `lsn.interview.what-is-marked` What interviewers mark | Mark two transcripts against the 10-point rubric (clarify and plan 2, reason aloud 2, method 2, answer and check 2, hints, pressure and time 2) with a quote as evidence for each mark | Two candidates give the same right answer to the same problem. One scores 9 and one 5. Why? (The 5 was silent, never checked the answer, and ignored a hint) | `mock-interview` (review), `free-response` | Trap: "the right answer is what counts". Edge: a wrong final number with a clear, checked method often scores above a silent right one [recalled from interview reports] | S, S, O, C |
| 135 | `lsn.interview.thinking-aloud` Thinking aloud | Narrate the structure (what you are computing and why) rather than the arithmetic, and keep talking through a pause | Re-solve lesson 19's three-in-a-row problem aloud; compare your transcript with a model one line by line | `puzzle-workbench` (aloud), `voice-capture` | Trap: reading the arithmetic out digit by digit. Edge: a short silence to think is fine if announced ("give me ten seconds to set up the states") | S, O, M, C |
| 136 | `lsn.interview.hints` Hints are information | Read what a hint says about the intended method, ask for one well, and use it without starting over | The interviewer says "think about the first toss". What method is being suggested? (First-step analysis: condition on the first outcome) | `mock-interview` (review), `card-sort` | Trap: treating a hint as a failure and getting flustered. Edge: refusing all hints to look strong costs the "uses hints" marks and the time | S, C, O, M |
| 137 | `lsn.interview.recovery` Catching and recovering from errors aloud | Run sanity checks before announcing an answer, and recover from an error mid-solution by naming it and repairing it | Halfway through you get a probability of 1.2. What do you say next? ("That cannot be right, a probability is at most 1; I double-counted the overlap, let me subtract it") | `puzzle-workbench` (aloud), `mock-interview` (review) | Trap: carrying on, or apologising at length. Edge: the interviewer may plant an error in a premise; checking premises aloud catches it | O, S, C, M |
| 138 | `lsn.interview.time-in-rounds` Time in a round | Plan a 30- to 45-minute round (typically two to four problems), know when to give an estimate and move on, and ask good closing questions | Twenty minutes into a 30-minute round, still on the first problem. What now? (Give the best current answer with its uncertainty, say how you would finish, and ask to move on) | `mock-interview`, `speed-timer` | Trap: finishing every problem whatever the clock says. Edge: some interviewers want depth on one problem; ask which they prefer at the start | O, S, C, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 134 is how this app's mock rubric marks (`widgets-finance-games.md` 8.6); lesson 137 is how a trader corrects a wrong quote on the desk.

Then the Level review and homework `set.interview.marking`.

**Level IV2: You and your work** (`lvl.interview.you`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 139 | `lsn.interview.your-story` Your story | Write and say a two-minute answer to "why this role, why us, why now", grounded in evidence from the path | Rank three answers to "why quant trading?" (The specific, evidenced one beats the passionate one and the money one) | `free-response` (voice), `card-sort` | Trap: a generic answer that fits every firm. Edge: a career changer's story is an asset when it names what transfers; the path's projects are the evidence | S, O, C, M |
| 140 | `lsn.interview.project-walkthrough` A project in three minutes | Walk through a 2.3 project: problem, method, result with its uncertainty, what failed, what next, and answer three follow-ups | Order these parts of a walk-through for maximum effect. (Problem and result first, then method, uncertainty, failure, next step) | `results-note`, `mock-interview` (project), `voice-capture` | Trap: a tour of the code. Edge: "what would you do differently?" needs a real answer; "nothing" fails it | O, S, C, M |
| 141 | `lsn.interview.research-discussion` Discussing a paper and a replication | Present the 3.1 replication (what the paper claimed, what you reproduced, how much survived out of sample) and answer "is the effect still there?" | "Is momentum still there?" How do you answer from your replication? (With your out-of-sample number, its interval and the caveats, not with the paper's) | `viva`, `results-note` | Trap: repeating the paper's abstract. Edge: an effect that halved out of sample is a normal finding; say so plainly | S, O, C, M |
| 142 | `lsn.interview.behavioural` Behavioural questions | Answer behavioural questions with the situation, task, action and result structure, including a failure and what changed after it | "Tell me about a mistake." Which of four answers is strongest? (The one that names a real mistake, its cost, the fix and the habit it created) | `free-response` (voice), `card-sort` | Trap: a disguised boast ("I work too hard"). Edge: a team story needs your own action in it, not the team's | S, S, O, C |
| 143 | `lsn.interview.market-awareness` Market awareness | Explain a week's moves in rates, equities, volatility and currencies in two sentences each, linking cause and price | A week in which yields rose and equities fell: one coherent explanation, and one check you would make? (Higher discount rates lower equity values; check whether the move was in real yields or inflation expectations) | `free-response`, `yield-curve-lab` | Trap: reciting headlines without a mechanism. Edge: "I don't know why it moved; here is how I would find out" beats a confident wrong story | S, O, M, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 140 is the CV's projects made spoken; lesson 143 is the "what's moving the market?" question that opens many trader interviews [recalled].

Then the Level review and homework `set.interview.you`.

(Market makers: mental arithmetic, probability and games; systematic funds: statistics, research and coding; banks' desks: derivatives and stochastic calculus; ML labs: coding and ML depth) [recalled; varies by firm] | `card-sort`, `value-table` | Trap: preparing one way for all. Edge: the curriculum's track table is a starting point; each firm's own careers page is the authority | S, S, C, M |

(For example: "Built a backtesting engine with costs, slippage and a held-out period; 41 tests; results note on GitHub") | `free-response`, `results-note` | Trap: listing tools instead of results. Edge: a self-taught CV needs the projects to carry the weight (curriculum: "a self-taught CV rarely clears the screen at the top names") | S, O, C, M |

In what order, and when do you submit partial code? (Read all three first; do the easiest fully; submit passing partial solutions before the clock ends) | `problem-workbench` (timed), `speed-drill` | Trap: one hard problem first. Offer B: £110,000 base and a guaranteed 20% first-year bonus. Expected first-year pay of each? (£135,000, uncertain; £132,000, certain) [illustrative numbers] | `bet-lab` (ev), `value-table` | Trap: comparing base salaries only. Edge: non-compete periods and deferred pay change the comparison more than a few thousand pounds of salary | N, C, S, O |

Transfer screens: lesson 145 is the curriculum's rule 5 made into a plan; lesson 148 is MG1's expected value applied to the learner's own career.

Then the Level review and homework `set.interview.process`.

What do you drill next? (Sanity checks: the second-method habit of lesson 38, under the clock) | `mock-interview` (log), `results-note` (readiness) | Trap: chasing more mocks without looking at the trend. Then the Level review and homework `set.interview.mocks`.

### 2.3 Coverage check

**3.3 against the curriculum's lines.**

| Curriculum line for 3.3 | Where it is met |
|---|---|
| "Fifty Challenging Problems in Probability, Mosteller" (first) | Book-log, worked cold from the start of 3.3 alongside PZ1 to PZ3 (section 5.2); the patterns in PZ1 to PZ5 |
| "A Practical Guide to Quantitative Finance Interviews, Xinfeng Zhou" (second) | Chapter 4 cold after PZ5, gated by `exm.iv.zhou-cold` and confirmed by the book-log (`3.3.zhou`); chapter 5 after PZ6; chapters 2 and 3 with QM4 to QM6; chapter 6 with MG5; chapter 7 with TR4 |
| "Heard on the Street, Timothy Crack" (third) | Book-log after Zhou, selected by track; its derivatives questions after MG5, its statistics questions after TR1 |
| "Quant Job Interview Questions and Answers, Mark Joshi and co-authors. Developer and desk quant roles only" (fourth) | Book-log on the developer track and for desk quants, after TR5; other tracks work Stefanica, Radoičić and Wang instead (decision 10) |
| "Mental arithmetic: Zetamac at 50 or more ... plus timed drills in the 80-questions-in-8-minutes format" | QM1 to QM3; the Daily 10 (outside the hours); 6 h of speed blocks; `3.3.zetamac`; the 80-in-8 goal |
| "Market-making games: quote a two-sided price on an unknown quantity and update it as trades come in. Practise aloud with a partner" | MG3 (lessons 84 to 89), MG4 (90 to 94, partner play in 93); 5 h of partner sessions; the markets mock (150) |
| "Betting games: expected value, when to hedge, Kelly sizing" | MG1 (73 to 78) and MG2 (79 to 83) |
| "Jane Street's Probability and Markets guide" | Read with MG3 (section 5.2); its expected-value and market-making ideas are lessons 84 to 88 |
| "Mock interviews, spoken aloud: interviewers mark the reasoning as it happens" | IV1 (the rubric and thinking aloud), every lesson's aloud screen (section 1.0.3), IV4 (mocks), the ten logged human mocks, `3.3.mocks` |
| Track table: trader rounds ("probability, mental arithmetic, market-making and betting games") | PZ, QM, MG |
| Track table: researcher rounds ("probability, statistics, regression, machine learning, a data take-home") | PZ, TR1 to TR3, TR6 and its 6-hour rehearsal |
| Track table: developer rounds ("algorithms, C++, systems design") | TR4, TR5; systems design only as the order-book design of lesson 122 and the latency lesson 128, a thin cover that canon-gap P13 (systems, concurrency and performance) would fill |
| The map line: "the interview books solved cold" | The book-log of all four books in order, and proposed part `3.3.books` (decision 14) |
| The habit "three interview-style probability problems a week" | Continues outside the hours, in cold mode in Stage 3 (`ASSESSMENT.md` 10.4) |
| Curriculum rule 5, "know the hiring calendar" | IV3 lesson 145 and the applications work in the drills block |

**Added for a top programme or an ML lab.** Bonferroni brackets (5), size-biased sampling (15), correlation bounds and orthant probabilities (28, 29), Brownian motion and Itô at interview depth (PZ6), estimation, Fermi and calibration (QM2, 89), invariants, pigeonhole and information counts (QM4), the equicorrelation bound and correlation-matrix validity (71, 72), replicating contingent bets (77), simultaneous and uncertain-edge Kelly (81, 82), Figgie (92), the digital's skew term and baskets against options (97, 99), the fundamental law of active management (106), ML-lab depth (TR3: backpropagation, initialisation, optimiser stability, attention, information theory), streaming statistics and the biased shuffle (120, 121), the take-home (TR6), the CV, online assessments and offers (IV3).

**Not covered, by decision.** Brainteasers that depend on a trick word ("lateral thinking" riddles) are left out: they appear in older interview folklore but carry no transferable method [our choice]. Fermi questions about a specific firm's business, and firm-specific games other than Figgie, are configuration in `mm-game` and `fermi-estimator`, not lessons. Systems design beyond lesson 122 waits for canon-gap P13.

---

## 3. Homework blueprint per level

### 3.0 Rules for every set

- **Issue, due and marking** follow `ASSESSMENT.md` 2.5: issued when the level's last lesson is done, due 7 days later, no feedback before submission, code items run their visible examples freely and their hidden tests at submission, long-form items self-marked against the rubric with Bertie's suggested mark and evidence quote beside the learner's.
- **Size and mix.** Levels of 4 or 5 lessons get **8 items: 3 at difficulty 3, 3 at difficulty 4, 2 at difficulty 5**. Levels of 6 lessons get **10 items: 4 at difficulty 3, 4 at difficulty 4, 2 at difficulty 5**. 3.2 sets take about 2 hours (8 items) and 2 hours 30 minutes (10 items), because most items are derivations; 3.3 sets take about 1 hour 30 minutes and 2 hours, because interview items are short and timed. Exactly one long-form item (a derivation, proof or written explanation with a 10-point rubric, usually difficulty 4) and, where the level has code, exactly one code drill (difficulty 3 or 4) sit inside those counts. In QM levels a **timed deck item** (D) stands in the drill's place; in IV levels the second long-form task does. At least half of each set's items are generator slots, so a redo is a fresh variant. At most two choice items per set.
- **3.3 sets are cold.** Every 3.3 item shows a soft clock with the time an interviewer would allow (5, 10 or 15 minutes by difficulty); the time taken is recorded and shown in the review, never marked. No calculator (decision 19), except in TR6 and the code drills.
- **Story variety.** Each set mixes at least three settings (dice, cards, coins, trades, rates, data, code), so a set tests the idea rather than the wording.
- **Long-form marking.** Every rubric has 4 to 10 points totalling 10 marks, and each point names the model-answer lines that evidence it (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 3.4). 7 of 10 is the bar wherever an outcome cites a rubric. In 3.3 the rubric for explanation items is the mock rubric's (clarify and plan, reasoning in order, method, answer and check, communication), so homework and mocks mark the same things.
- **Code drills** list their hidden tests here so CI can check that the reference solution passes and each known-wrong solution fails its named test (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 3.9). 3.2 drills use NumPy and the standard library, with the normal CDF from `math.erf`; 3.3 drills use the standard library, `micropython-ok` where written so. Exact answers compare as `Fraction`s; floating-point answers use `zqtest`'s `isclose` with relative tolerance $10^{-9}$ unless the test states another. Tests on a simulation fix the seed and check the estimate against the exact value within 4 standard errors, so they are deterministic. TR5's C++ drill runs on the learner's machine and returns by paste-verify (`widgets-programming.md` 6.11).
- **Resources held back.** No 3.3 item reproduces or re-parameterises a problem from Zhou chapters 4 and 5, and none knowingly reproduces a Mosteller, Crack or Joshi problem (decision 8). No 3.2 item reproduces a Shreve exercise or a Chin, Nel and Ólafsson problem; items "after" one are recreated with new numbers and wording (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 9.4).

Below, each level gives its set id, item mix, long-form item, code drill with its hidden tests, and two example problems in full: example A is the long-form item with its model answer and rubric, example B a difficulty-5 item with its worked answer and decision point.

### 3.1 Item 3.2: the 20 sets

**AT1 Replication on many periods** (`set.tree-pricing.replication`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the one-period no-arbitrage theorem. Code drill `drl.stoch.path-tree` (d4): `price_path(S0, u, d, r, n, payoff)` enumerates the $2^n$ paths with `Fraction`s and returns the price and $\Delta_0$. Hidden tests: the lesson 2 lookback gives $\frac{172}{125}$ with $\Delta_0 = \frac{13}{75}$; a European call struck at 5 gives $\frac{288}{125}$ (the 1.9 exit's number); an Asian call on the average of $S_1, S_2, S_3$ struck at 4 gives $\frac{608}{375}$; $u \le 1 + r$ raises `ValueError`; a payoff that ignores the path agrees with the recombining backward induction. Known-wrong: discounting once by $1 + r$ instead of $(1 + r)^3$ returns $\frac{43}{20}$ for the lookback and fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* In the one-period model with $S_0 > 0$, up factor $u$, down factor $d$ ($0 < d < u$) and interest rate $r > -1$, prove that there is no arbitrage if and only if $d < 1 + r < u$.
  *Model answer.* (1) A portfolio of $\Delta$ shares and $X_0 - \Delta S_0$ in the bank has time-1 value $X_1 = \Delta S_1 + (1 + r)(X_0 - \Delta S_0)$. An arbitrage has $X_0 = 0$, $X_1 \ge 0$ in both states and $X_1 > 0$ in one. With $X_0 = 0$: $X_1(H) = \Delta S_0(u - (1 + r))$ and $X_1(T) = \Delta S_0(d - (1 + r))$. (2) Sufficiency: if $d < 1 + r < u$ the two brackets have opposite strict signs, so for $\Delta \ne 0$ one of $X_1(H)$, $X_1(T)$ is negative, and for $\Delta = 0$ both are 0. No arbitrage. (3) Necessity, first case: if $1 + r \le d$, take $\Delta = 1$: $X_1(T) = S_0(d - 1 - r) \ge 0$ and $X_1(H) = S_0(u - 1 - r) > 0$ since $u > d \ge 1 + r$. An arbitrage. (4) Second case: if $1 + r \ge u$, take $\Delta = -1$: $X_1(H) = S_0(1 + r - u) \ge 0$ and $X_1(T) = S_0(1 + r - d) > 0$. An arbitrage. (5) Equivalently, $\tilde p = \frac{1 + r - d}{u - d}$ lies strictly between 0 and 1 exactly when $d < 1 + r < u$, which is why the risk-neutral probabilities exist exactly when there is no arbitrage.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the value of a zero-cost portfolio in both states (line 1). 3: sufficiency by opposite signs, including $\Delta = 0$ (line 2). 2: the case $1 + r \le d$ with the equality case (line 3). 2: the case $1 + r \ge u$ (line 4). 1: the link to $\tilde p \in (0, 1)$ (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* One period, $S_0 = 100$ moving to 120, 100 or 90, $r = 0$. A put struck at 105. Find the interval of prices that admit no arbitrage, and the cheapest portfolio of shares and cash that never pays less than the put.
  *Answer.* Risk-neutral vectors $(q_1, q_2, q_3)$, all positive, with $120q_1 + 100q_2 + 90q_3 = 100$ and $q_1 + q_2 + q_3 = 1$, satisfy $20q_1 = 10q_3$, so $q_1 = \frac{q_3}2$ and $q_2 = 1 - \frac32q_3$, with $0 < q_3 < \frac23$. The put pays 0, 5 and 15, so its price is $5q_2 + 15q_3 = 5 + 7.5q_3$, which ranges over the open interval $(5, 10)$. The upper end is the super-replication cost: hold $\Delta$ shares and cash $c$ with $120\Delta + c \ge 0$, $100\Delta + c \ge 5$, $90\Delta + c \ge 15$; the cheapest is $\Delta = -\frac12$, $c = 60$ (cost $-50 + 60 = 10$), which pays exactly 0 and 15 at the outer states and 10 at the middle one. Decision point: the binomial price on the 120 and 90 tree, 10, is the upper bound because that tree's replicating portfolio dominates the put at the middle state; the lower bound 5 is approached only as the measure puts all its mass on "unchanged".

**AT2 Probability on coin-toss space** (`set.tree-pricing.coin-space`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the martingale property of discounted wealth and the pricing formula. Code drill `drl.stoch.cond-exp` (d3): `cond_exp(X, n, N, p)` returns a dictionary from each length-$n$ prefix to $E_n[X]$, exactly. Hidden tests: $\widetilde E_1[S_3]$ is $\frac{25}2$ after H and $\frac{25}8$ after T; $E_0[E_2[X]] = E_0[X]$ for a random payoff; $E_N[X] = X$; $\frac{S_n}{(1 + r)^n}$ passes a martingale check under $\tilde p$ and fails under $p = \frac23$. Known-wrong: averaging over all tosses instead of the unseen ones returns the same number for every prefix and fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove that under $\widetilde P$ the discounted wealth $\frac{X_n}{(1 + r)^n}$ of any self-financing portfolio is a martingale, and deduce $V_0 = \widetilde E\left[\frac{V_N}{(1 + r)^N}\right]$ for any claim $V_N$ that can be replicated.
  *Model answer.* (1) The wealth equation: $X_{n+1} = \Delta_nS_{n+1} + (1 + r)(X_n - \Delta_nS_n)$, with $\Delta_n$ known at time $n$. (2) Divide by $(1 + r)^{n+1}$: $\frac{X_{n+1}}{(1 + r)^{n+1}} = \Delta_n\frac{S_{n+1}}{(1 + r)^{n+1}} + \frac{X_n - \Delta_nS_n}{(1 + r)^n}$. (3) Take $\widetilde E_n$, taking out what is known: $\widetilde E_n\left[\frac{X_{n+1}}{(1 + r)^{n+1}}\right] = \Delta_n\widetilde E_n\left[\frac{S_{n+1}}{(1 + r)^{n+1}}\right] + \frac{X_n - \Delta_nS_n}{(1 + r)^n}$. (4) The discounted share is a $\widetilde P$-martingale: $\widetilde E_n[S_{n+1}] = S_n(\tilde pu + \tilde qd) = S_n(1 + r)$, so the first term is $\Delta_n\frac{S_n}{(1 + r)^n}$. (5) The terms in $\Delta_n$ cancel, leaving $\frac{X_n}{(1 + r)^n}$: a martingale. (6) If $X_N = V_N$ on every path, the martingale property from 0 to $N$ gives $X_0 = \widetilde E\left[\frac{V_N}{(1 + r)^N}\right]$, and no arbitrage forces the price to equal the replicating cost $X_0$.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the wealth equation (line 1). 2: discounting and taking out $\Delta_n$ (lines 2 and 3). 3: $\widetilde E_n[S_{n+1}] = (1 + r)S_n$ from the definition of $\tilde p$ (line 4). 2: the cancellation (line 5). 2: the pricing formula with the no-arbitrage reason (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* In the lesson 2 tree, compare $\widetilde E_2[M_3]$ after HT and after TH, where $M_n$ is the running maximum, and use the comparison to show that the share price alone is not a sufficient state for the lookback.
  *Answer.* After HT the path is 4, 8, 4, so $S_2 = 4$ and $M_2 = 8$; the next price is 8 or 2, and $M_3 = 8$ either way: $\widetilde E_2[M_3] = 8$. After TH the path is 4, 2, 4, so $S_2 = 4$ and $M_2 = 4$; the next price is 8 (then $M_3 = 8$) or 2 (then $M_3 = 4$): $\widetilde E_2[M_3] = 6$. Same $S_2$, different conditional expectations, so the lookback's price at time 2 is not a function of $S_2$ alone; with $(S_2, M_2)$ it is. Decision point: the tempting check, "the share price is Markov, so everything is", answers a different question; what matters is whether the claim's future cash flow is a function of the state.

**AT3 State prices and changing measure** (`set.tree-pricing.state-prices`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: Bayes' formula for conditional expectations under a change of measure. Code drill `drl.stoch.state-prices` (d3): `state_prices(p, ptilde, r, N)` returns $\zeta(\omega)$ for every path; `price(V)` returns $E[\zeta V]$. Hidden tests: the HHH Arrow-Debreu price is $\frac8{125}$; state prices sum to $(1 + r)^{-N} = \frac{64}{125}$ for $N = 3$; a call struck at 5 prices to $\frac{288}{125}$, as by risk-neutral expectation; $\zeta$ is largest at TTT. Known-wrong: $\zeta = \frac{P}{\widetilde P}(1 + r)^{-N}$ fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* With $Z_n = E_n[Z]$ and $Z = \frac{\widetilde P}{P}$, prove that for any $Y$ known at time $N$, $\widetilde E_n[Y] = \frac1{Z_n}E_n[ZY]$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Fix an atom $A$ of $\mathcal F_n$ (the paths sharing the first $n$ tosses). The value of $\widetilde E_n[Y]$ on $A$ is $\frac{\sum_{\omega \in A}\widetilde P(\omega)Y(\omega)}{\widetilde P(A)}$. (2) Write $\widetilde P(\omega) = Z(\omega)P(\omega)$: numerator $\sum_{\omega \in A}Z(\omega)Y(\omega)P(\omega) = P(A)\,E_n[ZY]$ on $A$. (3) Denominator: $\widetilde P(A) = \sum_{\omega \in A}Z(\omega)P(\omega) = P(A)\,E_n[Z] = P(A)Z_n$ on $A$. (4) Divide: $\widetilde E_n[Y] = \frac{E_n[ZY]}{Z_n}$ on $A$, and so on every atom. (5) Check at $n = 0$: $Z_0 = E[Z] = 1$ and $\widetilde E[Y] = E[ZY]$; at $n = N$: $\widetilde E_N[Y] = Y$ and $\frac{ZY}{Z} = Y$.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: conditional expectation as an average over an atom (line 1). 3: the numerator by $\widetilde P = ZP$ (line 2). 2: the denominator as $P(A)Z_n$ (line 3). 2: the conclusion on every atom (line 4). 1: the two checks (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* Two periods, $X_0 = 1$, $p = \frac23$, $\tilde p = \frac12$, $r = \frac14$, $S_0 = 4$, $u = 2$, $d = \frac12$. Find the log-optimal terminal wealth on each path and the fraction of wealth held in the share at time 0 and at time 1 after H.
  *Answer.* $\zeta = \frac{Z}{(1 + r)^2}$ with $Z(\text{HH}) = \frac{1/4}{4/9} = \frac9{16}$, $Z(\text{HT}) = Z(\text{TH}) = \frac{1/4}{2/9} = \frac98$, $Z(\text{TT}) = \frac{1/4}{1/9} = \frac94$, so $X_2 = \frac1\zeta$ gives $\frac{25}9$, $\frac{25}{18}$, $\frac{25}{18}$ and $\frac{25}{36}$. Then $X_1 = \frac{\widetilde E_1[X_2]}{1 + r}$ gives $\frac53$ after H and $\frac56$ after T; $\Delta_0 = \frac{5/3 - 5/6}{8 - 2} = \frac5{36}$, a share of wealth $\frac{4\Delta_0}{1} = \frac59$; after H, $\Delta_1 = \frac{25/9 - 25/18}{16 - 4} = \frac{25}{216}$, a share $\frac{8 \cdot 25/216}{5/3} = \frac59$ again. Decision point: the log investor holds the same fraction of wealth in the share every period, whatever has happened: the myopic property of log utility, which is why Kelly sizing needs no forecast of the future beyond the next bet.

**AT4 American claims and stopping times** (`set.tree-pricing.american`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: no early exercise of American calls. Code drill `drl.stoch.american` (d4): `american(S0, u, d, r, N, payoff)` returns the price and the exercise map. Hidden tests: the put struck at 5 is $\frac{34}{25}$ for $N = 2$ and $\frac{174}{125}$ for $N = 3$; the European puts are $\frac{24}{25}$ and $\frac{108}{125}$; the American call equals the European call on 20 seeded trees with $r \ge 0$; the American price is at least the European on every seeded tree. Known-wrong: exercising at every in-the-money node returns 1 at the root for $N = 2$ (the immediate payoff) and fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* In the binomial model with $r \ge 0$ and no dividends, prove that the American call struck at $K$ has the same price as the European call.
  *Model answer.* (1) The European call's discounted price $\frac{c_n}{(1 + r)^n}$ is a $\widetilde P$-martingale, so $c_n = \widetilde E_n\left[\frac{(S_N - K)^+}{(1 + r)^{N - n}}\right]$. (2) $(x - K)^+$ is convex and $(x - K)^+ \ge x - K$, so $c_n \ge \widetilde E_n\left[\frac{S_N - K}{(1 + r)^{N - n}}\right] = S_n - \frac{K}{(1 + r)^{N - n}}$. (3) With $r \ge 0$, $\frac K{(1 + r)^{N - n}} \le K$, so $c_n \ge S_n - K$; also $c_n \ge 0$. Hence $c_n \ge (S_n - K)^+$: the European call is always worth at least the exercise value. (4) The American price $C_n$ satisfies $C_n \ge c_n$ (the holder may wait) and is the smallest supermartingale-type process above the payoff; since the European price already dominates the payoff at every node, the algorithm $C_n = \max\{(S_n - K)^+, \text{continuation}\}$ always chooses continuation, and $C_n = c_n$. (5) So early exercise is never strictly better; selling the call beats exercising it.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the martingale representation of the European price (line 1). 3: the bound by $x - K$ (line 2). 2: using $r \ge 0$ (line 3). 2: the comparison with the American algorithm (line 4). 1: the conclusion (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* Three periods, $S_0 = 4$, $u = 2$, $d = \frac12$, $r = \frac14$: price the American put struck at 5 and give the optimal stopping time on every path.
  *Answer.* Expiry values: $S_3 \in \{32, 8, 2, \frac12\}$ give payoffs 0, 0, 3, 4.5. Time 2: at $S = 16$ the put is worth 0; at $S = 4$ continuation $\frac{0.5 \times 0 + 0.5 \times 3}{1.25} = 1.2$ beats exercise (1); at $S = 1$ exercise (4) beats continuation 3. Time 1: at $S = 8$ continuation $\frac{0.5 \times 0 + 0.5 \times 1.2}{1.25} = 0.48$, exercise 0; at $S = 2$ exercise (3) beats continuation $\frac{0.5 \times 1.2 + 0.5 \times 4}{1.25} = 2.08$. Time 0: continuation $\frac{0.5 \times 0.48 + 0.5 \times 3}{1.25} = 1.392$ beats exercise (1). Price $\frac{174}{125} = 1.392$. Stopping time: $\tau^* = 1$ on every path starting T; $\tau^* = 3$ on HTT (payoff 3); never on HHH, HHT, HTH (payoff 0). Check: $\widetilde E\left[\frac{g(S_{\tau^*})}{1.25^{\tau^*}}\right] = \frac12 \cdot \frac3{1.25} + \frac18 \cdot \frac3{1.25^3} = 1.2 + 0.192 = 1.392$. Decision point: at $S = 4$ at time 2 the put is in the money and still not exercised, because one more period of optionality is worth 0.2 more.

**AT5 Random walks and random interest rates** (`set.tree-pricing.rates`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the first-passage generating function. Code drill `drl.stoch.rate-tree` (d4): `zero_price(rates, ptilde, m)` and `caplet(rates, ptilde, n, K)` on a non-recombining short-rate tree. Hidden tests: lesson 21's bond, 0.8997538; lesson 22's caplet, 0.0216450; lesson 23's forward price, 111.1415; a flat 5% tree prices the two-period zero at $1.05^{-2}$. Known-wrong: discounting at the average rate path returns 0.8984726 and fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For the symmetric random walk $M_n$ from 0 and $\tau_1 = \min\{n : M_n = 1\}$, show that $S_n = e^{\sigma M_n}\left(\frac2{e^\sigma + e^{-\sigma}}\right)^n$ is a martingale for every $\sigma > 0$, and derive $E[\alpha^{\tau_1}] = \frac{1 - \sqrt{1 - \alpha^2}}\alpha$ for $0 < \alpha < 1$.
  *Model answer.* (1) $E_n[S_{n+1}] = S_n\frac{2}{e^\sigma + e^{-\sigma}}\cdot\frac{e^\sigma + e^{-\sigma}}2 = S_n$. (2) The stopped process $S_{n \wedge \tau_1}$ is a martingale, so $E[S_{n \wedge \tau_1}] = 1$. (3) Up to $\tau_1$ the walk never exceeds 1, so $M_{n \wedge \tau_1} \le 1$ and $0 \le S_{n \wedge \tau_1} \le e^\sigma$: bounded. (4) Let $n \to \infty$. On $\{\tau_1 < \infty\}$, $S_{n \wedge \tau_1} \to e^\sigma\left(\frac2{e^\sigma + e^{-\sigma}}\right)^{\tau_1}$; on $\{\tau_1 = \infty\}$ it tends to 0 because the factor $\left(\frac2{e^\sigma + e^{-\sigma}}\right)^n \to 0$ and $e^{\sigma M_n} \le e^\sigma$. Bounded convergence gives $E\left[e^\sigma\left(\frac2{e^\sigma + e^{-\sigma}}\right)^{\tau_1}\mathbb 1\{\tau_1 < \infty\}\right] = 1$. (5) Let $\sigma \downarrow 0$: the factor tends to 1, so $P(\tau_1 < \infty) = 1$. (6) Set $\alpha = \frac2{e^\sigma + e^{-\sigma}}$ and solve for $e^{-\sigma}$: $\alpha e^{2\sigma} - 2e^\sigma + \alpha = 0$ gives $e^{-\sigma} = \frac{1 - \sqrt{1 - \alpha^2}}\alpha$ (the root below 1). So $E[\alpha^{\tau_1}] = e^{-\sigma} = \frac{1 - \sqrt{1 - \alpha^2}}\alpha$.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the martingale check (line 1). 2: stopping and the bound (lines 2 and 3). 2: the limit on both events, with the bounded convergence theorem named (line 4). 1: $P(\tau_1 < \infty) = 1$ (line 5). 3: inverting for $\alpha$ and choosing the right root (line 6), the creative step.
- *Example B (d5).* A three-period short-rate tree with $\tilde p = \frac12$: $R_0 = 5\%$; $R_1$ is 7% or 3%; from 7%, $R_2$ is 9% or 5%; from 3%, $R_2$ is 5% or 1%. Price the zero-coupon bond paying 1 at time 3, and a caplet paying $(R_2 - 5\%)^+$ at time 3 per unit notional.
  *Answer.* $B_{0,3} = \widetilde E[D_3] = \frac14\sum\frac1{1.05(1 + R_1)(1 + R_2)}$ over the four paths: $\frac14\left(\frac1{1.05 \cdot 1.07 \cdot 1.09} + \frac1{1.05 \cdot 1.07 \cdot 1.05} + \frac1{1.05 \cdot 1.03 \cdot 1.05} + \frac1{1.05 \cdot 1.03 \cdot 1.01}\right) \approx 0.86509$, a yield of about 4.95% a period. The caplet pays only on the path with $R_2 = 9\%$: $\frac14 \cdot \frac{0.04}{1.05 \cdot 1.07 \cdot 1.09} \approx 0.008166$. Decision point: the caplet's payment is discounted through $R_2$ itself, because the rate set at time 2 applies to the period ending at time 3; discounting from time 2 overstates it by the factor 1.09.

**BR1 Probability on infinite spaces** (`set.brownian.infinite-spaces`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the failure of $\lim E = E\lim$ and the dominated convergence theorem's hypothesis. Code drill `drl.stoch.importance` (d4): `naive(n, seed)` and `shifted(n, theta, seed)` estimate $P(Z > 4)$ and return estimate and standard error. Hidden tests: with $n = 10^5$ and seed 7 the shifted estimate lies within 4 standard errors of $3.1671 \times 10^{-5}$; the variance ratio naive to shifted at $\theta = 4$ exceeds 1,000; the mean of the weights under the shifted sampler lies within 4 standard errors of 1. Known-wrong: forgetting the likelihood ratio returns about 0.5 and fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* $U$ is uniform on $(0, 1)$ and $X_n = n\,\mathbb 1\{U < \frac1n\}$. Show that $X_n \to 0$ almost surely and $E[X_n] = 1$ for all $n$, and identify which hypothesis of the dominated convergence theorem fails, with proof.
  *Model answer.* (1) For each $u \in (0, 1)$, $X_n(u) = 0$ as soon as $n \ge \frac1u$, so $X_n(u) \to 0$; the event $U \in (0, 1)$ has probability 1. (2) $E[X_n] = nP(U < \frac1n) = n \cdot \frac1n = 1$. (3) So $\lim E[X_n] = 1 \ne 0 = E[\lim X_n]$. (4) Dominated convergence needs an integrable $Y$ with $\lvert X_n\rvert \le Y$ for all $n$. The smallest candidate is $Y = \sup_nX_n$; for $u \in [\frac1{k + 1}, \frac1k)$, $X_n(u) = n$ for $n \le k$, so $\sup_nX_n(u) = k > \frac1u - 1$. (5) $E[\sup_nX_n] \ge \int_0^1\left(\frac1u - 1\right)du = \infty$. No integrable bound exists, so the theorem does not apply. (6) Monotone convergence does not apply either: the sequence is not increasing.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: almost sure convergence (line 1). 1: the expectation (line 2). 1: the failure stated (line 3). 4: the supremum and its divergent integral (lines 4 and 5), the creative step. 2: monotone convergence ruled out (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* To estimate $p = P(Z > 4)$ for standard normal $Z$, sample $Y \sim \mathcal N(4, 1)$ and average $\mathbb 1\{Y > 4\}e^{-4Y + 8}$. Find the estimator's second moment in closed form and the variance reduction against plain sampling.
  *Answer.* The weight is the density ratio $\frac{\varphi(y)}{\varphi(y - 4)} = e^{-4y + 8}$. Second moment: $E[\mathbb 1\{Y > 4\}e^{-8Y + 16}]$; write $Y = 4 + W$ with $W$ standard normal: $e^{-16}E[\mathbb 1\{W > 0\}e^{-8W}] = e^{-16}e^{32}N(-8) = e^{16}N(-8) \approx 5.53 \times 10^{-9}$. Variance: $5.53 \times 10^{-9} - p^2 \approx 4.52 \times 10^{-9}$, with $p \approx 3.167 \times 10^{-5}$. Plain sampling has variance $p(1 - p) \approx 3.17 \times 10^{-5}$: a reduction of about 7,000 times, so 7,000 times fewer samples for the same error. Decision point: shifting the mean to the boundary of the rare event, not beyond it, is the near-optimal choice here; shifting much further makes the weights tiny on most samples and the variance grows again.

**BR2 Information and conditioning** (`set.brownian.conditioning`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: $E[X \mid X + Y] = \frac{X + Y}2$ by symmetry and partial averaging. Code drill `drl.stoch.cond-normal` (d3): `cond_mean_var(mu, Sigma, i, j, value)` for a bivariate normal, and `mc_check(...)`. Hidden tests: $E[X \mid X + Y = 3] = 1.5$ with conditional variance 0.5 for independent standard normals; for correlation 0.6, $E[Y \mid X = 2] = 1.2$ with variance 0.64; the Monte Carlo check agrees within 4 standard errors. Known-wrong: returning the unconditional variance fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* $X$ and $Y$ are independent and identically distributed with finite mean. Prove $E[X \mid X + Y] = \frac{X + Y}2$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Let $S = X + Y$. By symmetry the pairs $(X, S)$ and $(Y, S)$ have the same joint distribution, because swapping $X$ and $Y$ leaves $S$ unchanged and the swap does not change the joint law of $(X, Y)$. (2) Hence for every event $A = \{S \in B\}$ in $\sigma(S)$, $E[X\mathbb 1_A] = E[Y\mathbb 1_A]$. (3) So $E[X \mid S]$ and $E[Y \mid S]$ have the same partial averages on every event of $\sigma(S)$, and by uniqueness they are equal almost surely. (4) By linearity, $E[X \mid S] + E[Y \mid S] = E[S \mid S] = S$. (5) So $E[X \mid S] = \frac S2$.
  *Rubric (10).* 3: the symmetry of the joint law of $(X, S)$ and $(Y, S)$, with its reason (line 1). 2: equal partial averages (line 2). 2: uniqueness (line 3). 2: linearity and taking out what is known (line 4). 1: conclusion (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* $X$ and $Y$ are independent standard normals. Find $E[\max(X, Y) \mid X]$ and evaluate it at $X = 1$.
  *Answer.* By the independence lemma, $E[\max(X, Y) \mid X] = g(X)$ with $g(x) = E[\max(x, Y)] = xP(Y \le x) + E[Y\mathbb 1\{Y > x\}] = xN(x) + \varphi(x)$. At $x = 1$: $0.8413 + 0.2420 = 1.0833$. Check: averaging $g(X)$ over $X$ must give $E[\max(X, Y)] = \frac1{\sqrt\pi} \approx 0.564$ (lesson 27 of 3.3). Decision point: freezing $X$ at $x$ and averaging only over $Y$ is legitimate because $Y$ is independent of $X$; for correlated $X$ and $Y$ the lemma does not apply and $Y$'s conditional law given $X$ must be used.

**BR3 Building Brownian motion** (`set.brownian.construction`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: time inversion. Code drill `drl.stoch.bridge` (d4): `refine(path, times, seed)` doubles the grid by Brownian-bridge midpoints. Hidden tests: refining keeps every coarse point unchanged; the sample covariance of $(W(0.3), W(0.7))$ over $10^4$ seeded paths is within 4 standard errors of 0.3; conditional on $W(1) = 1$ the midpoint has mean 0.5 and variance 0.25 within tolerance. Known-wrong: midpoint variance $\frac{t - s}2$ fails the third test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Let $X(0) = 0$ and $X(t) = tW(\frac1t)$ for $t > 0$. Show that $X$ has the covariance of Brownian motion and explain what else must be checked for $X$ to be one.
  *Model answer.* (1) $X$ is a Gaussian process with mean 0, because each $X(t)$ is a multiple of a normal variable and any finite collection is a linear image of a jointly normal vector. (2) For $0 < s \le t$: $\mathrm{Cov}(X(s), X(t)) = st\,\mathrm{Cov}(W(\frac1s), W(\frac1t)) = st\min(\frac1s, \frac1t) = st \cdot \frac1t = s$. (3) So the covariance is $\min(s, t)$, the covariance of Brownian motion, and a Gaussian process is determined by its mean and covariance: the finite-dimensional laws match. (4) Paths: for $t > 0$, $X$ is continuous because $W$ is. (5) At 0 continuity needs $tW(\frac1t) \to 0$ as $t \downarrow 0$, that is $\frac{W(u)}u \to 0$ as $u \to \infty$, the strong law of large numbers for Brownian motion. With it, $X$ is a Brownian motion.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: Gaussian process (line 1). 3: the covariance computation (line 2). 2: finite-dimensional laws determined (line 3). 1: continuity away from 0 (line 4). 2: continuity at 0 by the law of large numbers (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* Given $W(1) = 0$, find the chance that $\max_{0 \le t \le 1}W(t) \ge 1$.
  *Answer.* By reflection, for $m > 0$ and $w \le m$, $P(M(1) \ge m, W(1) \in dw) = \varphi(2m - w)\,dw$ (the reflected endpoint is $2m - w$). Dividing by $P(W(1) \in dw) = \varphi(w)\,dw$: $P(M(1) \ge m \mid W(1) = w) = \frac{\varphi(2m - w)}{\varphi(w)} = e^{-2m(m - w)}$. With $w = 0$ and $m = 1$: $e^{-2} \approx 0.135$. Decision point: the unconditional answer 0.317 is wrong here because pinning the end at 0 removes the paths that wander up and stay up; the bridge formula is also the barrier-crossing correction of lesson 39.

**BR4 Roughness: variation and martingales** (`set.brownian.variation`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the mean and variance of the quadratic-variation sum. Code drill `drl.stoch.qv` (d3): `variations(n, T, seed)` returns first and quadratic variation of a seeded path. Hidden tests: for $n = 10^4$, $T = 2$ the quadratic variation lies within 4 of its standard deviations $\sqrt{\frac{2T^2}n}$ of 2; first variation divided by $\sqrt n$ is within tolerance of $\sqrt{\frac{2T}\pi}$; for $f(t) = 3t$ the quadratic variation with $n$ steps equals $\frac{9T^2}n$ exactly. Known-wrong: summing absolute increments for the quadratic variation fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For $n$ equal steps on $[0, T]$, prove $E\left[\sum_j(\Delta W_j)^2\right] = T$ and $\mathrm{Var}\left(\sum_j(\Delta W_j)^2\right) = \frac{2T^2}n$, and conclude that the sum converges to $T$ in mean square.
  *Model answer.* The full model answer is the derivation of section 7 (screens 6 to 8): each $\Delta W_j \sim \mathcal N(0, \frac Tn)$, so $E[(\Delta W_j)^2] = \frac Tn$ and the sum has mean $T$; $E[(\Delta W_j)^4] = 3\left(\frac Tn\right)^2$ gives $\mathrm{Var}((\Delta W_j)^2) = 2\left(\frac Tn\right)^2$; independence of increments adds the variances to $\frac{2T^2}n$; mean-square convergence follows because $E\left[\left(\sum(\Delta W_j)^2 - T\right)^2\right] = \frac{2T^2}n \to 0$.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: each increment's law (line 1). 2: the mean (line 2). 3: the fourth moment and the variance of one squared increment (line 3). 2: independence and the sum (line 4). 1: mean-square convergence (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* Show that $\sum_j\lvert\Delta W_j\rvert^3 \to 0$ in mean as $n \to \infty$, compute its expectation for $T = 1$, $n = 100$, and say why this justifies dropping third-order terms in Itô's formula.
  *Answer.* $\lvert\Delta W_j\rvert \stackrel d= \sqrt{\frac Tn}\lvert Z\rvert$ with $E\lvert Z\rvert^3 = 2\sqrt{\frac2\pi} \approx 1.596$, so $E\sum\lvert\Delta W_j\rvert^3 = n\left(\frac Tn\right)^{3/2}2\sqrt{\frac2\pi} = \frac{2\sqrt{2/\pi}\,T^{3/2}}{\sqrt n}$, which is about 0.160 for $T = 1$, $n = 100$, and tends to 0. In the Taylor expansion of $f(W)$ the third-order term is bounded by $\frac16\max\lvert f'''\rvert\sum\lvert\Delta W_j\rvert^3$, which therefore vanishes. Decision point: the second-order sum survives (it tends to $T$) and the third-order sum does not; that line, between powers 2 and 3, is the whole difference between Itô's formula and the ordinary chain rule.

**BR5 Hitting times and the maximum** (`set.brownian.hitting`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: exit from an interval with drift. Code drill `drl.stoch.hitting` (d4): `hit_prob(mu, sigma, a, b, n_paths, dt, seed)` simulates with the bridge crossing correction. Hidden tests: for $\mu = 0.1$, $\sigma = 1$, $a = b = 1$ the estimate is within 4 standard errors of 0.54983; for $\mu = 0$ it is within 4 standard errors of $\frac b{a + b}$; the expected exit time estimate is within 4 standard errors of 0.99668. Known-wrong: no bridge correction with $\Delta t = 0.01$ biases the exit time upwards beyond the tolerance.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For $X(t) = \mu t + \sigma W(t)$ with $\mu \ne 0$, $a, b > 0$ and $\tau$ the first exit time from $(-b, a)$, derive $P(X(\tau) = a)$ and $E[\tau]$.
