  *Model answer.* (1) With $\theta = \frac{2\mu}{\sigma^2}$, $Y(t) = e^{-\theta X(t)}$ is a martingale: by Itô, $dY = Y\left(-\theta\,dX + \frac12\theta^2\sigma^2\,dt\right) = Y\left(-\theta\mu + \frac12\theta^2\sigma^2\right)dt - \theta\sigma Y\,dW$, and the $dt$ coefficient is $-\frac{2\mu^2}{\sigma^2} + \frac{2\mu^2}{\sigma^2} = 0$. (2) $Y$ is bounded up to $\tau$ and $\tau < \infty$ almost surely, so optional stopping gives $1 = E[Y(\tau)] = Pe^{-\theta a} + (1 - P)e^{\theta b}$. (3) Solve: $P = \frac{1 - e^{\theta b}}{e^{-\theta a} - e^{\theta b}}$. (4) $X(t) - \mu t$ is a martingale; with $E[\tau] < \infty$ (it is dominated by a geometric number of unit-time trials), optional stopping gives $E[X(\tau)] = \mu E[\tau]$. (5) So $E[\tau] = \frac{aP - b(1 - P)}\mu$. (6) Check: $\mu = 0.1$, $\sigma = 1$, $a = b = 1$ gives $P \approx 0.550$ and $E[\tau] \approx 0.997$; as $\mu \to 0$, $P \to \frac b{a + b}$.
  *Rubric (10).* 3: the martingale $e^{-\theta X}$ with the Itô check (line 1). 2: optional stopping with its justification (line 2). 1: solving for $P$ (line 3). 2: the second martingale and its justification (line 4). 1: $E[\tau]$ (line 5). 1: a check (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* For $X(t) = \mu t + W(t)$ with $\mu = -0.5$, find $P\left(\max_{0 \le t \le 1}X(t) \ge 1\right)$, and its limit as the horizon grows.
  *Answer.* Girsanov turns the drifted motion into a driftless one with weight $e^{\mu W(1) - \frac12\mu^2}$; combined with the reflection principle this gives $P(\max_{t \le T}X(t) \ge m) = 1 - N\left(\frac{m - \mu T}{\sqrt T}\right) + e^{2\mu m}N\left(\frac{-m - \mu T}{\sqrt T}\right)$. With $m = 1$, $T = 1$, $\mu = -0.5$: $1 - N(1.5) + e^{-1}N(-0.5) \approx 0.0668 + 0.3679 \times 0.3085 \approx 0.180$. As $T \to \infty$ the first term tends to 0 and the second to $e^{2\mu m} = e^{-1} \approx 0.368$, lesson 50's answer. Decision point: the factor $e^{2\mu m}$ on the reflected term is where the drift enters; reflecting without it gives 0.375 at $T = 1$ (and the driftless formula gives 0.317): both are too high, and both tend to 1 rather than $e^{-1}$ as the horizon grows.

**IC1 The Itô integral** (`set.ito.integral`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the Itô isometry for simple integrands. Code drill `drl.stoch.ito-sums` (d3): `sums(path)` returns the left, right and average-endpoint sums of $\int W\,dW$. Hidden tests: right minus left equals the path's quadratic variation to $10^{-12}$; the average-endpoint sum equals $\frac12W(T)^2$ to $10^{-12}$; over 2,000 seeded paths the left sum's mean is within 4 standard errors of 0. Known-wrong: indexing the left sum with $W(t_{j+1})$ fails the third test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For a simple adapted integrand $\Delta(t) = \Delta_j$ on $[t_j, t_{j+1})$, prove $E\left[\left(\sum_j\Delta_jD_j\right)^2\right] = E\left[\sum_j\Delta_j^2(t_{j+1} - t_j)\right]$, where $D_j = W(t_{j+1}) - W(t_j)$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Expand: $\left(\sum_j\Delta_jD_j\right)^2 = \sum_j\Delta_j^2D_j^2 + 2\sum_{i < j}\Delta_i\Delta_jD_iD_j$. (2) Diagonal terms: $\Delta_j$ is known at $t_j$ and $D_j$ is independent of $\mathcal F(t_j)$, so $E[\Delta_j^2D_j^2] = E[\Delta_j^2E[D_j^2 \mid \mathcal F(t_j)]] = E[\Delta_j^2](t_{j+1} - t_j)$. (3) Cross terms with $i < j$: $\Delta_i$, $D_i$ and $\Delta_j$ are known at $t_j$, and $E[D_j \mid \mathcal F(t_j)] = 0$, so $E[\Delta_i\Delta_jD_iD_j] = E[\Delta_i\Delta_jD_iE[D_j \mid \mathcal F(t_j)]] = 0$. (4) Sum: $E[I^2] = \sum_jE[\Delta_j^2](t_{j+1} - t_j) = E\int_0^T\Delta(t)^2\,dt$.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the expansion (line 1). 3: the diagonal terms by conditioning (line 2). 4: the cross terms, naming which quantities are known at $t_j$ (line 3), the step that needs adaptedness. 1: the conclusion (line 4).
- *Example B (d5).* Compute $E\left[\left(\int_0^TW(t)^2\,dW(t)\right)^2\right]$, and $\mathrm{Var}\left(\int_0^1W(t)\,dt\right)$ by the isometry.
  *Answer.* By the isometry, $\int_0^TE[W(t)^4]\,dt = \int_0^T3t^2\,dt = T^3$. For the time integral, $\int_0^1W(t)\,dt = \int_0^1(1 - t)\,dW(t)$ (integrate $d(tW) = W\,dt + t\,dW$ and rearrange), so its variance is $\int_0^1(1 - t)^2\,dt = \frac13$. Decision point: the isometry applies to $\int\dots dW$, not to $\int\dots dt$; the rewriting by the product rule is the step that makes it applicable.

**IC2 Itô's formula** (`set.ito.formula`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: Itô's formula for $f(W)$ from Taylor's theorem. Code drill `drl.stoch.ito-check` (d4): `moment_path(k, t_grid, n_paths, seed)` estimates $E[W(t)^k]$ and `martingale_check(f, ...)` compares $E[f(t, W(t))]$ across times. Hidden tests: $E[W(2)^4]$ within 4 standard errors of 12; $W^2 - t$ and $e^{W - t/2}$ pass the flat-mean check at three times; $W^3$ passes it too (mean 0) but fails a conditional check from $W(1) = 1$, where $E[W(2)^3 \mid W(1) = 1] = 4$. Known-wrong: a martingale test on unconditional means only passes $W^3$ and fails the last test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Derive $f(W(T)) - f(W(0)) = \int_0^Tf'(W)\,dW + \frac12\int_0^Tf''(W)\,dt$ for $f$ with bounded, continuous third derivative, stating which terms vanish and why.
  *Model answer.* (1) Partition $[0, T]$ into $n$ steps; telescoping, $f(W(T)) - f(W(0)) = \sum_j[f(W_{j+1}) - f(W_j)]$. (2) Taylor to second order with remainder: each term is $f'(W_j)\Delta W_j + \frac12f''(W_j)(\Delta W_j)^2 + R_j$ with $\lvert R_j\rvert \le \frac16\max\lvert f'''\rvert\,\lvert\Delta W_j\rvert^3$. (3) $\sum_jf'(W_j)\Delta W_j \to \int_0^Tf'(W)\,dW$: left-point sums, the Itô integral. (4) $\sum_jf''(W_j)(\Delta W_j)^2 \to \int_0^Tf''(W)\,dt$: the quadratic-variation argument of lesson 42 with the weights $f''(W_j)$, because $(\Delta W_j)^2$ concentrates at $\Delta t$. (5) $\sum_j\lvert R_j\rvert \le c\sum_j\lvert\Delta W_j\rvert^3 \to 0$ (homework BR4 example B). (6) Collect: the formula, whose $\frac12f''\,dt$ term is the one ordinary calculus lacks.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: telescoping (line 1). 2: Taylor with the remainder bound (line 2). 2: the Itô integral as the limit of left sums (line 3). 3: the second-order sum to $\int f''\,dt$ by quadratic variation (line 4), the leap. 1: the remainder vanishes (line 5). 1: the conclusion (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Show $E[W(t)^{2k}] = (2k - 1)!!\,t^k$ using only Itô's formula, and compute $E[e^{aW(t)}]$ the same way.
  *Answer.* $d(W^{2k}) = 2kW^{2k - 1}\,dW + k(2k - 1)W^{2k - 2}\,dt$. Taking expectations (the $dW$ integral has mean 0), $m_{2k}(t) = k(2k - 1)\int_0^tm_{2k - 2}(s)\,ds$ with $m_0 = 1$. Induction: if $m_{2k - 2}(s) = (2k - 3)!!\,s^{k - 1}$, then $m_{2k}(t) = k(2k - 1)(2k - 3)!!\frac{t^k}k = (2k - 1)!!\,t^k$. For the exponential, $d(e^{aW}) = ae^{aW}\,dW + \frac12a^2e^{aW}\,dt$, so $m(t) = E[e^{aW(t)}]$ solves $m' = \frac12a^2m$ with $m(0) = 1$: $m(t) = e^{a^2t/2}$. Decision point: taking expectations kills the $dW$ term only because the integrand has finite second moment, which these polynomials and exponentials of Brownian motion do.

**IC3 Black-Scholes-Merton by replication** (`set.ito.bsm`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the BSM equation by replication. Code drill `drl.stoch.delta-hedge` (d4): `hedge(sigma_imp, sigma_real, sigma_hedge, n_steps, n_paths, seed)` sells a call, hedges discretely and returns the mean and standard deviation of the final P&L. Hidden tests: with all three volatilities equal, the mean P&L is within 4 standard errors of 0 and quadrupling `n_steps` roughly halves the standard deviation (ratio between 0.4 and 0.6); with implied 20% and real and hedge volatility 25%, the mean P&L is within 4 standard errors of $-1.8854$ and its standard deviation falls with `n_steps`; with hedging at the implied volatility, the mean is close to $-1.89$ but the standard deviation does not fall to 0. Known-wrong: rebalancing with the delta of the previous step's price misses the drift and fails the second test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Derive the Black-Scholes-Merton equation for $c(t, x)$ by replicating a call with shares and the bank, for $dS = \alpha S\,dt + \sigma S\,dW$ and interest rate $r$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Wealth: $dX = \Delta\,dS + r(X - \Delta S)\,dt$, so $d(e^{-rt}X) = \Delta e^{-rt}(\alpha - r)S\,dt + \Delta e^{-rt}\sigma S\,dW$. (2) Itô for $c(t, S(t))$: $dc = \left(c_t + \alpha Sc_x + \frac12\sigma^2S^2c_{xx}\right)dt + \sigma Sc_x\,dW$, so $d(e^{-rt}c) = e^{-rt}\left(-rc + c_t + \alpha Sc_x + \frac12\sigma^2S^2c_{xx}\right)dt + e^{-rt}\sigma Sc_x\,dW$. (3) Replication requires $d(e^{-rt}X) = d(e^{-rt}c)$ for all $t$. Match the $dW$ terms: $\Delta = c_x(t, S)$, the delta hedge. (4) Match the $dt$ terms with $\Delta = c_x$: $(\alpha - r)Sc_x = -rc + c_t + \alpha Sc_x + \frac12\sigma^2S^2c_{xx}$. (5) The $\alpha Sc_x$ terms cancel, leaving $c_t + rSc_x + \frac12\sigma^2S^2c_{xx} = rc$ for all $t < T$ and $x > 0$, with $c(T, x) = (x - K)^+$. (6) With $X(0) = c(0, S(0))$ the portfolio equals the call at every time, so no-arbitrage forces the price $c(0, S(0))$.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the discounted wealth differential (line 1). 2: Itô for $c$ (line 2). 2: matching $dW$ to get delta (line 3). 2: matching $dt$ and the cancellation of $\alpha$ (lines 4 and 5). 1: the terminal condition (line 5). 1: the pricing conclusion (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* You sell a one-year at-the-money call ($S = K = 100$, $r = 5\%$) at 20% implied volatility; the share's true volatility is 25%. You delta-hedge continuously using the delta at 25%. What is your final P&L, and why is it not random?
  *Answer.* Hedging with the true volatility replicates the call exactly at a cost of its price at 25%, 12.336; you received its price at 20%, 10.451. The P&L is $10.451 - 12.336 = -1.885$ (in time-0 money), the same on every path. Decision point: with the delta at the implied volatility instead, the expected loss is about the same but the P&L becomes path-dependent, $\int\frac12e^{-rt}\Gamma_iS^2(\sigma_i^2 - \sigma_r^2)\,dt$ with gamma at 20%, because the gamma you are short is concentrated where the share happens to wander.

**IC4 Many dimensions and simulation** (`set.ito.multidim`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the volatility of a ratio. Code drill `drl.stoch.euler-orders` (d4): `orders(scheme, mu, sigma, T, steps_list, n_paths, seed)` estimates strong and weak errors on GBM against the exact solution on the same increments and fits their orders. Hidden tests: Euler's fitted strong order lies in $[0.4, 0.6]$ and its weak order in $[0.8, 1.2]$; Milstein's strong order lies in $[0.8, 1.2]$; the weak error at 10 steps with $\mu = 0.1$, $T = 1$ is within 2% of 0.000549 (exact by formula, no simulation). Known-wrong: comparing against an independent exact sample instead of the same increments makes the strong error constant and fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For $dS_i = \alpha_iS_i\,dt + \sigma_iS_i\,dW_i$ ($i = 1, 2$) with $dW_1\,dW_2 = \rho\,dt$, show that $R = \frac{S_1}{S_2}$ is a geometric Brownian motion and find its volatility.
  *Model answer.* (1) $d\ln S_i = \left(\alpha_i - \frac12\sigma_i^2\right)dt + \sigma_i\,dW_i$ by Itô. (2) $\ln R = \ln S_1 - \ln S_2$, so $d\ln R = \left(\alpha_1 - \alpha_2 - \frac12\sigma_1^2 + \frac12\sigma_2^2\right)dt + \sigma_1\,dW_1 - \sigma_2\,dW_2$. (3) The noise term $\sigma_1W_1 - \sigma_2W_2$ is a continuous martingale with quadratic variation rate $\sigma_1^2 + \sigma_2^2 - 2\rho\sigma_1\sigma_2$, so by Lévy's characterisation it equals $\sigma_RB$ for a Brownian motion $B$ with $\sigma_R = \sqrt{\sigma_1^2 + \sigma_2^2 - 2\rho\sigma_1\sigma_2}$. (4) $\ln R$ is a Brownian motion with drift and volatility $\sigma_R$, so $R$ is a GBM. (5) With $\sigma_1 = 30\%$, $\sigma_2 = 20\%$ and $\rho = 0.5$, $\sigma_R = \sqrt{0.07} \approx 26.5\%$.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: logs of each share (line 1). 2: the difference (line 2). 4: the quadratic variation of the combined noise and Lévy's characterisation (line 3), the step that turns two motions into one. 1: the GBM conclusion (line 4). 1: the number (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* For GBM with $\mu = 0.1$, $T = 1$ and $S_0 = 1$, compute the Euler scheme's weak error $\lvert E[S_T] - E[S_T^{\text{Euler}}]\rvert$ exactly for 10 and 20 steps, and derive its leading term in $\Delta t$.
  *Answer.* Each Euler step multiplies the mean by $1 + \mu\Delta t$, so $E[S_T^{\text{Euler}}] = (1 + \mu\Delta t)^N$ while $E[S_T] = e^{\mu T}$. For $N = 10$: $e^{0.1} - 1.01^{10} = 1.105171 - 1.104622 = 0.000549$; for $N = 20$: $e^{0.1} - 1.005^{20} = 0.000275$, half as much. Expanding $N\ln(1 + \mu\Delta t) = \mu T - \frac12\mu^2T\Delta t + O(\Delta t^2)$ gives error $\approx e^{\mu T}\frac12\mu^2T\Delta t$, which predicts 0.000553 for $N = 10$. Decision point: the weak error needs no simulation at all here; a Monte Carlo estimate of it would need billions of paths to resolve 0.0005 against the payoff's spread.

**RN1 Girsanov and the risk-neutral measure** (`set.risk-neutral.girsanov`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: Girsanov's theorem for constant $\theta$. Code drill `drl.stoch.rn-pricer` (d3): `price_by_integration(payoff, S0, r, sigma, T)` integrates against the risk-neutral lognormal density with a trapezoid rule and `math.erf`. Hidden tests: the at-the-money call gives 10.45058 within $10^{-4}$; the digital call 0.53232; the asset-or-nothing call 63.6831; a put plus the share minus the call equals $Ke^{-rT}$ within $10^{-6}$. Known-wrong: integrating with the drift $\alpha = 12\%$ returns about 15.5 and fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Let $Z(t) = e^{-\theta W(t) - \frac12\theta^2t}$ for a constant $\theta$, $\widetilde P(A) = E[Z(T)\mathbb 1_A]$ and $\widetilde W(t) = W(t) + \theta t$. Prove that $\widetilde W$ is a Brownian motion under $\widetilde P$ on $[0, T]$.
  *Model answer.* (1) $Z$ is a positive $P$-martingale with $Z(0) = 1$ (lesson 44), so $\widetilde P$ is a probability measure equivalent to $P$. (2) By Lévy's characterisation it suffices that $\widetilde W$ is a continuous $\widetilde P$-martingale with $[\widetilde W, \widetilde W](t) = t$. (3) Continuity: $W$ is continuous and $\theta t$ is. Quadratic variation: adding the finite-variation term $\theta t$ does not change it, and quadratic variation is a pathwise quantity, the same under equivalent measures, so $[\widetilde W, \widetilde W](t) = t$. (4) Martingale: by lesson 34, $\widetilde E[\widetilde W(t) \mid \mathcal F(s)] = \frac{E[\widetilde W(t)Z(t) \mid \mathcal F(s)]}{Z(s)}$, so it suffices that $\widetilde WZ$ is a $P$-martingale. (5) Itô's product rule with $dZ = -\theta Z\,dW$: $d(\widetilde WZ) = \widetilde W\,dZ + Z\,d\widetilde W + d\widetilde W\,dZ = -\theta\widetilde WZ\,dW + Z\,dW + \theta Z\,dt - \theta Z\,dt = (Z - \theta\widetilde WZ)\,dW$, with no $dt$ term: a martingale. (6) So $\widetilde W$ is a $\widetilde P$-Brownian motion.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: $Z$ a positive martingale (line 1). 1: the route through Lévy (line 2). 2: continuity and quadratic variation under the new measure (line 3). 2: Bayes' formula to move the martingale question to $P$ (line 4). 3: the product rule and the cancelling $dt$ terms (line 5). 1: conclusion (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* With $S_0 = 100$, $r = 5\%$, $\sigma = 20\%$ and $T = 1$, price a claim paying $\ln S_T$ and an asset-or-nothing put paying $S_T\mathbb 1\{S_T < 100\}$.
  *Answer.* Under $\widetilde P$, $\ln S_T \sim \mathcal N\left(\ln S_0 + (r - \frac12\sigma^2)T, \sigma^2T\right)$, so the log claim is worth $e^{-rT}(\ln 100 + 0.03) = 0.951229 \times 4.63517 \approx 4.4091$. For the asset-or-nothing put, change to the share measure, under which $\ln S_T$ has mean $\ln S_0 + (r + \frac12\sigma^2)T$: its price is $S_0\widehat P(S_T < K) = S_0N(-d_1) = 100N(-0.35) \approx 36.32$. Check: with the asset-or-nothing call, 63.68, the two sum to $S_0 = 100$, as they must. Decision point: the second claim pays in shares, so the share is the natural numéraire; computing it under $\widetilde P$ needs an extra completing-the-square step that the change of numéraire does once and for all.

**RN2 Completeness, the fundamental theorems and replication** (`set.risk-neutral.ftap`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the first fundamental theorem. Code drill `drl.stoch.static-rep` (d4): `replicate(f, fprime, K0, strikes, call_put_prices, S0, r, T)` prices a smooth payoff from a strip of options. Hidden tests: for $f(x) = x^2$, $K_0 = 100$ and strikes every 1 from 0.5 to 599.5, the price is within 0.1 of 10,941.74; with strikes every 0.25 it is within 0.01; the bond-and-forward part alone is 10,487.71. Known-wrong: weights $f''(K)$ without the $dK$ factor fail the first test by a factor of the grid spacing.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove that if a risk-neutral measure $\widetilde P$ exists (equivalent to $P$, with every discounted portfolio a $\widetilde P$-martingale), then no arbitrage exists.
  *Model answer.* (1) Suppose a portfolio has $X(0) = 0$, $P(X(T) \ge 0) = 1$ and $P(X(T) > 0) > 0$. (2) Its discounted value is a $\widetilde P$-martingale, so $\widetilde E[D(T)X(T)] = X(0) = 0$. (3) Equivalent measures share their null sets, so $\widetilde P(X(T) \ge 0) = 1$. (4) A non-negative random variable with zero expectation is zero almost surely: $\widetilde P(D(T)X(T) > 0) = 0$, and $D(T) > 0$, so $\widetilde P(X(T) > 0) = 0$. (5) By equivalence again, $P(X(T) > 0) = 0$, contradicting (1). So no arbitrage exists.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the definition of an arbitrage (line 1). 2: the martingale step (line 2). 2: equivalence for $X(T) \ge 0$ (line 3). 3: non-negative with zero mean implies zero (line 4). 2: back to $P$ and the contradiction (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* Two shares on one Brownian motion: $\alpha_1 = 10\%$, $\sigma_1 = 20\%$; $\alpha_2 = 16\%$, $\sigma_2 = 30\%$; $r = 2\%$. Build an arbitrage, state its riskless return, and show that no risk-neutral measure exists.
  *Answer.* Hold $x_1$ dollars in share 1 and $x_2$ in share 2, financed at $r$; the noise is $(x_1\sigma_1 + x_2\sigma_2)\,dW$, zero when $x_1 = -\frac{\sigma_2}{\sigma_1}x_2 = -1.5x_2$. With $x_2 = 1$ and $x_1 = -1.5$, the excess drift is $1 \times (0.16 - 0.02) - 1.5 \times (0.10 - 0.02) = 0.14 - 0.12 = 0.02$: a riskless 2% a year above the bank on USD 1 of gross exposure per leg, scaled as far as you like. A risk-neutral measure would need one market price of risk $\theta$ with $\alpha_i - r = \sigma_i\theta$ for both shares, giving $\theta = 0.4$ and $\theta \approx 0.467$, a contradiction. Decision point: different expected returns alone are no arbitrage; only different Sharpe ratios on the same noise are.

**RN3 SDEs, Feynman-Kac and numerical pricing** (`set.risk-neutral.pde`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the Feynman-Kac theorem. Code drill `drl.stoch.fd-pricer` (d4): `crank_nicolson(S0, K, r, sigma, T, n_s, n_t, rannacher=True)` prices a call on a log-price grid. Hidden tests: within $10^{-3}$ of 10.45058 at $n_s = 400$, $n_t = 200$; the explicit scheme at a time step above its stability limit returns a value above $10^6$ in absolute size (blow-up detected); halving both steps reduces the error by roughly 4 (second order). Known-wrong: no Rannacher start gives a delta that oscillates near the strike and fails a smoothness test on the computed delta.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Let $dX = \beta(t, X)\,dt + \gamma(t, X)\,dW$ and $g(t, x) = E^{t,x}[h(X(T))]$. Show that, if $g$ is smooth, it solves $g_t + \beta g_x + \frac12\gamma^2g_{xx} = 0$ with $g(T, x) = h(x)$.
  *Model answer.* (1) By the Markov property (lesson 85), $E[h(X(T)) \mid \mathcal F(t)] = g(t, X(t))$. (2) The left side is a martingale in $t$ (a conditional expectation of a fixed variable, by the tower property). (3) Itô's formula: $dg(t, X(t)) = \left(g_t + \beta g_x + \frac12\gamma^2g_{xx}\right)dt + \gamma g_x\,dW$. (4) A continuous martingale that is an Itô process has zero $dt$ term, so $g_t + \beta g_x + \frac12\gamma^2g_{xx} = 0$ along the path. (5) Paths visit every $(t, x)$ in the state space (with positive density for non-degenerate $\gamma$), so the equation holds everywhere. (6) At $t = T$, $g(T, x) = h(x)$. The discounted version $E[e^{-r(T - t)}h(X(T))]$ adds $-rg$ to the equation, which with $\beta = rx$ and $\gamma = \sigma x$ is the BSM equation.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the Markov property (line 1). 2: the martingale (line 2). 2: Itô (line 3). 2: zero drift and the PDE (lines 4 and 5). 1: the terminal condition (line 6). 1: the discounted version linked to BSM (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* In the Vasicek model $dR = \kappa(\theta - R)\,dt + \sigma\,d\widetilde W$ with $R(0) = 3\%$, $\kappa = 0.5$, $\theta = 4\%$, $\sigma = 1\%$, price the one-year zero-coupon bond and give its yield.
  *Answer.* With $B(t, T) = e^{-C(\tau)R(t) - A(\tau)}$ and $\tau = T - t$, the bond PDE gives $C' = 1 - \kappa C$ and $A' = \kappa\theta C - \frac12\sigma^2C^2$ with $C(0) = A(0) = 0$, so $C(\tau) = \frac{1 - e^{-\kappa\tau}}\kappa$ and $A(\tau) = \left(\theta - \frac{\sigma^2}{2\kappa^2}\right)(\tau - C) + \frac{\sigma^2C^2}{4\kappa}$. At $\tau = 1$: $C \approx 0.78694$, $A \approx 0.0085108$, $B(0, 1) = e^{-0.78694 \times 0.03 - 0.0085108} \approx 0.96839$, a yield of about 3.21%. Decision point: the yield lies between today's 3% and the long-run 4% because rates are expected to drift up, and slightly below that drift's effect because of convexity (the $-\frac{\sigma^2}{2\kappa^2}$ and $\frac{\sigma^2C^2}{4\kappa}$ terms).

**EX1 Exotic and American options** (`set.exotics-rates.exotics`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the perpetual American put by smooth pasting. Code drill `drl.stoch.barrier-mc` (d4): `up_and_out(S0, K, B, r, sigma, T, n_steps, n_paths, seed, bridge)` prices by simulation. Hidden tests: with the bridge correction and 250 steps the estimate is within 4 standard errors of 1.17607; without it the estimate is significantly higher (more than 4 standard errors above); a barrier below the strike gives 0 for an up-and-out call struck above it. Known-wrong: checking the barrier only at the final time returns the price of the payoff $(S_T - K)^+\mathbb 1\{S_T < B\}$ (a call spread less a digital paying $B - K$) and fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Derive the perpetual American put's value $v(x) = (K - L)\left(\frac xL\right)^{-2r/\sigma^2}$ for the rule "exercise at the first time $S$ falls to $L$", and find the best $L$.
  *Model answer.* (1) A perpetual claim has no time variable, so its price $v(x)$ solves the BSM equation without $v_t$: $rxv' + \frac12\sigma^2x^2v'' = rv$ for $x > L$. (2) Try $v = x^p$: $\frac12\sigma^2p(p - 1) + rp - r = 0$, with roots $p = 1$ and $p = -\frac{2r}{\sigma^2}$. (3) As $x \to \infty$ the put must be worth 0, so only $p = -\frac{2r}{\sigma^2}$ survives: $v(x) = c\,x^{-2r/\sigma^2}$. (4) At $x = L$, $v(L) = K - L$, so $v(x) = (K - L)\left(\frac xL\right)^{-2r/\sigma^2}$. (5) Maximise over $L$ at fixed $x$: $\frac d{dL}\left[(K - L)L^{2r/\sigma^2}\right] = 0$ gives $L^* = \frac{2r}{2r + \sigma^2}K$. (6) Smooth pasting: at $L^*$, $v'(L^*) = -1$, the payoff's slope. (7) For $K = 100$, $r = 5\%$, $\sigma = 20\%$: $L^* = \frac{0.1}{0.14} \times 100 \approx 71.43$ and $v(100) \approx 28.57 \times 1.4^{-2.5} \approx 12.32$.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the time-free equation (line 1). 2: the power solutions (line 2). 1: the boundary at infinity (line 3). 1: the value at $L$ (line 4). 2: optimising $L$ (line 5). 1: smooth pasting (line 6). 1: the numbers (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Price an up-and-out call with $S_0 = K = 100$, barrier 120, $T = 1$, $r = 5\%$, $\sigma = 20\%$, from the killed density of $X = \ln\frac S{S_0}$, and explain why its vega is negative.
  *Answer.* Under $\widetilde P$, $X(t) = \nu t + \sigma\widetilde W(t)$ with $\nu = r - \frac12\sigma^2 = 0.03$. Reflection with Girsanov gives the density of $X(T)$ on paths whose maximum stays below $b = \ln 1.2$: $f(x) = \frac1{\sigma\sqrt T}\left[\varphi\left(\frac{x - \nu T}{\sigma\sqrt T}\right) - e^{2\nu b/\sigma^2}\varphi\left(\frac{x - 2b - \nu T}{\sigma\sqrt T}\right)\right]$ for $x < b$. The price is $e^{-rT}\int_0^b(S_0e^x - K)f(x)\,dx \approx 1.176$, against 10.451 for the vanilla; a simulation with the bridge correction gives $1.175 \pm 0.007$. More volatility raises the chance of hitting the barrier faster than it raises the payoff below it, so the vega is about $-13$ per unit of volatility. Decision point: the reflected term carries the factor $e^{2\nu b/\sigma^2}$; dropping it (reflecting driftless motion) gives a different, wrong price.

**EX2 Numéraires and interest-rate models** (`set.exotics-rates.rates`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: Margrabe's formula by change of numéraire. Code drill `drl.stoch.vasicek` (d3): `bond_mc(r0, kappa, theta, sigma, T, n_paths, seed)` simulates the exact Ornstein-Uhlenbeck transition and the integral of the rate; `bond_affine(...)` gives the formula. Hidden tests: the formula gives 0.968391 for homework RN3's inputs; the simulation is within 4 standard errors of it; $P(R(1) < 0)$ for lesson 98's inputs is 0.000228 within $10^{-5}$. Known-wrong: discounting by $e^{-R(T)T}$ instead of $e^{-\int_0^TR}$ fails the second test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Two shares follow GBMs with volatilities $\sigma_1$, $\sigma_2$ and correlation $\rho$, no dividends. Price the option paying $(S_1(T) - S_2(T))^+$ by taking $S_2$ as numéraire.
  *Model answer.* (1) Under the measure $P^{(2)}$ with numéraire $S_2$, every traded price divided by $S_2$ is a martingale. (2) The price is $S_2(0)E^{(2)}\left[\frac{(S_1(T) - S_2(T))^+}{S_2(T)}\right] = S_2(0)E^{(2)}[(R(T) - 1)^+]$ with $R = \frac{S_1}{S_2}$. (3) $R$ is a $P^{(2)}$-martingale and a GBM with volatility $\sigma_R = \sqrt{\sigma_1^2 + \sigma_2^2 - 2\rho\sigma_1\sigma_2}$ (homework IC4), so the expectation is a Black-Scholes call with zero rate, spot $R(0)$ and strike 1. (4) Price: $S_1(0)N(d_1) - S_2(0)N(d_2)$ with $d_{1,2} = \frac{\ln\frac{S_1(0)}{S_2(0)} \pm \frac12\sigma_R^2T}{\sigma_R\sqrt T}$. (5) The interest rate does not appear, because both legs are shares. (6) For $S_1 = S_2 = 100$, $\sigma_1 = 30\%$, $\sigma_2 = 20\%$, $\rho = 0.5$, $T = 1$: $\sigma_R \approx 0.2646$, $d_{1,2} = \pm0.1323$, price $\approx 10.52$.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the numéraire's martingale property (line 1). 2: rewriting the payoff in units of $S_2$ (line 2). 3: the ratio's law under the new measure (line 3). 1: the formula (line 4). 1: why $r$ vanishes (line 5). 1: the number (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A caplet on a six-month rate fixes in one year and pays at 1.5 years: forward rate 4%, strike 4%, Black volatility 20%, discount factor to the payment date 0.94, notional 1,000,000. Price it, and say why Black's formula is exact under the right measure.
  *Answer.* Under the 1.5-year forward measure the forward rate $F$ is a martingale; if it is lognormal with volatility 20%, the caplet is $\text{notional} \times 0.5 \times B(0, 1.5) \times [FN(d_1) - KN(d_2)]$ with $d_{1,2} = \pm\frac12\sigma\sqrt T = \pm0.1$: $1{,}000{,}000 \times 0.5 \times 0.94 \times 0.04 \times (N(0.1) - N(-0.1)) \approx 1{,}497.5$. Decision point: discounting from the payment date, not the fixing date, is what makes the forward rate a martingale under that bond's measure; using the one-year bond's measure would put a drift on $F$.

**EX3 Beyond constant volatility** (`set.exotics-rates.smile`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: Dupire's formula from the forward equation. Code drill `drl.stoch.merton` (d4): `merton_call(S0, K, r, sigma, T, lam, muJ, deltaJ, n_terms)` by the Poisson mixture, and `implied_vol(price, ...)` by bisection. Hidden tests: the at-the-money call is 11.6617 within $10^{-4}$; the implied volatilities at strikes 80, 100, 120 are 24.40%, 23.22%, 22.63% within 0.01 points; with $\lambda = 0$ the price equals Black-Scholes. Known-wrong: omitting the drift compensation $-\lambda k$ fails the first test (the discounted share is no longer a martingale).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* With zero rates and dividends, derive Dupire's formula $\sigma_{\text{loc}}^2(K, T) = \frac{2\,\partial_TC(K, T)}{K^2\,\partial_{KK}C(K, T)}$ for the model $dS = \sigma(t, S)S\,dW$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Breeden-Litzenberger: $\partial_{KK}C(K, T) = q(K, T)$, the risk-neutral density of $S_T$ at $K$. (2) The density solves the forward (Fokker-Planck) equation $\partial_Tq = \frac12\partial_{KK}\left(\sigma^2(T, K)K^2q\right)$. (3) Substitute $q = \partial_{KK}C$: $\partial_T\partial_{KK}C = \frac12\partial_{KK}\left(\sigma^2K^2\partial_{KK}C\right)$. (4) Integrate twice in $K$ from $\infty$, where $C$ and its derivatives vanish: $\partial_TC = \frac12\sigma^2(T, K)K^2\partial_{KK}C$. (5) Solve for $\sigma^2$. (6) Check: if implied volatility is flat at $\sigma_0$, Black-Scholes prices satisfy $\partial_TC = \frac12\sigma_0^2K^2\partial_{KK}C$, so local volatility is $\sigma_0$ everywhere.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: Breeden-Litzenberger (line 1). 2: the forward equation (line 2). 2: substitution (line 3). 3: integrating twice with the boundary terms justified (line 4), the step that turns a density statement into a price statement. 1: the formula and the flat check (lines 5 and 6).
- *Example B (d5).* In Merton's model with jump intensity 0.5 a year, $J \sim \mathcal N(-0.1, 0.15^2)$, diffusion volatility 20%, $r = 5\%$, price the at-the-money one-year call and find its implied volatility; then explain why the strike-80 put has a higher implied volatility than the strike-120 call.
  *Answer.* With $k = E[e^J] - 1 = e^{-0.1 + 0.01125} - 1 \approx -0.0849$ and $\lambda' = \lambda(1 + k)$, the price is $\sum_ne^{-\lambda'T}\frac{(\lambda'T)^n}{n!}\mathrm{BS}(\sigma_n, r_n)$ with $\sigma_n^2 = \sigma^2 + \frac{n\delta^2}T$ and $r_n = r - \lambda k + \frac{n\ln(1 + k)}T$: about 11.66, an implied volatility of 23.2%, against 10.45 at 20% without jumps. The jumps are downward on average, so they fatten the left tail of $\ln S_T$; deep out-of-the-money puts, which pay in that tail, become relatively dearer: 24.4% at 80 against 22.6% at 120. Decision point: the drift correction $-\lambda k$ is positive here (jumps are down on average), so the diffusion part drifts up to keep the discounted price a martingale; forgetting it misprices every strike.

### 3.2 Item 3.2: the Stochastic Lab project (`prj.stoch.lab`, 11.5 hours)

One repository on the learner's machine, built in four parts as the levels finish, with pytest tests in every part (the 1.2 exit habit) and a one-page results note at the end, in the format of 2.3's notes, including what failed. It runs on NumPy and the standard library, so every test also runs inside the app's Pyodide; plots use matplotlib on the learner's machine only. Milestones are verified by paste (`setup-checklist` project mode): the `pytest -q` summary and the printed tables below, which the app recomputes [our choice].

| Part | After | Hours | What is built | Tests the paste must show |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1. Paths and variation | BR5 | 2.5 | Brownian paths by bridge refinement on a counter-based generator; first and quadratic variation against the partition; the reflection principle and the bridge crossing correction | Coarse points unchanged by refinement; quadratic variation within 4 standard deviations of $T$ at $n = 10^4$; $P(\max_{t \le 1}W \ge 1)$ within 4 standard errors of 0.3173 |
| 2. Itô and SDEs | IC4 | 3.0 | Euler and Milstein for GBM with fitted strong and weak orders; exact Ornstein-Uhlenbeck sampling against Euler; CIR with full truncation, and the Feller condition's effect on hitting zero | Fitted orders in the IC4 drill's bands; OU's stationary variance $\frac{\sigma^2}{2\kappa}$ recovered within tolerance |
| 3. Three pricers agree | RN3 | 3.5 | A European call by formula, CRR tree, Crank-Nicolson with a Rannacher start, and Monte Carlo with the share as control variate; an up-and-out call by formula and by bridge-corrected simulation; an American put by tree and by Longstaff-Schwartz | All European prices within $10^{-3}$ of 10.45058 (Monte Carlo within its interval); the barrier within its interval of 1.17607; the American put by tree 6.0896 [verified at $n = 1{,}000$ in `stage1-dsa-cpp.md`] and by Longstaff-Schwartz within 0.02 of it |
| 4. Changing measure | EX1 | 2.5 | A deep out-of-the-money digital ($K = 160$) priced by plain and by importance-sampled simulation; the variance ratio; the results note | Both estimates within their intervals of the closed form $e^{-rT}N(d_2)$ (risk-neutral exercise chance about 0.0139); a variance ratio above 20 (about 28 when the mean is shifted to the strike) |

The capstone drill `drl.stoch.exit-three-pricers` (exams block, 3 hours, section 4.2) is part 3 rebuilt as an in-app drill with hidden tests, so 3.2's completion does not depend on a paste.

### 3.3 Item 3.3: the 28 sets

**PZ1 Method under the clock** (`set.puzzles.method`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: an explanation, as if aloud, of a small-cases argument. Code drill `drl.iv.enumerate` (d3): `prob(event, space)` enumerates a finite space of labelled outcomes and returns a `Fraction`; `pin_triples()` uses it. Hidden tests: `pin_triples()` returns $\frac{197}{1250}$; the product-even question gives $\frac{56}{81}$ with replacement and $\frac{13}{18}$ without; at least one ace in five cards gives $\frac{18472}{54145}$; an empty event gives 0. Known-wrong: enumerating unordered hands with `combinations_with_replacement` fails the third test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Explain, as you would aloud in an interview, how you find the expected number of rounds in lesson 2's hat game for $n$ people: small cases, the conjecture, and an argument that proves it.
  *Model answer.* (1) Clarify: each round is a uniformly random matching of the remaining people to their hats; matched people leave; we want $E[T]$ for $T$ the number of rounds. (2) $n = 1$: one round. $n = 2$: each round ends the game with chance $\frac12$ (the identity), else nobody leaves; a geometric wait with mean 2. $n = 3$: from 3 people a round leaves 0 (chance $\frac13$: the two 3-cycles), 1 (chance $\frac12$: the three transpositions) or 3 (chance $\frac16$); first-step analysis with $E_1 = 1$ and $E_2 = 2$ gives $E_3 = 1 + \frac13E_3 + \frac12E_2$, so $E_3 = 3$. (3) Conjecture: $E[T] = n$. (4) Argument: a random matching of $m$ people has 1 fixed point on average (indicators, 1.5). So if $X_k$ is the number left after round $k$, $E[X_k - X_{k+1} \mid X_k] = 1$ while $X_k > 0$, which makes $X_k + k$ a martingale up to $T$. (5) Optional stopping (bounded increments, $E[T] < \infty$ because each round ends the game with chance at least $\frac1{m!}$): $E[X_T + T] = X_0 = n$, and $X_T = 0$, so $E[T] = n$. (6) Check: the answer is an integer, grows linearly, and equals the small cases.
  *Rubric (10, the mock rubric).* 2: clarification of what a round is (line 1). 2: small cases done correctly, including $n = 3$ by first step (line 2). 1: the conjecture stated (line 3). 3: the martingale argument with the indicator fact (line 4), the creative step. 1: optional stopping justified (line 5). 1: the check (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A 6-digit PIN is chosen uniformly (leading zeros allowed). Find exactly the chance that some digit appears at least three times.
  *Answer.* Count PINs in which every digit appears at most twice: choose $a$ digits to appear twice and $6 - 2a$ to appear once, then arrange: $\sum_{a=0}^3\binom{10}a\binom{10 - a}{6 - 2a}\frac{6!}{2^a} = 151{,}200 + 453{,}600 + 226{,}800 + 10{,}800 = 842{,}400$. So the answer is $1 - \frac{842{,}400}{10^6} = \frac{197}{1{,}250} = 0.1576$. Check by the Bonferroni bracket of lesson 5: the union bound is 0.1585 and the only overlaps are two digits each appearing exactly three times, $\binom{10}2\binom63 = 900$ PINs, $0.0009$: $0.1585 - 0.0009 = 0.1576$. Decision point: the complement by multiplicities is exact and fast; inclusion-exclusion over digits is exact here only because overlaps of three digits are impossible in six places.

**PZ2 Expectation at speed** (`set.puzzles.expectation`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the tail-sum formula and the "until a decrease" problem. Code drill `drl.iv.expectations` (d3): `expected(count, space)` by enumeration and `tail_sum(p_greater, n_max)`. Hidden tests: colour changes with five red and five blue give 5; the maximum of three dice gives $\frac{119}{24}$; the tail sum of $\frac1{n!}$ to 20 terms is within $10^{-15}$ of $e$; the pay-to-reroll value with cost 1 is 4. Known-wrong: computing $E[\max]$ as $\max E$ (3.5) fails the second test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove $E[N] = \sum_{n \ge 0}P(N > n)$ for a random variable taking values in $\{0, 1, 2, \dots\}$, and use it to find the expected number of uniform draws until a draw is smaller than the one before.
  *Model answer.* (1) Write $N = \sum_{n \ge 0}\mathbb 1\{N > n\}$, since exactly $N$ of the indicators $n = 0, \dots, N - 1$ are 1. (2) Take expectations; the terms are non-negative, so the sum and expectation can be exchanged (monotone convergence): $E[N] = \sum_nP(N > n)$. (3) Let $N$ be the number of draws until the first decrease. $N > n$ exactly when the first $n$ draws are increasing ($n \ge 1$; and $N > 0$ always). (4) The $n!$ orderings of $n$ independent uniforms are equally likely (symmetry; ties have probability 0), so $P(N > n) = \frac1{n!}$. (5) $E[N] = 1 + 1 + \frac1{2!} + \frac1{3!} + \dots = e$. (6) Check: $N \ge 2$ always, and $e \approx 2.72$ is above 2.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the indicator representation (line 1). 2: the exchange justified (line 2). 2: the event $\{N > n\}$ identified (line 3). 3: $\frac1{n!}$ by symmetry (line 4). 1: the sum and the check (lines 5 and 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A deck is shuffled and dealt face up. Expected position of the second ace?
  *Answer.* The four aces cut the 48 other cards into five gaps (before the first ace, between aces, after the last); by symmetry each gap holds $\frac{48}5 = 9.6$ cards on average. The second ace's position is 2 plus the cards in the first two gaps: $2 + 2 \times 9.6 = \frac{106}5 = 21.2$. In general the $j$th of $k$ special cards in a deck of $N$ sits at $\frac{j(N + 1)}{k + 1}$ on average. Decision point: the five gaps are exchangeable (every arrangement of the aces among the positions is equally likely), which is the step that makes a one-line answer possible.

**PZ3 Conditional probability under pressure** (`set.puzzles.conditional`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: sequential Bayes in odds form. Code drill `drl.iv.bayes` (d3): `posterior(prior, likelihoods, observations)` in odds form with `Fraction`s. Hidden tests: lesson 13's posterior is $\frac{147}{272}$; ten right days give a posterior above 0.9 and nine do not; with equal likelihoods the posterior equals the prior; the coin-box problem gives $\frac56$ for keeping the coin. Known-wrong: multiplying probabilities instead of odds without renormalising fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* A signal is right with probability $a$ in state 1 and $b < a$ in state 2; the prior probability of state 1 is $\pi$. The signal is right on $k$ independent days in a row, the state fixed. Derive the posterior, and the smallest $k$ that takes it above $c$. Evaluate for lesson 13's numbers and $c = 0.9$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Prior odds $\frac\pi{1 - \pi}$. (2) Each right day multiplies the odds by the likelihood ratio $\frac ab$; independence given the state makes the ratios multiply. (3) Posterior odds $O_k = \frac\pi{1 - \pi}\left(\frac ab\right)^k$ and posterior probability $\frac{O_k}{1 + O_k}$. (4) Above $c$ when $O_k > \frac c{1 - c}$, that is $k > \frac{\ln\frac c{1 - c} - \ln\frac\pi{1 - \pi}}{\ln\frac ab}$. (5) With $a = 0.7$, $b = 0.5$, $\pi = 0.3$, $c = 0.9$: $\frac{\ln 9 - \ln\frac37}{\ln 1.4} = \frac{2.197 + 0.847}{0.336} \approx 9.05$, so $k = 10$ (posterior 0.925; at $k = 9$ it is 0.899). (6) Check: $k = 3$ gives $\frac{147}{272} \approx 0.540$, lesson 13's answer.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the odds form (line 1). 2: multiplying ratios with the independence reason (line 2). 2: the posterior (line 3). 2: solving for $k$ (line 4). 1: the numbers (line 5). 1: the check (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Ann and Bob draw cards in turn from a shuffled deck without replacement, Ann first; the first to draw an ace wins. Find Ann's chance exactly.
  *Answer.* The first ace is at position $k$ with probability $\frac{\binom{52 - k}3}{\binom{52}4}$ (the other three aces lie after it). Ann wins when $k$ is odd: $\frac{\sum_{k \text{ odd}}\binom{52 - k}3}{\binom{52}4} = \frac{140{,}725}{270{,}725} = \frac{433}{833} \approx 0.5198$. Decision point: the with-replacement formula $\frac1{2 - p}$ with $p = \frac1{13}$ gives $\frac{13}{25} = 0.52$, close only because the deck is large; with 4 aces in 8 cards the exact answer is $\frac{23}{35}$ and the formula's $\frac23$ is visibly wrong.

**PZ4 States, chains and games** (`set.puzzles.states`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: $k$ equal faces in a row. Code drill `drl.iv.first-step` (d4): `expected_steps(transitions, start, targets)` solves first-step equations exactly. Hidden tests: three equal faces in a row gives 43; the octahedron gives 6; deuce with $p = \frac35$ gives $\frac9{13}$ and $\frac{50}{13}$ points; the race "66 against 16" gives $\frac5{12}$ for 66. Known-wrong: resetting to the start state after every miss gives 216 for the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* A fair $m$-sided die is rolled until the same face comes up $k$ times in a row. Find the expected number of rolls.
  *Model answer.* (1) States: the current run length $j = 1, \dots, k - 1$ after the first roll. (2) From run length $j$ the next roll extends the run with chance $\frac1m$ and resets it to 1 otherwise, so $E_j = 1 + \frac1mE_{j+1} + \frac{m - 1}mE_1$, with $E_k = 0$. (3) Every equation has the same form, so by induction downwards from $E_{k-1} = 1 + \frac{m - 1}mE_1$, $E_{k-i} = \left(1 + \frac1m + \dots + \frac1{m^{i-1}}\right)\left(1 + \frac{m - 1}mE_1\right)$. Setting $i = k - 1$ and solving the resulting linear equation for $E_1$ gives $E_1 = \frac{m^k - m}{m - 1}$. (4) Add the first roll: $1 + E_1 = \frac{m^k - 1}{m - 1} = 1 + m + \dots + m^{k-1}$. (5) Contrast with one specific face, where the overlap rule (lesson 20) gives $m + m^2 + \dots + m^k$, 258 for $m = 6$ and $k = 3$: allowing any face shortens the wait by a factor of $m$. (6) $m = 6$, $k = 3$: $1 + 6 + 36 = 43$; $m = 2$, $k = 2$: 3 (two equal coin tosses in a row).
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the states (line 1). 2: the equations (line 2). 3: the solution (line 3). 1: the first roll (line 4). 1: the cross-check (line 5). 1: the numbers (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Points are won independently with chance 0.6. Find the chance of winning a tennis game (first to 4 points, but at 3-3 you must lead by 2).
  *Answer.* Win without deuce: 4-0, 4-1, 4-2 with chances $p^4$, $4p^4q$ and $10p^4q^2$. Reach 3-3 with chance $\binom63p^3q^3 = 20p^3q^3$, then win from deuce with $\frac{p^2}{p^2 + q^2}$. Total $p^4(1 + 4q + 10q^2) + 20p^3q^3\frac{p^2}{p^2 + q^2} = \frac{29{,}889}{40{,}625} \approx 0.7357$. Decision point: counting "4-2" as $\binom64$ arrangements is wrong because the last point must be the winner's: $\binom52 = 10$.

**PZ5 Continuous and geometric problems** (`set.puzzles.continuous`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the correlation bound. Code drill `drl.iv.geometric-mc` (d3): `estimate(region, n, seed)` returns a Monte Carlo estimate with standard error. Hidden tests: the square-centre region within 4 standard errors of 0.218951; the "largest exceeds the sum of the others" region within 4 standard errors of 0.5; the disc's mean distance within 4 standard errors of $\frac23$. Known-wrong: sampling the disc by uniform radius fails the third test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Given $\mathrm{corr}(X, Y) = \rho_1$ and $\mathrm{corr}(Y, Z) = \rho_2$, show that $\mathrm{corr}(X, Z) \ge \rho_1\rho_2 - \sqrt{(1 - \rho_1^2)(1 - \rho_2^2)}$, and evaluate for $\rho_1 = \rho_2 = 0.8$.
  *Model answer.* (1) The correlation matrix with entries $1$, $\rho_1$, $\rho_2$ and unknown $\rho = \mathrm{corr}(X, Z)$ must be positive semidefinite, since it is the covariance matrix of the standardised variables. (2) Its determinant must be non-negative: $1 + 2\rho_1\rho_2\rho - \rho_1^2 - \rho_2^2 - \rho^2 \ge 0$. (3) This is a quadratic inequality in $\rho$: $\rho^2 - 2\rho_1\rho_2\rho + \rho_1^2 + \rho_2^2 - 1 \le 0$, with roots $\rho_1\rho_2 \pm\sqrt{\rho_1^2\rho_2^2 - \rho_1^2 - \rho_2^2 + 1} = \rho_1\rho_2 \pm\sqrt{(1 - \rho_1^2)(1 - \rho_2^2)}$. (4) So $\rho$ lies between the roots; the $2 \times 2$ minors are already non-negative, so these are the only constraints. (5) With 0.8 and 0.8: $0.64 - 0.36 = 0.28$, and the upper end is 1. (6) Geometric reading: correlations are cosines of angles between vectors; angles of $\arccos 0.8 \approx 36.9°$ each can add to at most $73.7°$, whose cosine is 0.28.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: positive semidefiniteness and why (line 1). 2: the determinant (line 2). 3: solving the quadratic (line 3). 1: sufficiency of the conditions (line 4). 1: the number (line 5). 1: the angle reading (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A point is uniform in a square. Find the chance it is closer to the centre than to the nearest edge.
  *Answer.* Centre the square at the origin with side 1 and use the eighth $0 \le y \le x \le \frac12$, where the nearest edge is $x = \frac12$. The condition $\sqrt{x^2 + y^2} < \frac12 - x$ simplifies to $x < \frac14 - y^2$. Together with $y \le x$ this needs $y \le \frac14 - y^2$, that is $y \le y_0 = \frac{\sqrt2 - 1}2$. The eighth's area is $\int_0^{y_0}\left(\frac14 - y^2 - y\right)dy$; multiplying by 8 gives $\frac{4\sqrt2 - 5}3 \approx 0.2190$. Decision point: in the full square the boundary is four parabolic arcs, which is hard to integrate; the eighth-symmetry turns it into one integral of a polynomial.

**PZ6 Stochastic processes at interview depth** (`set.puzzles.stochastic`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: exit with drift. Code drill `drl.iv.bm-sim` (d3): standard-library Brownian simulation (`zqtest`'s seeded normals) for hitting and maximum probabilities. Hidden tests: $P(\max_{t \le 1}W \ge 0.5)$ within 4 standard errors of 0.617 (with the bridge correction); $E[W_2^4]$ within 4 standard errors of 12; the drift exit probability within 4 standard errors of 0.867. Known-wrong: discrete monitoring without the correction underestimates the maximum probability beyond the tolerance.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* $X_t = t + W_t$ starts at 0. Find the chance it hits 2 before $-1$ and the expected time to exit, explaining each martingale used.
  *Model answer.* (1) Look for $\lambda$ with $e^{\lambda X_t}$ a martingale: Itô gives the $dt$ coefficient $\lambda + \frac12\lambda^2$, zero at $\lambda = -2$. So $e^{-2X_t}$ is a martingale. (2) It is bounded before the exit time $\tau$, and $\tau$ is finite, so $1 = E[e^{-2X_\tau}] = Pe^{-4} + (1 - P)e^{2}$. (3) $P = \frac{e^2 - 1}{e^2 - e^{-4}} \approx 0.867$. (4) $X_t - t = W_t$ is a martingale with $E[\tau] < \infty$, so $E[X_\tau] = E[\tau]$. (5) $E[\tau] = 2P - 1 \times (1 - P) = 3P - 1 \approx 1.60$. (6) Checks: $P$ exceeds the driftless $\frac13$ because of the upward drift, and $E[\tau]$ is below the driftless $2 \times 1 = 2$.
  *Rubric (10, the mock rubric).* 2: finding the exponential martingale (line 1). 2: optional stopping with its justification (line 2). 1: $P$ (line 3). 2: the second martingale (line 4). 1: $E[\tau]$ (line 5). 2: the checks, said as they would be aloud (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Find $P(W_1 > 0, W_2 > 0)$.
  *Answer.* $(W_1, W_2)$ is bivariate normal with variances 1 and 2 and covariance 1, so $\mathrm{corr} = \frac1{\sqrt2}$. The orthant formula (lesson 28) gives $\frac14 + \frac{\arcsin(1/\sqrt2)}{2\pi} = \frac14 + \frac{\pi/4}{2\pi} = \frac38$. Decision point: rewriting $W_2 = W_1 + (W_2 - W_1)$ with independent pieces gives the same answer by integrating over $W_1$, but the orthant formula takes one line once the correlation is found.

**PZ7 Solving cold** (`set.puzzles.cold`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5), all under cold rules. Long-form: a follow-up variance. Code drill `drl.iv.check-two-ways` (d3): `exact(...)` and `simulate(...)` for the expected number of distinct suits in $k$ cards. Hidden tests: $k = 3$ gives $\frac{997}{425}$; $k = 5$ gives $\frac{7411}{2380}$; the simulation for $k = 5$ with seed 3 lies within 4 standard errors of the exact value; $k = 52$ gives 4. Known-wrong: treating the cards as dealt with replacement ($4(1 - (\frac34)^k)$) fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Six balls are dropped independently and uniformly into six boxes. Find the variance of the number of empty boxes, and explain why the binomial formula fails.
  *Model answer.* (1) $X = \sum_{i=1}^6I_i$ with $I_i = 1$ when box $i$ is empty; $P(I_i = 1) = \left(\frac56\right)^6$, so $E[X] = 6\left(\frac56\right)^6 \approx 2.009$. (2) $E[X^2] = E[X] + \sum_{i \ne j}P(I_i = I_j = 1) = E[X] + 30\left(\frac46\right)^6$. (3) $\mathrm{Var}(X) = E[X] + 30\left(\frac46\right)^6 - E[X]^2 \approx 2.009 + 2.634 - 4.037 \approx 0.606$ (exactly $\frac{36{,}611{,}855}{60{,}466{,}176}$). (4) The binomial formula $6p(1 - p) \approx 1.336$ assumes independent indicators; here $P(I_i = I_j = 1) = \left(\frac46\right)^6 \approx 0.0878$ is less than $p^2 \approx 0.112$: empty boxes are negatively correlated (if one is empty, the balls crowd the others). (5) Check by brute force over the $6^6 = 46{,}656$ placements: the same fraction.
  *Rubric (10, the mock rubric).* 2: the indicator set-up and the mean (line 1). 3: the second moment with pairs (line 2). 2: the variance (line 3). 2: the explanation of the negative correlation (line 4). 1: the check (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* Five cards are dealt. Expected number of different suits among them?
  *Answer.* By indicators on suits: $4\left(1 - \frac{\binom{39}5}{\binom{52}5}\right) = 4\left(1 - \frac{575{,}757}{2{,}598{,}960}\right) = \frac{7{,}411}{2{,}380} \approx 3.114$. Check: it lies between the 3-card answer, 2.35 (more cards never lose a suit), and 4. Decision point: the full distribution of the number of suits needs inclusion-exclusion over four cases; the expectation needs one complement.

**QM1 Speed formats** (`set.mental-pressure.formats`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the guessing rule. Timed deck item (in the drill's place): a 40-item Zetamac-format deck in 120 seconds, target 18 correct at the start of 3.3 and rising with the learner's median. 

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Under $+1$ for a right answer and $-1$ for a wrong one, derive the rule for answering an item when you can rule out some of its $k$ options, and say how it changes if skipping is not allowed.
  *Model answer.* (1) If $j$ options remain and you guess uniformly among them, the chance of being right is $\frac1j$. (2) Expected score of answering: $\frac1j - \left(1 - \frac1j\right) = \frac2j - 1$. (3) Answer when this is positive, that is when $j = 1$; at $j = 2$ the value is 0; for $j \ge 3$ it is negative (a blind guess among 5 is worth $-0.6$). (4) If you have a real lean, so that $P(\text{right}) = q$, answer when $2q - 1 > 0$, that is $q > \frac12$. (5) If skipping is allowed, a skip scores 0 and saves time, so skip at $j \ge 2$ unless time is plentiful. (6) If skipping is not allowed, every item is answered; the decision becomes how long to spend, because time spent raising $q$ on one item is taken from later items (lesson 52).
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the chance per guess (line 1). 2: the expected score (line 2). 2: the threshold (line 3). 2: the general rule with a lean (line 4). 1: skipping (line 5). 1: the no-skip variant (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A timed deck of 10 items in 60 seconds, no calculator: $37 \times 43$; $0.45 \times 0.8$; $\frac7{16}$; 1.2% of 4,500; $125 \times 64$; $999 \times 7$; $84 \div 0.28$; $17^2$; $\frac67$ to three decimal places; $2{,}016 \div 12$.
  *Answer.* 1,591 ($40^2 - 3^2$); 0.36; 0.4375; 54; 8,000; 6,993; 300; 289; 0.857; 168. Pass mark 9 of 10 within the time. Decision point: four of the ten have a structural shortcut (difference of squares, $125 \times 8 = 1{,}000$, $1{,}000 - 1$, dividing by a decimal by scaling both numbers); seeing them in under a second is the skill the deck trains.

**QM2 Estimation and quick quant numbers** (`set.mental-pressure.estimation`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: a Fermi estimate written up. Timed deck item: 20 estimation items in 200 seconds, each within 5%.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Estimate the number of commercial flights that take off worldwide on a typical day, writing the decomposition, the range for each factor and the combined range, then compare with the sourced figure in the fact bank [check the bank's figure and date].
  *Model answer.* (1) Decompose: number of commercial aircraft in service times average flights per aircraft per day. (2) Aircraft: between 20,000 and 30,000 (narrow-bodies dominate) [recalled order of magnitude]; take the geometric mean, about 24,000. (3) Flights per aircraft per day: short-haul aircraft fly 4 to 6 legs, long-haul 1 to 2; a blended 3 to 5, say 4. (4) Product: about 100,000 a day, with a range of roughly 60,000 to 150,000 from the factor ranges. (5) Cross-check from passengers: about 4 to 5 billion passenger journeys a year [recalled] at about 120 passengers a flight gives 35 to 40 million flights a year, about 100,000 a day. (6) Compare with the bank's figure and say which factor was furthest off.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: a decomposition into boundable factors (line 1). 2: a range for each factor with reasons (lines 2 and 3). 2: combining by multiplication with the range (line 4). 2: an independent cross-check (line 5). 2: the comparison and the honest error analysis (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Without a calculator: $2^{64}$ to two significant figures; $e^{-0.05}$ to three decimal places; $\sqrt{252}$ to two decimal places.
  *Answer.* $\log_{10}2^{64} = 64 \times 0.30103 = 19.266$, and $10^{0.266} \approx 1.85$, so $2^{64} \approx 1.8 \times 10^{19}$ (exactly 18,446,744,073,709,551,616). $e^{-0.05} \approx 1 - 0.05 + 0.00125 = 0.951$. $\sqrt{252}$: $16^2 = 256$, one Newton step $16 - \frac4{32} = 15.875$; a Newton step for a square root always overshoots slightly, so the root is just below 15.875: 15.87. Decision point: for $10^{0.266}$ use $\log_{10}2 = 0.301$ and $\log_{10}1.8 = 0.255$; interpolating between these anchors gives 1.85 in seconds.

**QM3 Test strategy** (`set.mental-pressure.test-day`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: choosing a pace. Timed deck item: one full 80-in-8 sitting in the configured format, scored as net.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For a test of $M$ items in $T$ seconds marked $+1$ and $-1$, with accuracy $a(t)$ when spending $t$ seconds an item, derive the net score as a function of $t$ and explain why the optimum is often exactly at $t = \frac TM$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Items answered: $\min\left(M, \frac Tt\right)$. (2) Net per item: $a(t) - (1 - a(t)) = 2a(t) - 1$. (3) Net score $n(t) = \min\left(M, \frac Tt\right)(2a(t) - 1)$. (4) For $t < \frac TM$ the item count is capped at $M$ and $n(t) = M(2a(t) - 1)$ rises with $t$, because accuracy rises with time. (5) For $t > \frac TM$, $n(t) = \frac Tt(2a(t) - 1)$, which falls once accuracy saturates. (6) So when accuracy is still rising at $t = \frac TM$, the best pace is exactly the one that finishes the test: never rush to finish early. (7) For 80 items in 480 seconds, the answer is 6 seconds an item.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: items answered (line 1). 1: net per item (line 2). 2: the net function (line 3). 2: the capped region (line 4). 2: the uncapped region (line 5). 1: the conclusion (line 6). 1: the number (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* With $a(t) = 1 - \frac12e^{-(t - 2)}$ for $t \ge 2$ seconds, 80 items and 480 seconds, find the best $t$ and the net score, and compare $t = 5$ and $t = 7$.
  *Answer.* At $t = 6$ (the cap), $a = 1 - \frac12e^{-4} \approx 0.9908$ and the net is $80 \times 0.9817 \approx 78.5$. At $t = 5$: $80 \times (2(1 - \frac12e^{-3}) - 1) \approx 76.0$; at $t = 7$: $\frac{480}7 \times 0.9933 \approx 68.1$. The optimum is $t = 6$. Decision point: going slower than the cap costs far more than going faster, because unanswered items score nothing while fast answers still score most of a point.

**QM4 Logic, invariants and constructions** (`set.mental-pressure.logic`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: an invariant proof. Code drill `drl.iv.reachable` (d3): `reachable(start, moves)` searches small puzzle state spaces exhaustively. Hidden tests: the board game from 1 to 10 reaches exactly $\{1, 3, 5, 7, 9\}$; the take-away game's losing positions below 30 are the multiples of 5; the 7 and 11 timers reach 15 minutes. Known-wrong: a search that stops at the first solution reports $\{1\}$ and fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove that in lesson 56's board game the last number is odd, and that it cannot be 11.
  *Model answer.* (1) A move replaces $a$ and $b$ by $\lvert a - b\rvert$. (2) $a + b$ and $\lvert a - b\rvert$ differ by $2\min(a, b)$, an even number, so the parity of the board's sum never changes. (3) The starting sum is 55, odd, so after nine moves the single number left is odd. (4) Every number on the board is always between 0 and 10: $\lvert a - b\rvert \le \max(a, b) \le 10$. So 11 is impossible. (5) Which odd numbers occur is a separate question, settled by exhibiting move orders (all of 1, 3, 5, 7 and 9 occur).
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the move (line 1). 4: the invariant with its reason (line 2), the creative step. 2: the conclusion (line 3). 1: the bound (line 4). 1: separating reachability from the invariant (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* Measure exactly 9 minutes with a 4-minute and a 7-minute sand timer.
  *Answer.* Start both. At 4, turn the 4-minute timer. At 7 the 7-minute timer empties; turn it (the 4-minute timer has 1 minute left). At 8 the 4-minute timer empties; the 7-minute timer has run 1 minute since it was turned, so turn it again: 1 minute of sand runs back, ending at 9. Decision point: 9 is $4 + 4 + 1$, and the 1 minute is manufactured from the gap between 7 and 8; looking only for whole runs of 4 and 7 cannot find it.

**QM5 Number puzzles** (`set.mental-pressure.numbers`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: repeated-block divisibility. Code drill `drl.iv.mod-cycles` (d3): `last_digits(base, exp, k)` by cycles, without `pow`'s third argument. Hidden tests: $7^{2026}$ ends in 9; $3^{2026}$ ends in 29; $2^{100}$ ends in 76; the cycle length of 3 modulo 100 is 20. Known-wrong: using the exponent modulo 10 fails the second test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Show that every number of the form $abcabc$ is divisible by 7, 11 and 13, and find the corresponding statement for $abcdabcd$.
  *Model answer.* (1) $abcabc = abc \times 1000 + abc = abc \times 1001$. (2) $1001 = 7 \times 11 \times 13$, so the three primes divide it, whatever the digits. (3) Similarly $abcdabcd = abcd \times 10{,}001$. (4) $10{,}001 = 73 \times 137$ (try primes up to $\sqrt{10{,}001} \approx 100$; 73 works). (5) So every $abcdabcd$ is divisible by 73 and 137. (6) In general a block repeated twice is divisible by $10^k + 1$, whose factors depend on $k$.
  *Rubric (10).* 3: the factorisation by 1001 (line 1). 2: $1001 = 7 \cdot 11 \cdot 13$ (line 2). 2: the four-digit case (line 3). 2: factoring 10,001 (line 4). 1: the generalisation (lines 5 and 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Find the last two digits of $3^{2026}$.
  *Answer.* Modulo 4, $3^{2026} \equiv (-1)^{2026} = 1$. Modulo 25, $3^{20} \equiv 1$ (Euler's theorem, $\varphi(25) = 20$), and $2026 \equiv 6 \pmod{20}$, so $3^{2026} \equiv 3^6 = 729 \equiv 4 \pmod{25}$. The number below 100 that is 1 mod 4 and 4 mod 25 is 29. Check: $3^6 = 729$ ends in 29, and $3^{20} \equiv 1 \pmod{100}$ directly. Decision point: splitting 100 into 4 and 25 (the Chinese remainder theorem) avoids computing the cycle of 3 modulo 100 by hand.

**QM6 Calculus and linear algebra at interview speed** (`set.mental-pressure.calc-linalg`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the equicorrelation bound. Code drill `drl.iv.psd-check` (d3): `is_correlation(matrix)` by an exact $LDL^\top$ factorisation (Cholesky without square roots, which are not rational) in `Fraction`s; a zero pivot passes only if the rest of its column is zero, so semidefinite boundary cases are accepted. Hidden tests: lesson 72's matrix is rejected; the $4 \times 4$ matrix with off-diagonals $\frac12$ is accepted; equicorrelation $-\frac14$ in 5 dimensions is accepted (on the boundary) and $-\frac13$ rejected; a matrix with an entry above 1 is rejected. Known-wrong: checking only that entries lie in $[-1, 1]$ fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Show that $n$ random variables can have all pairwise correlations equal to $\rho$ exactly when $-\frac1{n - 1} \le \rho \le 1$.
  *Model answer.* (1) The correlation matrix is $(1 - \rho)I + \rho J$, with $J$ the all-ones matrix. (2) $J$ has eigenvalue $n$ (eigenvector $\mathbf 1$) and 0 with multiplicity $n - 1$. (3) So the matrix has eigenvalues $1 + (n - 1)\rho$ once and $1 - \rho$ with multiplicity $n - 1$. (4) Positive semidefinite exactly when both are non-negative: $\rho \ge -\frac1{n - 1}$ and $\rho \le 1$. (5) Sufficiency: any positive semidefinite matrix with unit diagonal is the correlation matrix of some normal vector. (6) Intuition: $\mathrm{Var}(X_1 + \dots + X_n) = n(1 + (n - 1)\rho) \ge 0$ already gives the lower bound.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the matrix form (line 1). 2: $J$'s eigenvalues (line 2). 2: the matrix's eigenvalues (line 3). 2: the inequalities (line 4). 1: sufficiency (line 5). 1: the variance check (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* $\mathrm{corr}(X, Y) = 0.6$ and $\mathrm{corr}(Y, Z) = 0.8$. Find every possible value of $\mathrm{corr}(X, Z)$.
  *Answer.* By homework PZ5's bound, $\rho$ lies in $\left[0.48 - \sqrt{0.64 \times 0.36}, 0.48 + \sqrt{0.64 \times 0.36}\right] = [0.48 - 0.48, 0.48 + 0.48] = [0, 0.96]$. Both ends give a singular matrix. Decision point: the lower end is exactly 0, so $X$ and $Z$ can be uncorrelated although both are strongly correlated with $Y$: the angles $\arccos 0.6 \approx 53.1°$ and $\arccos 0.8 \approx 36.9°$ add to $90°$.

**MG1 Pricing a bet** (`set.mm-games.pricing-bets`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: replicating a contingent bet. Code drill `drl.iv.replicate-bets` (d4): `stakes(target, n)` returns the even-money stake at every node of an $n$-toss tree for any target payoff on the number of heads. Hidden tests: lesson 77's target gives a first stake of 40; after one head and one tail the stake is 80; the value at the root is 0 for a target with zero mean; a target that pays the number of heads gives a stake of $\frac12$ at every node. Known-wrong: staking the target's full size at every node fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Even-money bets of any size are offered on each of three fair coin tosses, placed before each toss. Construct the betting strategy that pays $+$£80 if at least two of the three tosses are heads and $-$£80 otherwise, and explain why it needs no view on the coin.
  *Model answer.* (1) Work backwards from the target. After two tosses: HH has already won ($+80$ whatever happens), TT has already lost ($-80$); after HT or TH the last toss decides, so the value is the average, 0, and the stake on the last toss is $\frac{80 - (-80)}2 = 80$ on heads. (2) After one toss: from H the next states are HH ($+80$) and HT (0): value 40, stake $\frac{80 - 0}2 = 40$ on heads. From T: TH (0) and TT ($-80$): value $-40$, stake 40 on heads. (3) At the start: H leads to 40 and T to $-40$: value 0, stake 40 on heads. (4) Check every path: HHH: $+40 + 40 + 0 = +80$; HTH: $+40 - 40 + 80 = +80$; HTT: $+40 - 40 - 80 = -80$; TTT: $-40 - 40 + 0 = -80$. (5) The construction uses only the payoff and the even-money terms: the stakes replicate the target exactly, as a delta hedge replicates an option (1.7 lesson 67); the start value 0 is the fair price at even money. (6) The stake is largest (80) when the outcome is most in doubt, the betting form of gamma.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the values after two tosses (line 1). 2: after one toss (line 2). 1: the start (line 3). 2: the path check (line 4). 2: the replication argument (line 5). 1: the gamma remark (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* You backed the home team at decimal odds 4.0 for £100. Now the odds are home 2.0, draw 3.5, away 5.0. What stakes on the draw and on the away win make your profit the same in every outcome, and what is it?
  *Answer.* With $x$ on the draw and $y$ on the away win, profits are: home $300 - x - y$; draw $2.5x - y - 100$; away $4y - x - 100$. Equal profits give $400 = 3.5x$ and $400 = 5y$: $x \approx$ £114.29 and $y =$ £80, locking in £105.71 whatever happens. Decision point: the new home odds of 2.0 do not enter at all; only the prices of the outcomes you bet on matter, because your existing home bet already covers the home outcome.

**MG2 Sizing** (`set.mm-games.sizing`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: continuous Kelly from Itô's formula. Code drill `drl.iv.kelly` (d3): `kelly(outcomes)` maximises expected log wealth over stakes by golden-section search (standard library). Hidden tests: $p = 0.4$ at 2 to 1 gives 0.1; two simultaneous even-money bets at 0.6 give 0.1923 each; a bet with negative edge gives 0; the growth per bet at twice Kelly for $p = 0.55$ even money is $-1.377 \times 10^{-4}$ within $10^{-6}$ (just below zero). Known-wrong: maximising expected wealth returns the largest allowed stake and fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* A share follows $dS = \mu S\,dt + \sigma S\,dW$ and cash earns $r$. You keep a constant fraction $f$ of wealth in the share. Derive the growth rate of wealth and the fraction that maximises it.
  *Model answer.* (1) Wealth: $\frac{dX}X = f\frac{dS}S + (1 - f)r\,dt = (r + f(\mu - r))\,dt + f\sigma\,dW$. (2) Itô for $\ln X$: $d\ln X = \left(r + f(\mu - r) - \frac12f^2\sigma^2\right)dt + f\sigma\,dW$. (3) The long-run growth rate is the drift of $\ln X$: $g(f) = r + f(\mu - r) - \frac12f^2\sigma^2$. (4) Maximise: $g'(f) = \mu - r - f\sigma^2 = 0$ gives $f^* = \frac{\mu - r}{\sigma^2}$. (5) At $f^*$, $g = r + \frac{(\mu - r)^2}{2\sigma^2} = r + \frac12S^2$ with $S$ the Sharpe ratio. (6) At $2f^*$, $g = r$: twice Kelly earns only the risk-free rate, the continuous form of lesson 80.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the wealth equation (line 1). 3: Itô for the log, with the $-\frac12f^2\sigma^2$ term (line 2), the step that makes the optimum interior. 1: growth as the log drift (line 3). 2: the optimum (line 4). 1: growth at the optimum (line 5). 1: twice Kelly (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Two assets with excess returns 6% and 4%, volatilities 20% and 15%, correlation 0.5. Find the Kelly fractions and the extra growth, and compare with sizing each asset alone.
  *Answer.* $f^* = \Sigma^{-1}(\mu - r)$ with $\Sigma = \begin{pmatrix}0.04 & 0.015\\0.015 & 0.0225\end{pmatrix}$: $\det\Sigma = 0.000675$, so $f_1 = \frac{0.0225 \times 0.06 - 0.015 \times 0.04}{0.000675} \approx 1.11$ and $f_2 = \frac{-0.015 \times 0.06 + 0.04 \times 0.04}{0.000675} \approx 1.04$. The extra growth is $\frac12(\mu - r)^\top\Sigma^{-1}(\mu - r) \approx 5.4\%$ a year. Sized alone the fractions would be $\frac{0.06}{0.04} = 1.5$ and $\frac{0.04}{0.0225} \approx 1.78$, a total of 3.28 times wealth, about 50% more than the joint 2.15, because the positive correlation makes the two bets partly the same bet. Decision point: adding single-asset Kelly fractions over-bets correlated assets (`sum-individual-kellys`).

**MG3 Making markets** (`set.mm-games.making-markets`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the posterior after a trade. Code drill `drl.iv.posterior` (d3): `after_trade(values, prior, quote, side, informed)` returns the posterior mean by Bayes. Hidden tests: lesson 86's buy at 4 gives 4.3; with `informed = 0` the mean is unchanged; with `informed = 1` the posterior after a buy at 4 is uniform on 5 and 6 (mean 5.5); a sell at 3 with `informed = 0.5` gives 2.7 by symmetry. Known-wrong: ignoring the noise traders (treating every buyer as informed) fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* A hidden value $V$ has prior $\pi$ on a finite set. A counterparty is informed with chance $\mu$ (buys at your offer $a$ exactly when $V > a$) and otherwise buys with chance $\frac12$. Derive the posterior after a buy at $a$, and evaluate for one die with $a = 4$ and $\mu = \frac12$.
  *Model answer.* (1) $P(\text{buy} \mid V = v) = \mu\mathbb 1\{v > a\} + (1 - \mu)\frac12$. (2) Bayes: $P(V = v \mid \text{buy}) \propto \pi(v)\left[\mu\mathbb 1\{v > a\} + \frac{1 - \mu}2\right]$. (3) For the die, $v \in \{1, \dots, 6\}$, weights $\frac14$ for $v \le 4$ and $\frac34$ for $v \in \{5, 6\}$, total 2.5. (4) Posterior mean $\frac{0.25 \times 10 + 0.75 \times 11}{2.5} = \frac{10.75}{2.5} = 4.3$. (5) Re-quote around 4.3, wider than before if the informed share is high. (6) Checks: $\mu = 0$ leaves 3.5; $\mu = 1$ gives 5.5.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the likelihood of a buy (line 1). 2: Bayes (line 2). 2: the weights (line 3). 2: the mean (line 4). 1: the re-quote (line 5). 1: the checks (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* In lesson 88's three-card market, the first card is a king and the second a 2. What are the expected total and its standard deviation now, and what market would you make?
  *Answer.* The last card is uniform on the 50 cards left, whose total is $364 - 13 - 2 = 349$: mean 6.98, so the expected total is $15 + 6.98 = 21.98$. The last card's standard deviation over those 50 cards is about 3.65. A market such as 21 at 23 is centred on the mean and narrower than one standard deviation, which is reasonable because the remaining uncertainty is symmetric and nobody at the table knows the last card. Decision point: the second reveal undoes most of the first: a low card brings the total back near the original 21, and a market left at 26 to 27 is stale in the other direction.

**MG4 Trading games** (`set.mm-games.trading-games`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: Figgie inference. Code drill `drl.iv.figgie` (d4): `goal_posterior(hand)` over the twelve deck compositions. Hidden tests: lesson 92's hand gives clubs $\frac{42{,}075}{110{,}794}$; a hand with no cards of a suit still gives that suit a positive chance of being the 12-card suit; the four probabilities sum to 1; a hand of 10 spades makes spades the 12-card suit with chance $\frac{33}{34} \approx 0.971$. Known-wrong: returning the suit with the most cards as the goal fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Explain how to infer the likely goal suit in Figgie from a 10-card hand, and compute it for 4 spades, 3 clubs, 2 hearts, 1 diamond.
  *Model answer.* (1) Rules [searched]: 40 cards; one suit has 12, two have 10, one has 8; the goal suit is the suit of the same colour as the 12-card suit, so it has 10 or 8 cards. (2) The 12 compositions (which suit has 12, which has 8) are equally likely a priori. (3) Given a composition with suit sizes $n_s$, the hand's chance is $\frac{\prod_s\binom{n_s}{h_s}}{\binom{40}{10}}$ (multivariate hypergeometric). (4) The posterior on compositions is proportional to $\prod_s\binom{n_s}{h_s}$; sum over compositions with a given 12-card suit. (5) For this hand: 12-card suit spades 38.0%, clubs 27.5%, hearts 20.0%, diamonds 14.6%. (6) The goal is the partner of the 12-card suit: clubs 38.0%, spades 27.5%, diamonds 20.0%, hearts 14.6%. (7) Trade: buy clubs, which you hold three of and which are most likely the goal; spades, your longest suit, are only the second most likely goal.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the rules, including the goal's link to the 12-card suit (line 1). 1: the prior (line 2). 2: the hypergeometric likelihood (line 3). 2: the posterior (line 4). 1: the numbers (line 5). 1: mapping to the goal (line 6). 1: the trade (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Markets: $X$ 10 at 11; $Y$ 4 at 5; $X - Y$ 7.5 at 8.5; $X + Y$ 13.5 at 14.5. Find every arbitrage and its profit per unit.
  *Answer.* Check each product against every way of building it from the others, always buying at offers and selling at bids. $X + Y$ from its parts: buying $X$ and $Y$ costs 16 and selling them raises 14, while the sum trades 13.5 at 14.5: no arbitrage. $X - Y$ from its parts: buying $X$ and selling $Y$ costs $11 - 4 = 7$, and $X - Y$ can be sold at 7.5: sell $X - Y$, buy $X$, sell $Y$, for a riskless 0.5. (The reverse, buying $X - Y$ at 8.5 against selling $X$ at 10 and buying $Y$ at 5, loses.) $Y$ from the two combinations: buying $X + Y$ at 14.5 and selling $X - Y$ at 7.5 makes $2Y$ for 7, that is 3.5 a unit, below $Y$'s bid of 4: do it and sell two $Y$ at 4, for a riskless 1.0 (0.5 per $Y$). The same pair cannot make $X$ cheaply: $2X$ costs $14.5 + 8.5 = 23$ (11.5 a unit) against $X$'s bid of 10, and sells for $13.5 + 7.5 = 21$ (10.5 a unit) against its offer of 11. So two arbitrages; a linear programme over all buy and sell quantities, normalised to one unit of gross trading, confirms these are the only two (every other arbitrage portfolio is a combination of them), and the second earns more per unit traded (0.25 against 0.17). Decision point: checking mid-prices ($X - Y$ mid 8 against $X$ mid 10.5 minus $Y$ mid 4.5, which is 6) shows a mispricing of 2 that is mostly spread; only bids against offers decide.

**MG5 Options at interview speed** (`set.mm-games.options-speed`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: no-arbitrage bounds and convexity. Code drill `drl.iv.quote-check` (d3): `arbitrages(strikes, calls, puts, S, r, T)` lists violated inequalities. Hidden tests: lesson 95's 14, 8.5, 2 gives the butterfly; 14, 8, 3 gives none; a put-call parity violation is found with the right direction; a call spread priced above its strike width discounted is flagged. Known-wrong: a check of monotonicity only fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove that call prices are convex in the strike, and that $0 \le C(K_1) - C(K_2) \le (K_2 - K_1)e^{-rT}$ for $K_1 < K_2$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Payoffs: $(S - K)^+$ is convex in $K$ for each $S$; a positive combination of convex functions is convex, and the price is a discounted expectation (a positive combination) of payoffs. (2) Without a model: for $K_1 < K_2 < K_3$ with $K_2 = \lambda K_1 + (1 - \lambda)K_3$, the portfolio long $\lambda$ calls at $K_1$, long $1 - \lambda$ at $K_3$, short one at $K_2$ has a non-negative payoff at every $S$ (check the four regions), so its price is non-negative: $C(K_2) \le \lambda C(K_1) + (1 - \lambda)C(K_3)$. (3) The call spread $C(K_1) - C(K_2)$ pays between 0 and $K_2 - K_1$, so its price lies between 0 and $(K_2 - K_1)e^{-rT}$. (4) A quote outside either bound gives a trade with a non-negative payoff and a negative cost.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: convexity of payoffs (line 1). 4: the model-free butterfly argument with the regions checked (line 2). 3: the call-spread bounds (line 3). 1: the arbitrage (line 4).
- *Example B (d5).* Share and strike 100, one year, zero rates, at-the-money volatility 20%, skew $\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial K} = -0.001$ per unit of strike. Price a digital call paying 1 if $S_T > 100$, with and without the skew.
  *Answer.* The digital is $-\frac{dC}{dK}$, and $C(K) = \mathrm{BS}(K, \sigma(K))$, so $-\frac{dC}{dK} = -\frac{\partial\mathrm{BS}}{\partial K} - \nu\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial K} = N(d_2) - \nu\sigma'(K)$. Here $d_2 = -0.1$, $N(d_2) \approx 0.4602$, and vega $\nu = S\varphi(d_1)\sqrt T = 100\varphi(0.1) \approx 39.70$. With the skew: $0.4602 + 39.70 \times 0.001 \approx 0.4999$. Decision point: with a negative skew, lower strikes have higher volatility, so the call price falls faster in strike than the flat model says, and the digital is worth more; pricing it at $N(d_2)$ undercharges by 4 points.

**TR1 Statistics questions** (`set.technical.statistics`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: omitted-variable bias. Code drill `drl.iv.ab-size` (d3): `n_per_arm(p1, p2, alpha, power)` with the pooled-variance formula and an inverse normal by bisection on `math.erf`. Hidden tests: 10% to 11% at 5% two-sided and 80% power gives 14,751 (rounded up); halving the effect roughly quadruples $n$; power 0.5 gives a smaller $n$ than 0.8. Known-wrong: omitting the power term gives about 7,200 and fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* The true model is $y = \beta_0 + \beta_1x_1 + \beta_2x_2 + \varepsilon$ with $E[\varepsilon \mid x_1, x_2] = 0$. Derive the probability limit of the slope from regressing $y$ on $x_1$ alone, and sign the bias in an example.
  *Model answer.* (1) The short regression's slope is $\hat b = \frac{\widehat{\mathrm{Cov}}(x_1, y)}{\widehat{\mathrm{Var}}(x_1)}$. (2) Substitute $y$: $\mathrm{Cov}(x_1, y) = \beta_1\mathrm{Var}(x_1) + \beta_2\mathrm{Cov}(x_1, x_2) + \mathrm{Cov}(x_1, \varepsilon)$, and the last term is 0. (3) So $\hat b \to \beta_1 + \beta_2\frac{\mathrm{Cov}(x_1, x_2)}{\mathrm{Var}(x_1)}$. (4) The bias is $\beta_2$ times the slope of $x_2$ on $x_1$: zero only if $x_2$ is irrelevant or uncorrelated with $x_1$. (5) Example: $\beta_1 = 1$, $\beta_2 = 2$, equal variances, correlation 0.5: slope $1 + 2 \times 0.5 = 2$. (6) Finance reading: a momentum signal's return regressed without the market factor absorbs the factor's effect when the two are correlated.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the slope formula (line 1). 3: the covariance expansion with the zero term justified (line 2). 2: the limit (line 3). 1: when the bias vanishes (line 4). 1: the example (line 5). 1: an application (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* An analyst duplicates every row of a regression dataset with $n = 50$ rows and $p = 2$ coefficients. By what exact factor do the reported standard errors change?
  *Answer.* The coefficients are unchanged. The residual sum of squares doubles and the degrees of freedom go from $n - p$ to $2n - p$, so $\hat\sigma^2$ changes by $\frac{2(n - p)}{2n - p}$; $X^\top X$ doubles, so $(X^\top X)^{-1}$ halves. The standard errors change by $\sqrt{\frac{2(n - p)}{2n - p} \cdot \frac12} = \sqrt{\frac{n - p}{2n - p}} = \sqrt{\frac{48}{98}} \approx 0.700$, slightly more than the $\frac1{\sqrt2} \approx 0.707$ usually quoted. Decision point: the quick answer, "divide by $\sqrt2$", is right only as $n \to \infty$; the exact factor shows where the degrees of freedom enter, and either way the smaller errors are fake.

**TR2 Machine-learning questions** (`set.technical.ml`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: ridge regression for an orthonormal design. Code drill `drl.iv.auc` (d3): `auc(scores, labels)` by pair counting, ties counted as one half. Hidden tests: lesson 112's six scores give $\frac79$; all positives above all negatives give 1; reversed labels give $1 - \text{AUC}$; all scores equal give $\frac12$. Known-wrong: ignoring ties fails the last test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For a design with $X^\top X = I$, derive the ridge estimate minimising $\frac12\lVert y - X\beta\rVert^2 + \frac\lambda2\lVert\beta\rVert^2$ and its bias and variance, and say when ridge beats least squares.
  *Model answer.* (1) Setting the gradient to zero: $-X^\top(y - X\beta) + \lambda\beta = 0$, so $\hat\beta_\lambda = (X^\top X + \lambda I)^{-1}X^\top y = \frac1{1 + \lambda}\hat\beta_{\text{OLS}}$. (2) With $y = X\beta + \varepsilon$ and $\mathrm{Var}(\varepsilon) = \sigma^2I$: $E[\hat\beta_\lambda] = \frac\beta{1 + \lambda}$, bias $-\frac{\lambda\beta}{1 + \lambda}$. (3) $\mathrm{Var}(\hat\beta_\lambda) = \frac{\sigma^2}{(1 + \lambda)^2}I$.
