(4) Mean squared error per coefficient: $\frac{\lambda^2\beta_j^2 + \sigma^2}{(1 + \lambda)^2}$, against $\sigma^2$ for least squares. (5) Its derivative in $\lambda$ at 0 is $-2\sigma^2 < 0$, so some ridge always beats least squares; the best is $\lambda = \frac{\sigma^2}{\beta_j^2}$ for one coefficient. (6) Interpretation: shrinkage trades a little bias for a lot of variance when signals are weak relative to noise, the usual case in return prediction.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the estimate (line 1). 2: the bias (line 2). 2: the variance (line 3). 2: the mean squared error and the derivative argument (lines 4 and 5). 2: the interpretation (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Bagged trees each have prediction variance 1 and pairwise correlation 0.3. How many trees are needed to get within 5% of the variance of an infinite ensemble?
  *Answer.* The average of $B$ trees has variance $\rho + \frac{1 - \rho}B = 0.3 + \frac{0.7}B$, tending to 0.3. Within 5%: $0.3 + \frac{0.7}B \le 0.315$, so $B \ge \frac{0.7}{0.015} \approx 46.7$: 47 trees. Decision point: past about 50 trees more trees buy almost nothing; only lowering $\rho$ (feature subsampling) lowers the floor of 0.3.

**TR3 ML-lab depth** (`set.technical.ml-lab`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: backpropagation for a two-layer network. Code drill `drl.iv.backprop` (d4): NumPy `grads(W1, b1, W2, b2, x, y)` for a relu network with squared loss, checked by finite differences. Hidden tests: lesson 113's scalar case gives 4 and $-2$; random seeded shapes agree with central differences to $10^{-6}$ relative; a dead unit has zero gradient in its incoming weights. Known-wrong: omitting the relu mask fails the second test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For $h = \mathrm{relu}(W_1x + b_1)$, $\hat y = W_2h + b_2$ and $L = \frac12\lVert\hat y - y\rVert^2$, derive $\frac{\partial L}{\partial W_2}$, $\frac{\partial L}{\partial b_2}$, $\frac{\partial L}{\partial W_1}$ and $\frac{\partial L}{\partial b_1}$.
  *Model answer.* (1) $\delta_2 = \frac{\partial L}{\partial\hat y} = \hat y - y$. (2) $\frac{\partial L}{\partial W_2} = \delta_2h^\top$ and $\frac{\partial L}{\partial b_2} = \delta_2$. (3) Back through the linear layer: $\frac{\partial L}{\partial h} = W_2^\top\delta_2$. (4) Through the relu, with $z = W_1x + b_1$: $\delta_1 = (W_2^\top\delta_2) \odot \mathbb 1\{z > 0\}$. (5) $\frac{\partial L}{\partial W_1} = \delta_1x^\top$ and $\frac{\partial L}{\partial b_1} = \delta_1$. (6) Shapes check: each gradient has its parameter's shape; the finite-difference check confirms the formulas.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the output error (line 1). 2: the second layer's gradients (line 2). 2: the transpose step (line 3). 2: the relu mask (line 4). 1: the first layer's gradients (line 5). 1: the shape check (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Gradient descent on $f(x) = \frac12(x_1^2 + 100x_2^2)$ with a fixed step size. Find the step that minimises the worst-case convergence factor, that factor, and the number of steps to reduce the error by $10^6$.
  *Answer.* Each coordinate contracts by $\lvert1 - \eta\lambda_i\rvert$ with $\lambda = 1, 100$. The worst factor is minimised when $1 - \eta = -(1 - 100\eta)$, that is $\eta = \frac2{101}$, giving $\frac{99}{101} \approx 0.9802$. Steps: $\frac{\ln 10^6}{\ln\frac{101}{99}} \approx 690.8$, so 691. Decision point: the condition number 100 sets the pace; a step tuned to the steep direction makes the flat one crawl, which is what momentum and Adam's per-coordinate scaling address.

**TR4 Coding rounds** (`set.technical.coding`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: reservoir sampling with $k$ items. Code drill `drl.iv.order-book` (d4): `Book` with `add(id, side, price, qty)`, `cancel(id)` and matching by price-time priority. Hidden tests: a crossing order fills against the best price first and then by time; a cancel removes the order and an empty level disappears; partial fills leave the right residual; 10,000 random operations agree with a brute-force reference book. Known-wrong: matching by arrival time across price levels fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove that reservoir sampling with a reservoir of $k$ (keep the first $k$ items; item $i > k$ replaces a uniformly chosen kept item with chance $\frac ki$) leaves every item of an $n$-item stream in the reservoir with chance $\frac kn$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Claim, by induction on $n \ge k$: after $n$ items each has chance $\frac kn$ of being kept. (2) Base $n = k$: all are kept. (3) Step: item $n + 1$ is kept with chance $\frac k{n + 1}$ by construction. (4) An earlier item, kept after $n$ items with chance $\frac kn$, survives step $n + 1$ unless the new item enters and evicts it: chance $\frac k{n + 1} \cdot \frac1k = \frac1{n + 1}$. (5) So it is kept with chance $\frac kn\left(1 - \frac1{n + 1}\right) = \frac kn \cdot \frac n{n + 1} = \frac k{n + 1}$. (6) Memory is $k$ items and one counter, and $n$ need not be known in advance.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the claim (line 1). 1: the base (line 2). 2: the new item (line 3). 3: survival of old items (line 4), the step most answers miss. 2: the algebra (line 5). 1: the memory remark (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* The naive shuffle swaps position $i$ with a uniformly random position in the whole array, for $i = 0, 1, 2, 3$. For 4 elements, show it is biased and find the most and least likely orders' counts out of the $4^4$ swap sequences.
  *Answer.* $4^4 = 256$ equally likely swap sequences map to $4! = 24$ orders; 256 is not a multiple of 24, so the orders cannot all be equally likely. Enumeration gives counts from 8 (two orders) to 15 (one order), against a uniform $\frac{256}{24} \approx 10.7$. Decision point: the counting argument proves bias without enumeration; the enumeration shows its size, a ratio of almost 2 between the extremes.

**TR5 C++ and systems questions** (`set.technical.cpp-systems`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: copy and move semantics. Code drill `drl.iv.cpp-ring-buffer` (d4, C++ on the learner's machine): a fixed-capacity single-producer single-consumer ring buffer with tests in doctest; returned by paste-verify of the test summary and a timing (`widgets-programming.md` 6.11). Hidden tests in the starter suite: push then pop returns items in order; a full buffer rejects a push; wrap-around preserves order; a two-thread test passes 1,000,000 items with a checksum. Known-wrong: a buffer that uses `size == capacity` without atomics fails the two-thread test under ThreadSanitizer.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Explain the rule of zero, three and five, what a move constructor does, and why a move constructor should be `noexcept`.
  *Model answer.* (1) Rule of three (C++98): a class that needs a user-defined destructor, copy constructor or copy assignment usually needs all three, because it owns a resource. (2) Rule of five (C++11): add the move constructor and move assignment; declaring any of the five suppresses some implicit ones. (3) Rule of zero: prefer members that manage themselves (`std::vector`, `std::unique_ptr`), so the class declares none of the five. (4) A move constructor steals the source's resource (pointer, buffer) and leaves the source valid but unspecified, typically empty: $O(1)$ instead of a deep copy. (5) `std::vector` reallocation moves elements only if the move constructor cannot throw (`std::move_if_noexcept`); otherwise it copies, to keep the strong exception guarantee. (6) So a throwing or unmarked move constructor silently turns every reallocation into copies.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the rule of three (line 1). 1: the rule of five (line 2). 2: the rule of zero (line 3). 2: what a move does (line 4). 3: `noexcept` and `move_if_noexcept` (lines 5 and 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Predict the output of this program, compiled with GCC 13 at `-O2` and `-std=c++20`, then predict it again with `noexcept` removed from the move constructor:
  ```cpp
  #include <cstdio>
  #include <utility>
  #include <vector>
  struct T { T() {} T(const T&) { std::puts("copy"); } T(T&&) noexcept { std::puts("move"); } };
  T make() { T t; return t; }
  int main() { std::vector<T> v; v.reserve(2); T a; v.push_back(a); v.push_back(std::move(a)); v.push_back(make()); }
  ```
  *Answer.* With `noexcept`: copy, move, move, move, move: the copy of `a`; the move of `a`; for the third push, the returned object is built in place by named return value optimisation, then the reallocation from capacity 2 constructs the new element by move and moves the two old elements. Without `noexcept`: copy, move, move, copy, copy: the reallocation copies the old elements. [verified with GCC 13.3 and Clang 18.1 at `-O0` and `-O2`] Decision point: the third line's object is moved, not copied, in both versions, because it is an rvalue; only the relocation of existing elements depends on `noexcept`.

**TR6 The data take-home** (`set.technical.take-home`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: a results note. The 6-hour take-home rehearsal (drills block) uses a seeded synthetic dataset from `market-gen` with planted defects; this set rehearses its parts. Code drill `drl.iv.leakage-audit` (d4): `audit(df)` on a pandas frame flags future-dated features, duplicate keys across folds and unadjusted splits. Hidden tests: each of the three planted defects is flagged with its column; a clean frame returns no flags; the audit runs in under 2 seconds on 100,000 rows in Pyodide [unverified on a phone]. Known-wrong: an audit that checks only nulls fails all three flag tests.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Write the one-page results note for a take-home in which a gradient-boosting model predicted next-day returns of 200 shares with an out-of-sample rank correlation of 0.03 over two years.
  *Model answer, in outline.* (1) The answer first: "a weak but positive signal: out-of-sample rank IC 0.03 (95% interval about 0.01 to 0.05 from a block bootstrap), worth testing further, not trading yet". (2) Data and universe, with how survivorship was handled. (3) Method: features, the time-ordered split with purging, the baseline (a linear model, IC 0.02). (4) Evidence: IC by year and by size bucket, turnover, a cost estimate that leaves the net Sharpe near zero. (5) What failed: two feature families that leaked through announcement times and were removed. (6) Next: a longer sample, a cost model, and a pre-registered test.
  *Rubric (10, the `results-note` rubric).* 2: the answer with its size and uncertainty first (line 1). 1: data (line 2). 2: method and baseline (line 3). 2: evidence including costs (line 4). 2: what failed (line 5). 1: next steps (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A feature has rank correlation 0.95 with next-day returns under random cross-validation and 0.01 under a time-ordered split. Give two explanations and the test that tells them apart.
  *Answer.* (1) Look-ahead: the feature is computed with information from after the prediction time (an end-of-day value joined to the morning). (2) Duplicates or near-duplicates across folds: the same day's data appears in training and validation under random splits, and the model memorises it. Test: rebuild the feature strictly point-in-time and shift it one period later; if the random-split score collapses, it was look-ahead. Separately, group the random folds by date; if the score collapses there, it was cross-fold duplication. Decision point: the time-ordered score, 0.01, is the honest one either way; the diagnosis matters for fixing the pipeline, not for believing the signal.

**IV1 How interviews are marked** (`set.interview.marking`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Two long-form items (no code): marking a transcript, and an aloud solution self-marked. The items are rubric-marked written or spoken tasks and sort-and-order items on transcripts.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Mark the supplied 12-minute transcript of a candidate solving "expected rolls until two different faces have each appeared twice" against the 10-point mock rubric, quoting the transcript for every mark.
  *Model answer, in outline.* (1) Clarify and plan, 1 of 2: the candidate restates the problem but never says what "different" excludes; quote. (2) Reasoning aloud, 2 of 2: states are named before any equation; quote. (3) Method, 2 of 2: first-step analysis on the state "(faces seen once, faces seen twice)"; quote. (4) Answer and check, 1 of 2: a number is given, but no check by a smaller case or a bound; quote. (5) Hints, pressure and time, 1 of 2: the hint about symmetry is accepted but the candidate restarts instead of using it; quote. Total 7 of 10, with the three changes that would have scored 10.
  *Rubric (10, for the marker).* 2 per rubric line: the mark matches the model's within 1 and the quote supports it.
- *Example B (d5).* Solve aloud, recorded through `voice-capture`, "a random walk on the four vertices of a tetrahedron moves to a uniformly random other vertex each step; expected steps to reach a given vertex from another", then self-mark against the rubric with quotes.
  *Answer.* Each step reaches the target with chance $\frac13$ whatever the current vertex, so the wait is geometric: 3 steps. A strong transcript says this symmetry in its first sentence, checks it against the two-vertex case (1 step) and the general $n$-vertex complete graph ($n - 1$ steps), and finishes in under three minutes. Decision point: setting up a three-state chain works but costs time; noticing that every non-target vertex looks the same from the target's point of view is the interview-speed route.

**IV2 You and your work** (`set.interview.you`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Two long-form items: the career story and a project walk-through, both spoken and transcribed.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Record a two-minute answer to "why quant research, and why us?" for a named target firm, then transcribe and self-mark it.
  *Model answer, in outline.* (1) A specific trigger from the learner's path (for example, the 3.1 replication in which half the effect disappeared out of sample). (2) What the learner found they enjoy in it (the honest test, not the result). (3) Evidence of the skills (the three 2.3 projects, each in one line with a number). (4) Why this firm: one fact from its own pages or talks, connected to the learner's interest [the fact must be sourced]. (5) A close that asks for nothing and states what the learner would bring.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: a specific trigger (line 1). 2: motivation that is about the work (line 2). 3: evidence with numbers (line 3). 2: a sourced firm-specific reason (line 4). 1: under two minutes with a clean close (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* Present one 2.3 project in three minutes, then answer three follow-ups chosen by Bertie from its results note ("why should I believe it?", "what would you do differently?", "what broke?"), recorded and self-marked.
  *Answer, in outline.* The walk-through leads with the problem and the result with its uncertainty, then method, then what failed, then next steps; each follow-up answer cites a number from the note and admits one limitation. A strong answer to "why should I believe it?" names the trial count, the held-out period, the costs and the leakage checks before defending any number. Decision point: the follow-ups are where marks are made; a polished three minutes followed by a defensive answer scores below a plainer walk-through followed by candid ones.

**IV3 The process** (`set.interview.process`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Two long-form items: an application plan and an offer comparison.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Write the application plan for the next 12 months: target firms by type, their windows (from each firm's own pages, dated), the order of applications, the referrals sought, the online assessments expected, and the weekly hours each step takes.
  *Model answer, in outline.* (1) 15 to 25 targets in three tiers by fit and difficulty. (2) Each with its window, source and date [checked by the learner]. (3) Applications ordered so that practice interviews at lower-tier targets come before the top tier. (4) Two or three referral routes with a named contact or event. (5) The assessment types expected per firm and the in-app rehearsal for each. (6) A weekly schedule that fits the stage's hours.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: targets in tiers with reasons (line 1). 2: windows with sources and dates (line 2). 2: ordering for practice (line 3). 1: referrals (line 4). 2: assessment preparation (line 5). 1: a feasible schedule (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Offer A: £90,000 base and a discretionary bonus of 0% (chance 0.2), 50% (0.5) or 100% (0.3) of base. Offer B: £110,000 base and a guaranteed 20% first-year bonus. Compare expected first-year pay and its 10th percentile, then say what else decides [illustrative numbers].
  *Answer.* A's expected pay is $90{,}000 \times (1 + 0 \times 0.2 + 0.5 \times 0.5 + 1.0 \times 0.3) = $ £139,500, with a 10th percentile of £90,000 (no bonus); B pays £132,000 with certainty. A is worth £7,500 more on average with a 20% chance of being £42,000 worse. Beyond year one: the bonus history after the first year, the length of any non-compete and how it is paid, deferred compensation, and which team's work and training the learner wants. Decision point: the expected-value comparison is MG1's; the choice also depends on the learner's savings and appetite for variance, MG1 lesson 78's question.

**IV4 Mock interviews** (`set.interview.mocks`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). The set is the mocks themselves: two Bertie mocks by track, one mock run as the interviewer, and their written reviews; the ten logged human mocks sit in the drills block.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* After a 30-minute Bertie probability round, write the review: the timeline's markers, the three lowest rubric lines with quotes, and one drill for each.
  *Model answer, in outline.* (1) Timeline: first clarifying question at 0:40, plan stated at 1:30, first number at 6:10, a hint at 9:00, answer at 12:30, no check. (2) Lowest lines: "answer and check" (no second method; quote), "clarify" (a missing assumption about replacement; quote), "time" (12 minutes on a 10-minute problem; quote). (3) Drills: lesson 38's second-method habit, lesson 1's clarify template, lesson 41's ten-minute rule, each scheduled for the next week.
  *Rubric (10).* 3: an accurate timeline (line 1). 4: the lowest lines with quotes (line 2). 3: one specific, scheduled drill per line (line 3).
- *Example B (d5).* Run a 30-minute mock as the interviewer for a partner, from the interviewer sheet, and mark them; then write how your own answer to the same problem would have scored.
  *Answer, in outline.* The sheet's question, solution, hint ladder and rubric are followed; hints are given at the agreed times; the marks carry quotes; the self-comparison names at least one rubric line on which the partner did better. Decision point: marking someone else shows which behaviours the rubric rewards (a stated plan, a check) more clearly than being marked does, which is why it is a d5 item.

### 3.4 Item 3.3: the books, the mocks and the drills

**The books, in the curriculum's order** (external reading block, 89.5 h). Each is worked cold in `puzzle-workbench` book-log mode (`ASSESSMENT.md` 11.9): the learner enters the number of problems in each chapter of their own edition once, then logs each problem by reference with its outcome (solved cold, solved with a hint, failed, or seen) and time. No problem text is stored. A **seen** problem (recognised from earlier) leaves both the numerator and the denominator and never counts as cold; before 3.3 the weekly bank serves only original problems, so a book problem should rarely be seen (revised by the `docs/PLAN.md` review).

| Order | Book | When | How much | Logged against |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Mosteller, *Fifty Challenging Problems in Probability* | From week 1, beside PZ1 to PZ3 | All 56 problems [recalled count; the learner enters it from the Dover edition] | `3.3.books` (proposed) |
| 2 | Zhou, *A Practical Guide to Quantitative Finance Interviews*, chapter 4 | After PZ5 and before `exm.iv.zhou-cold`'s second sitting | Every problem | `3.3.zhou` (80% cold) |
| 3 | Zhou, chapters 2, 3, 5, 6 and 7 | Chapter 2 and 3 with QM4 to QM6; 5 after PZ6; 6 after MG5; 7 after TR4 | Every problem | `3.3.books` (proposed) |
| 4 | Crack, *Heard on the Street* | After Zhou | About 150 of 242 quantitative questions: all the pure quantitative and logic ones, the derivatives ones on the trader track and for desk quants, the statistics ones on the researcher track | `3.3.books` (proposed) |
| 5 | Joshi, Denson and Downes, *Quant Job Interview Questions and Answers* (developer track and desk quants), or Stefanica, Radoičić and Wang, *150 Most Frequently Asked Questions on Quant Interviews* (others) | After TR5, or after TR3 | Chapters on the track's rounds | `3.3.books` (proposed) |
| 6 | Jane Street, *Probability and Markets* | With MG3 | All of it, once | Read, not logged |

**The mocks.** Ten human mocks over the last ten weeks of 3.3, at least three with strangers (lesson 153), rotated through the track's rounds; each logged in `mock-interview` with its rubric marks and partner type. Five Bertie mocks beyond the three lessons' mocks fill the gaps between them. Each mock's debrief adds its weakest items to the Redo queue.

**The drills.** Speed blocks (6 h) for a learner below a 45 median at the start of 3.3: 20-minute sessions of `strategy-trainer` on the slowest keys and one 80-in-8 sitting a week. Market-making sessions aloud with a partner (5 h): ten 30-minute sessions in `mm-game` partner mode, alternating roles. The take-home rehearsal (6 h): one seeded synthetic dataset with planted defects, a brief, a 6-hour clock that the learner may split over two days, and a results note marked with the `results-note` rubric and Bertie's suggestion beside it. Applications work (4 h) and online-assessment rehearsals (1.5 h) follow IV3.

---

## 4. Topic exam blueprint and the exit tests

### 4.1 Item 3.2: the finals-style paper (`exm.stoch.finals`)

3.2 has no curriculum exit, so its topic exam is what closes it (with the capstone drill, section 4.2). It is of `ASSESSMENT.md`'s `finals` kind, in the shape of a master's-level stochastic calculus final: every question is a model to set up before it is computed, and 40 marks are derivations.

| Property | Value |
|---|---|
| Duration | 180 minutes on one clock (wall-clock, with the 15-minute pause budget of `ASSESSMENT.md` decision 3) |
| Items | 8 multi-part questions, 100 marks, marked per part |
| Difficulty mix, by marks | 20 at d2, 50 at d3, 20 at d4, 10 at d5 (design research 8.4), checked per form by CI within 5 percentage points |
| Format mix, by marks | Long-form 40 (four questions of 10, meeting the `finals` kind's 40% floor); numeric 42; expression 14; choice 2 (inside a mixed question, at most 2 marks a part); sort or match 2 |
| Aids | No calculator. A one-page formula card: the normal CDF table, the Itô multiplication table, the Black-Scholes formula and Greeks, and the moment generating function of the normal; nothing else |
| Answers | Closed forms accepted and expected: $N(\cdot)$, $e^x$, $\ln$, $\sqrt\cdot$ may be left unevaluated; numeric items also accept decimals to 4 significant figures |
| Pass mark | **70 of 100** [our choice: the curriculum names no bar for 3.2; 70% is the bar of every finals-style paper on the path] |
| Forms | At least 3 parallel forms; 60 of 100 marks from generator slots (Q1, Q2, Q4, Q7 and Q8(a) to (c): $12 + 12 + 14 + 14 + 8$), so a resit never repeats a parameter point; each long-form slot draws from an authored pool of at least six items |
| Practice | One practice paper from the practice pool (never the exam reserve), in this paper's shape |
| Resit | 7-day cool-down and a remediation set on the failed skills (`ASSESSMENT.md` 3.8 for finals-style papers) |
| Help | None: Bertie is away app-wide while the paper runs |

**Slots.**

| Slot | Levels | Content | Format | Difficulty by part | Marks |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Q1 | AT1 to AT3 | Trees: (a) replicate a one-step claim and give its price and hedge; (b) price a path-dependent claim on a three-period tree; (c) a Radon-Nikodym derivative or a state price for given paths | N, N, N | (a) d2 4, (b) d3 4, (c) d3 4 | 12 |
| Q2 | AT4, AT5 | American claims and rates: (a) decide which random times are stopping times; (b) price an American put on a three-period tree and give the exercise nodes; (c) a zero-coupon bond or caplet on a short-rate tree | S, N, N | (a) d2 2, (b) d3 6, (c) d3 4 | 12 |
| Q3 | BR1, BR2 | Measure and conditioning, written out (pool: the convergence counterexample and the dominated convergence hypothesis; $E[X \mid X + Y]$ by symmetry; a normal's change of measure and its use for a rare event; the independence lemma for a given $f$; Bayes' formula under a change of measure; $\sigma(X^2)$ against $\sigma(X)$) | Long-form | d3 | 10 |
| Q4 | BR3 to BR5 | Brownian motion: (a) variances and covariances of combinations; (b) a reflection probability; (c) a hitting probability or exit time with drift; (d) a bridge or zero-set probability | N, N, X, N | (a) d2 4, (b) d3 4, (c) d4 4, (d) d2 2 | 14 |
| Q5 | BR4, IC1, IC2 | Quadratic variation and Itô, written out (pool: $E\sum(\Delta W)^2$ and its variance; the isometry for simple integrands; $\int W\,dW$ from left sums; $E[W^{2k}]$ by Itô's formula; Itô's formula from Taylor's theorem; a martingale test with its correction term) | Long-form | d4 | 10 |
| Q6 | IC3, RN1 | Pricing by replication or by measure, written out (pool: the BSM equation by replication; the call price as a risk-neutral expectation; Girsanov for constant $\theta$; the hedging P&L with a wrong volatility; a power claim's price and its PDE check) | Long-form | d3 | 10 |
| Q7 | RN1 to RN3 | Risk-neutral pricing and PDEs: (a) a change of drift or a market price of risk; (b) the PDE that a given expectation solves (Feynman-Kac); (c) build an arbitrage from two shares on one motion, or price a Vasicek bond | N, X, X | (a) d2 4, (b) d3 4, (c) d4 6 | 14 |
| Q8 | EX1 to EX3 | Exotics, numéraires and volatility models: (a) sign a barrier or Asian sensitivity; (b) a perpetual American put's exercise level and value; (c) an exchange option or caplet price from given inputs; (d) written out (pool: a one-touch or knock-out price from the maximum of drifted Brownian motion; Margrabe by numéraire; Dupire from the forward equation; Merton's price as a Poisson mixture; the HJM drift condition) | C, N, N, long-form | (a) d2 2, (b) d3 4, (c) d2 2, (d) d5 10 | 18 |

**Totals.** Marks 100. By difficulty: d2 $4 + 2 + 4 + 2 + 4 + 2 + 2 = 20$; d3 $4 + 4 + 6 + 4 + 10 + 4 + 10 + 4 + 4 = 50$; d4 $4 + 10 + 6 = 20$; d5 10. Long-form 40 (Q3, Q5, Q6, Q8(d)). Generator slots 60.

**Coverage.** Every form tests all five courses and at least 17 of the 20 levels (Q3's and Q8(d)'s pools rotate the rest), above `ASSESSMENT.md`'s 80% rule. By course, marks run AT 24, BR 24, IC and RN 34 between them (Q5 to Q7, whose pools mix the two), and EX 18, against lesson-hour shares of 23%, 26%, 36% and 14%: EX is over-weighted on purpose, because exotics, numéraires and the smile are what a desk-quant interview tests most [recalled].

**Worked example of one slot (Q8(d), d5).** "Under $\widetilde P$, $S(t) = S(0)e^{\nu t + \sigma\widetilde W(t)}$ with $\nu = r - \frac12\sigma^2$. Derive the probability that $S$ touches a barrier $B > S(0)$ before $T$, and price a one-touch that pays 1 at $T$ if it has. Evaluate for $S(0) = 100$, $B = 120$, $r = 5\%$, $\sigma = 20\%$, $T = 1$." Model answer: (1) With $X = \ln\frac S{S(0)}$ and $b = \ln\frac B{S(0)}$, touching means $\max_{t \le T}X(t) \ge b$. (2) Change measure to remove the drift: under $\widehat P$ with $\frac{d\widehat P}{d\widetilde P} = e^{-\frac\nu\sigma\widetilde W(T) - \frac{\nu^2}{2\sigma^2}T}$, $\frac X\sigma$ is a Brownian motion. (3) Reflection for the driftless motion, with the density weight carried along, gives $P(\max X \ge b) = 1 - N\left(\frac{b - \nu T}{\sigma\sqrt T}\right) + e^{2\nu b/\sigma^2}N\left(\frac{-b - \nu T}{\sigma\sqrt T}\right)$. (4) Numbers: $b = \ln 1.2 \approx 0.1823$, $\nu = 0.03$: $1 - N(0.762) + e^{0.2735}N(-1.062) \approx 0.2231 + 1.3145 \times 0.1442 \approx 0.4127$. (5) The one-touch paid at $T$ is $e^{-rT} \times 0.4127 \approx 0.3926$. (6) Check: it exceeds the chance of ending above the barrier, $1 - N(0.762) \approx 0.223$, and is close to twice it, as reflection predicts when the drift is small; a bridge-corrected simulation gives $0.4132 \pm 0.0008$. Rubric (10): 1 for the reduction to the maximum (line 1); 3 for the change of measure (line 2), the leap; 3 for the reflected formula with the $e^{2\nu b/\sigma^2}$ factor (line 3); 1 for the numbers (line 4); 1 for discounting (line 5); 1 for a check (line 6). CI checks every line with SymPy, recomputes the numbers with SciPy, and runs the simulation.

### 4.2 Item 3.2: the proposed completion, `3.2.done`

The curriculum gives 3.2 no exit; without one it has no completion state, and the derivatives add-on (canon-gap P16) needs one. Proposed, for the owner (open item 1):

| Part | Gate | Confirm | Why this |
|---|---|---|---|
| `3.2.done` | (1) `exm.stoch.finals` at 70%; (2) the capstone drill `drl.stoch.exit-three-pricers`: a European call by formula, CRR tree, Crank-Nicolson and control-variate Monte Carlo agreeing within $10^{-3}$ (Monte Carlo within its 95% interval), an up-and-out call within its interval of 1.17607, and an American put by tree within $10^{-3}$ of 6.0896 at 1,000 steps, all hidden tests passing | Optional: 30 Chin, Nel and Ólafsson problems logged cold at 70% (`problem-sets` refs) | The paper tests the derivations; the drill tests that the learner can make the three numerical routes agree, which is what a desk quant does on the first day. A book confirm is optional because the solved-problems book is paid |

```ts
// proposed completion data for 3.2; uses ASSESSMENT.md 2.10's ExitPart unchanged
{ id: '3.2.done',
  text: 'Stochastic calculus completed: the finals-style paper at 70% and three pricers that agree',
  gate: [ { kind: 'exam', exam: 'exm.stoch.finals', pass: 0.7 },
          { kind: 'drill', drills: ['drl.stoch.exit-three-pricers'] } ],
  confirm: [],
  track: { deferTo: 3, tracks: ['derivatives'] },     // shown only to learners who opted into the add-on
  estHours: 6 }
```

`estHours` 6 is the paper and the drill (the practice paper and marking are spent time). If the owner prefers no completion part, 3.2 closes on the paper alone and the drill becomes ungated practice.

### 4.3 Item 3.3: the written interview paper (`exm.iv.paper`)

A paper sat under interview conditions: no calculator, no notes, Bertie away, a visible clock, and a separate timed speed section. It closes topic 3.3 together with the exit parts (decision 12).

| Property | Value |
|---|---|
| Duration | 120 minutes: a 6-minute speed section on its own clock first (`ASSESSMENT.md` decision 27), then 114 minutes for the rest, with a suggested split shown |
| Items | 15 questions, 100 marks: the speed section, 6 probability questions, 2 brainteaser and quick-maths questions, 3 markets and options questions, 2 track questions, 1 written explanation |
| Difficulty mix, by marks | 20 at d2, 50 at d3, 20 at d4, 10 at d5 |
| Format mix, by marks | Fixed by slot on every form: timed deck 10 (S0); numeric 41; expression 6 (A5(b), B2(a)); choice 2 (C3(a)); sort or match 3 (C3(b)); long-form 20 (A6, E1). The other 18 marks (B1 5, C2(a) 3, D1 5, D2 5) take the format their slot lists, which varies by form and track: numeric, order, a quote in `mm-game` (G), predict the output (T), expression or sort. Choice is therefore 2 marks on every form |
| Aids | None: no calculator, no formula card (interview conditions, decision 19) |
| Answers | Exact fractions and closed forms accepted; numeric items accept 3 significant figures |
| Pass mark | **70 of 100** (the path's bar for topic exams) |
| Forms | At least 3 parallel forms; 59 of 100 marks from generator slots (S0, A1, A3, A4, A5, B2, C1, C2, C3); the track questions D1 and D2 are drawn from the learner's track pool |
| Practice | One practice paper from the practice pool |
| Resit | 3-day cool-down and a remediation set (`ASSESSMENT.md` 3.8 for topic exams) |
| Rules for originality | Every item is original (decision 8); no item reproduces a Zhou chapter 4 or 5 problem |

**Slots.**

| Slot | Levels | Content | Format | Difficulty by part | Marks |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| S0 | QM1, QM3 | Speed section: 30 arithmetic items in 6 minutes in the Zetamac mix, free entry; marks = correct items ÷ 3 | D | d2 10 | 10 |
| A1 | PZ1, PZ2 | Symmetry, complements and indicators: (a) a symmetry probability; (b) an expected count by indicators | N, N | (a) d2 2, (b) d3 4 | 6 |
| A2 | PZ3 | Conditioning: a Bayes update in odds form, or a decision after evidence | N | d3 6 | 6 |
| A3 | PZ4 | States and chains: (a) an expected waiting time by first-step analysis; (b) a pattern race or a "win by two" probability | N, N | (a) d3 4, (b) d4 2 | 6 |
| A4 | PZ5 | Continuous: (a) an order-statistic or volume probability; (b) an orthant probability or a correlation bound | N, N | (a) d3 4, (b) d5 2 | 6 |
| A5 | PZ6 | Stochastic processes: (a) a Brownian covariance; (b) a hitting probability with drift, or a moment by Itô's formula | N, X | (a) d2 2, (b) d3 4 | 6 |
| A6 | PZ2 to PZ5, PZ7 | Written out as if aloud (pool: a waiting time derived and generalised to $n$; a variance by indicator pairs; a conditional expectation with the symmetry argument justified; an expected value by tail sums): the plan, the derivation, a second-method check | Long-form | d4 | 10 |
| B1 | QM4, QM5 | A brainteaser: an invariant, pigeonhole, backward-induction, information or number puzzle, with a two-sentence argument | N or O | d3 5 | 5 |
| B2 | QM6 | Quick maths: (a) a limit or integral; (b) eigenvalues of a structured matrix or the validity of a correlation matrix | X, N | (a) d2 2, (b) d3 3 | 5 |
| C1 | MG1 | Bets: (a) a fair price; (b) a hedge to lock in, or a cross-book arbitrage | N, N | (a) d2 2, (b) d3 4 | 6 |
| C2 | MG2, MG3 | Sizing and quoting: (a) a Kelly fraction for given odds, or a quote after a trade in `mm-game`; (b) Kelly for simultaneous bets or correlated assets | N or G, N | (a) d3 3, (b) d5 3 | 6 |
| C3 | MG5 | Options: (a) an arbitrage in a list of quotes; (b) the sign or size of a Greek; (c) a digital with skew, or a basket against options | C, S, N | (a) d2 2, (b) d3 3, (c) d4 3 | 8 |
| D1 | TR by track | Researcher: a sample size or omitted-variable bias. Developer: predict a program's output and its complexity. Trader: a market after a reveal with an informed counterparty | N, T or G | d4 5 | 5 |
| D2 | TR by track | Researcher: an ML-lab derivation (a gradient, a stability bound, attention's scale). Developer: a C++ semantics question (copies and moves, invalidation). Trader: a multi-outcome Kelly or a linked-market arbitrage | X, S or N | d5 5 | 5 |
| E1 | IV1, IV2 | Written explanation (pool: explain to a non-specialist why a strategy's backtested Sharpe ratio overstates its future one; explain why a delta-hedged option's P&L depends on realised volatility; explain how you would check a colleague's probability answer in a minute), marked with the mock rubric's communication lines | Long-form | d3 | 10 |

**Totals.** Marks 100. By difficulty: d2 $10 + 2 + 2 + 2 + 2 + 2 = 20$; d3 $4 + 6 + 4 + 4 + 4 + 5 + 3 + 4 + 3 + 3 + 10 = 50$; d4 $2 + 10 + 3 + 5 = 20$; d5 $2 + 3 + 5 = 10$. Long-form 20. Generator slots 59. Every form examines all five courses; by course the marks run PZ 40, QM 20, MG 20, TR 10, IV 10, against lesson-hour shares of 27%, 20%, 18%, 21% and 14%. Probability is over-weighted on purpose because the exit and the interviews weight it most (curriculum 1.5: "the subject interviews test hardest"); TR is under-weighted because its material was examined in the 1.6, 2.1, 1.8 and 1.9 papers and its depth is track-specific, so the two track questions sample it rather than cover it.

### 4.4 Item 3.3: the exit, rebuilt in the app

The curriculum's three checkboxes become three exit parts, as `ASSESSMENT.md` 2.9 already lists them; this file fixes their instruments and adds the proposed fourth part for the map line.

**The cold set (`exm.iv.zhou-cold`, kind `cold-set`).** 20 recreated green-book-style problems, weighted as Zhou's chapter 4 is by section [recalled section titles; check against the learner's edition, open item 6]: basic probability and set operations 2; combinatorial analysis 4; conditional probability and Bayes 5; discrete and continuous distributions 3; expected value, variance and covariance 4; order statistics 2. Difficulty 4 at d2, 10 at d3, 4 at d4, 2 at d5. Two sittings of 60 minutes and 10 problems, at least a day apart; cold rules (no hints, no Bertie, one attempt, the solution after); each problem marked solved (1) only for the exact answer with a method line that supports it. Pass 16 of 20. Forms: an authored pool of at least 120 original problems tagged by section and difficulty, plus generator families for 8 of the 20 slots; no learner sees a problem twice in a cold set.

| Part | Curriculum line | Gate (in the app) | Confirm (logged) | Taught in | Runs on |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `3.3.zhou` | 80% of the probability chapter in Zhou solved cold | `exm.iv.zhou-cold` at 80% (16 of 20) | `book-log` of Zhou chapter 4 in the learner's own copy: problems solved cold at least 80% of the chapter's count, entered from their edition | PZ1 to PZ5, PZ7 | Both |
| `3.3.zetamac` | Zetamac at 50 or more | Median of the last 7 study days' cold Zetamac-format scores, physical keyboard, at least 5 such days, at 50 or more (`ASSESSMENT.md` 9.3) | None: the in-app run is the Zetamac format | QM1, QM3, the Daily 10, the speed blocks | Both |
| `3.3.mocks` | Ten mock interviews done, at least three with a stranger | `mock-interview` log: ten mocks with a human partner, each with its rubric marked, and at least three with partner type "stranger" (self-declared) | The log is the evidence | IV1 to IV4 | Both |
| `3.3.books` (proposed, decision 14) | The map line: "the interview books solved cold" | `book-log` of every book on the track's list (Mosteller; Zhou chapters 2 to 7; Crack's selected questions; Joshi or Stefanica by track), each with at least 70% of its logged problems solved cold, Zhou chapter 4's 80% counted under `3.3.zhou` | The log is the evidence | All of 3.3 | Both |

```ts
// exit data for 3.3, using ASSESSMENT.md 2.10's ExitPart; 3.3.books needs book-log's enum widened (request 1 below)
{ id: '3.3.zhou',
  text: '80% of the probability chapter in Zhou solved cold',
  gate: [ { kind: 'exam', exam: 'exm.iv.zhou-cold', pass: 0.8 } ],
  confirm: [ { kind: 'book-log', book: 'zhou', chapter: '4', share: 0.8 } ],
  estHours: 14 }          // the two 60-minute sittings (2) and the chapter worked cold (12)

{ id: '3.3.zetamac',
  text: 'Zetamac at 50 or more',
  gate: [ { kind: 'median', metric: 'zetamac-cold', days: 7, minRuns: 5, target: 50 } ],
  confirm: [],
  estHours: 6 }           // the speed reserve; the Daily 10 is outside the hours

{ id: '3.3.mocks',
  text: 'Ten mock interviews done, at least three with a stranger',
  gate: [ { kind: 'log-count', log: 'mock-human', target: 10 },
          { kind: 'log-count', log: 'mock-stranger', target: 3 } ],
  confirm: [],
  estHours: 15 }

// proposed: the map line's "interview books solved cold"
{ id: '3.3.books',
  text: 'The interview books, in order, solved cold',
  gate: [ { kind: 'book-log', book: 'mosteller', chapter: 'all', share: 0.7 },
          { kind: 'book-log', book: 'zhou', chapter: '2,3,5,6,7', share: 0.7 },
          { kind: 'book-log', book: 'crack', chapter: 'selected', share: 0.7 } ],   // plus 'joshi' or 'stefanica' by track (request 2)
  confirm: [],
  estHours: 61.5 }        // Mosteller 12, Zhou chapters 2, 3, 5, 6, 7 26, Crack 22, Jane Street 1.5; Joshi or Stefanica sits in the track variant
```

**Requests to `ASSESSMENT.md` 2.10** (no new instrument kinds): (1) widen `book-log`'s `book` enum to `['mosteller', 'zhou', 'crack', 'joshi', 'stefanica']` and let `chapter` take a list or `all`; (2) a track-selected gate list, as `stage1-dsa-cpp.md` section 8's request 3 also needs, so that `3.3.books` can name Joshi on the developer track and Stefanica elsewhere.

**How faithful the rebuild is.** The three checkboxes are met exactly: the real chapter at 80% cold (with our paper gating first, `ASSESSMENT.md` decision 1), the Zetamac score on default settings, and ten human mocks with three strangers. The in-app cold set adds a safeguard the curriculum's line cannot have: a learner who has seen some of the chapter's problems before still has to solve 16 of 20 unseen ones. The stage-level "books solved cold" is the one addition, and it is proposed, not imposed.

### 4.5 The Stage 2 exit test, rebuilt

The curriculum's Stage 2 map line reads "Three finished projects, each with tests and a written results note"; 2.1 and 2.2 have their own exit lines; 2.3 has no separate exit line, so its bar is the map line together with the item's rule that every project ships with a README, tests and a one-page results note that includes what failed; 2.4 has none, and `stage1-dsa-cpp.md` section 4.3 proposes one. Stage 2 is therefore closed when every topic is closed (topic paper and exit parts), with the map line's projects bar shown first. On the Trader and Researcher tracks, `1.9.exit` moves here (`stage1-dsa-cpp.md` 4.4).

| Topic | Topic paper | Exit part | Gate (in the app) | Confirm (logged) | Defined in | Runs on |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2.1 Machine learning | `exm.ml` | `2.1.exit` | `ml-workbench` capstone passing its pipeline and leakage audits; the topic exam at 70% with `slotMin` 0.7 on its three closed-book explanation slots (leakage, regularisation, bias and variance) | Optional: the learner's own repository | `ASSESSMENT.md` 2.9; `stage2-ml-papers.md` 4.3 | Web app unless scikit-learn ships in the Artifact |
| 2.2 Time series | `exm.ts` | `2.2.exit` | `exm.ts` at 70% with `slotMin` 0.7 on Q15 ("why returns, not prices"); capstone `drl.ts.exit-coint` | None | `stage1-statistics-ts.md` 4.6 | Paper both; drill where statsmodels ships |
| 2.3 Projects | `exm.proj.methods` (proposed there) | `2.3.exit` | Three project records marked finished (`ASSESSMENT.md` 11.4), of three different kinds, each with tests passing (a pasted `pytest -q`, none failed), a README and a results note at 7 of 10 with its predicates passing, including what failed | The public repositories | `stage1-finance-projects.md` 4.2.3 | Both (pastes); repository check on the web |
| 2.4 Contests | None | `2.4.exit` (proposed) | 30 contests logged and upsolved; `arena-3` with at least 3 of 6 solved | None | `stage1-dsa-cpp.md` 4.3 | Both |
| 1.9 C++ (Trader and Researcher tracks) | `exm.cpp` (sat in Stage 1) | `1.9.exit` | `exit-1.9` paste-verify: the pricer's doctest summary and timings | None | `stage1-dsa-cpp.md` 4.4 | Paste both; build local |

Stage page line: "Stage 2 exit: projects 2 of 3 finished (tests and results notes). Topics closed: 2.1, 2.2; open: 2.3, 2.4."

**If the owner accepts canon-gap's Stage 2 proposals**, each new item (2.0a numerical methods, 2.0b optimisation, 2.0c portfolio theory, 2.0d microstructure, 2.0e systems, 2.2b financial econometrics, 2.2c research method) brings its own topic paper and exit part into this table, and P1's checklist (pre-registration, bias audit, deflated Sharpe ratio, bootstrap interval) joins `2.3.exit` as `notePredicates` (`ASSESSMENT.md` 2.9). No schema change is needed beyond the `projectKind` list that `stage1-finance-projects.md` open item 6 requests.

**The trader-track conflict** (`ASSESSMENT.md` 2.11): the curriculum's track table gives traders "one project", the Stage 2 exit asks for three. The planner follows the exit (three), ordering the options dashboard first on the Trader track, until the owner decides.

**Sit it first.** On entering Stage 2 the stage page offers what can be sat cold: the 2.1 topic exam, `exm.ts` with its capstone, `exm.proj.methods`, `arena-3`, and, for a learner who arrives with projects, "Submit an existing project": a project record created from an existing repository, checked against the same finished rule. A learner who passes everything skips the stage; anything failed becomes the study list by level.

```ts
// the Stage 2 exit as data: a stage closes when its listed parts are met (ASSESSMENT.md 2.12)
{ stage: 2,
  headline: { part: '2.3.exit', label: 'Three finished projects, each with tests and a written results note' },
  parts: ['2.1.exit', '2.2.exit', '2.3.exit', '2.4.exit'],
  trackParts: { trader: ['1.9.exit'], researcher: ['1.9.exit'] } }   // deferred from Stage 1
```

### 4.6 The Stage 3 exit test, rebuilt

The curriculum's Stage 3 map line reads "One paper replicated out of sample; the interview books solved cold", and the 3.3 checklist has three boxes. Stage 3 is closed when 3.1 and 3.3 are closed; 3.2 is optional and closes separately for learners on the derivatives add-on.

| Topic | Topic paper | Exit part | Gate (in the app) | Confirm (logged) | `estHours` | Runs on |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 3.1 Research papers | `exm.papers` (proposed in `stage2-ml-papers.md` 4.4; `ASSESSMENT.md` 2.8 lists none) | `3.1.exit` | A project record of kind `replication` marked finished, with the results note's `oos-section` predicate passing (a plain statement of how much of the effect survived after publication) | The repository | 41 (`stage2-ml-papers.md` 4.5) | Both |
| 3.2 Stochastic calculus (add-on only) | `exm.stoch.finals` | `3.2.done` (proposed) | Section 4.2 | Optional | 6 | Both |
| 3.3 Interview preparation | `exm.iv.paper` (proposed) | `3.3.zhou`, `3.3.zetamac`, `3.3.mocks`, and `3.3.books` (proposed) | Section 4.4 | Section 4.4 | 14, 6, 15, 61.5 | Both |

Stage page line, map line first: "Stage 3 exit: replication finished (1 of 1). Interview books solved cold: Mosteller 82%, Zhou chapter 4 76% (needs 80%), Crack 41%. Zetamac median 47 (needs 50). Mocks 7 of 10 (2 of 3 with strangers)."

**Sit it first.** A learner arriving at Stage 3 can sit `exm.iv.zhou-cold` and `exm.iv.paper` at once, log a Zetamac median, and submit an existing replication; the real Zhou chapter and the books are offered only after the in-app cold set is passed, because a real chapter can be solved cold once (`ASSESSMENT.md` decision 1). Mocks cannot be tested out of: the exit asks for them done.

**The end of the path.** Passing the Stage 3 exit completes the curriculum's 3,100 hours (more on tracks with accepted proposals). The weekly probability problems continue in cold mode, the Daily 10 continues, and the app offers the interview banks as standing practice, because the hiring calendar (IV3) usually runs past the last lesson.

## 5. Reading and resource map per level

### 5.0 How to use this map

- **One primary resource per topic** (curriculum rule 1). For 3.2 the spine is **Shreve, *Stochastic Calculus for Finance* I and II** (Springer 2004), the curriculum's pick; **Baxter and Rennie, *Financial Calculus*** (CUP 1996) is the short first read before course BR; **MIT 18.S096 Fall 2013** is the lecture parallel; **Chin, Nel and Ólafsson, *Problems and Solutions in Mathematical Finance*, volume 1: Stochastic Calculus** (Wiley 2014; chapters: general probability theory, the Wiener process, stochastic differential equations, change of measure, the Poisson process [searched]) is the solved-problems book. For 3.3 the spine is **the four books in the curriculum's order** (Mosteller, Zhou, Crack, Joshi), worked cold through the book-log (section 3.4), with **Jane Street's *Probability and Markets*** for MG3.
- **Problems over reading** (rule 2). Every row says what to do. In 3.2 the budget is 27 hours of reading and watching and 15 hours of external problems, about 1.35 and 0.75 hours per level; in 3.3 the books are problem-solving, not reading, and are logged per problem.
- **Second explanations.** Björk, Øksendal, Glasserman and Gatheral (3.2), and ISLP, Goodfellow, Bengio and Courville, Meyers and learncpp (3.3) appear where they teach a level's idea better or deeper; read the alternative instead of the spine for that section, not as well. Rows marked "stretch" or "optional" are outside the budget.
- **Held back.** Zhou chapters 4 and 5 are never used in lessons or homework (decision 8); they are worked cold only. Shreve's exercises and Chin, Nel and Ólafsson's problems are not reproduced in homework.
- **[check]** marks a chapter, section, lecture or problem number recalled but not confirmed here. **Shreve**: volume II's eleven chapter titles were confirmed by search (general probability theory; information and conditioning; Brownian motion; stochastic calculus; risk-neutral pricing; connections with partial differential equations; exotic options; American derivative securities; change of numéraire; term-structure models; introduction to jump processes) [searched]; these section numbers and titles were confirmed by search: volume I §1.3 (computational considerations), §3.3 (capital asset pricing), §5.4 (the perpetual American put) and §6.5 (futures); volume II §3.5 (Markov property), §3.6 (first passage time distribution), §3.7 (reflection principle), §4.4 (the Itô-Doeblin formula) and §4.7 (the Brownian bridge) [searched]; the other section numbers are as I recall them and fit around these [check]. **MIT 18.S096 Fall 2013**: lectures 5 (Stochastic Processes I), 17 (Stochastic Processes II) and 18 (Itō Calculus), all by Choongbum Lee, 19 (Black-Scholes Formula, Risk-neutral Valuation, Vasily Strela) and 21 (Stochastic Differential Equations, Choongbum Lee) were confirmed [searched]; other lecture numbers are [check]. **Zhou**: chapter titles as recalled (1 General Principles; 2 Brain Teasers; 3 Calculus and Linear Algebra; 4 Probability Theory; 5 Stochastic Process and Stochastic Calculus; 6 Finance; 7 Algorithms and Numerical Methods) [check against the learner's copy]. **Mosteller**: problems 1 to 13 confirmed by title (1 The sock drawer, 2 Successive wins, 3 The flippant juror, 4 Trials until first success, 5 Coin in square, 6 Chuck-a-luck, 7 Curing the compulsive gambler, 8 Perfect bridge hand, 9 Craps, 10 An experiment in personal taste for money, 11 Silent cooperation, 12 Quo vadis?, 13 The prisoner's dilemma) [searched], with 19, 42 and 47 confirmed in `stage1-probability.md`; the rest [check]. **Crack**: the 24th edition (2023) has 242 quantitative questions [searched]; its chapter grouping (pure quantitative and logic, financial economics, derivatives, statistics, non-quantitative) is [recalled; check].

## 6. Verification approach

Every auto-marked item has two independent implementations of its answer (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 6.2): the TypeScript generator (or the author's declared answer) that builds the item, and a Python verifier written from the generator's plain-language `semantics` without reading the TypeScript, run in CI under `tools/zqverify` with pinned SymPy, NumPy, SciPy and mpmath. CI also replays every listed distractor through the runtime grader and requires it to be marked wrong with its intended diagnosis. This file adds four rules of its own [our choice]:

1. **Exact where the answer is exact.** Every tree, card, dice, chain, game and counting answer is an exact `Fraction` or SymPy rational, computed by enumeration or by an exact linear solve.
2. **Two routes for every continuous answer.** A closed form is checked against a second route that shares no algebra with it: a numerical integral (SciPy `quad` or mpmath at 30 digits), a PDE solve, a tree, or a seeded simulation with at least $10^6$ draws (or exact Brownian-bridge maxima where a path maximum is involved) within 4 standard errors. Disagreement fails CI.
3. **Simulations are never graded on their estimate** (as in `stage1-probability.md` decision 8). Every simulation screen and every Monte Carlo test is seeded; CI requires its displayed result to lie within 4 standard errors of the exact value, so no seed shows a misleading run.
4. **Arbitrage claims are linear programmes.** Every "there is an arbitrage" answer must be a feasible solution with positive value of the linear programme over the quoted instruments (SciPy `linprog`), and every "there is none" must have optimum 0.

| Family | Items | CI: second implementation | Runtime check | Traps the check must catch |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Binomial trees, path-dependent claims, measure changes, state prices | AT1 to AT3 | Backward induction against enumeration of all $2^N$ paths in exact fractions; $E[Z] = 1$, $\widetilde E[X] = E[ZX]$ and $\sum\zeta = (1 + r)^{-N}$ asserted on every generated tree | `numeric` with exact fractions; `set` for paths | Real probabilities used for prices; a lookback priced on the recombining tree; $Z$ upside down |
| Incomplete one-period models | AT1 | The price interval by a linear programme over risk-neutral vectors, against the closed-form endpoints | `interval` | Open ends reported as closed; one price reported |
| American claims and stopping times | AT4 | Backward induction against brute force over every stopping rule for $N \le 4$ (every adapted exercise map enumerated); stopping-time items by checking $\{\tau = n\} \in \mathcal F_n$ on the enumerated space | `numeric`, `region` for exercise maps | Exercising wherever in the money; a rule that peeks ahead accepted as a stopping time |
| Random walks and short-rate trees | AT5 | Generating functions by SymPy series against path counts; bond and caplet prices by exact path sums against backward induction | `numeric`, `expression` | Discounting at average rates; paying a caplet at its fixing date |
| Measure, conditioning, change of measure | BR1, BR2 | SymPy integrals and limits; conditional normal moments by linear algebra against simulation; importance-sampling moments by closed form $e^{\theta^2}N(-a - \theta)$ against mpmath quadrature | `numeric`, `expression` | A limit and an expectation exchanged without a bound; the conditional variance left unconditional |
| Brownian motion: covariances, bridges, hitting, maxima, zeros | BR3 to BR5 | Closed forms (SciPy and mpmath) against seeded simulations with exact bridge maxima; joint densities integrated numerically to recover their marginals; drifted formulas checked at $\mu \to 0$ against the driftless ones | `numeric`, `expression` | A factor of 2 missing in reflection; the drift factor $e^{2\mu m/\sigma^2}$ dropped; independence assumed between $W(s)$ and $W(t)$ |
| Itô integrals and Itô's formula | IC1, IC2 | A small symbolic Itô engine in SymPy (the multiplication table applied to polynomial and exponential functions of $t$ and $W$) recomputes every differential; moments by the Itô recursion against Gaussian moment formulas; isometry answers against SymPy integrals | `expression` probed at 8 seeded points; `numeric` | The ordinary chain rule; drift counted in quadratic variation; $\int\dots dt$ treated by the isometry |
| BSM, Greeks, hedging P&L, power claims | IC3, RN1 | The formula against numerical integration of the risk-neutral payoff, a CRR tree at 2,000 steps, Crank-Nicolson and Monte Carlo; Greeks against central finite differences; the PDE residual of every closed form computed by SymPy and required to be 0 | `numeric` to 4 significant figures | Real drift in a price; theta per year read as per day; $N(d_1)$ and $N(d_2)$ swapped |
| Multi-dimensional Itô and simulation orders | IC4 | Ratio volatilities by SymPy; Euler and Milstein orders fitted by regression of log errors on log steps over seeded runs, bands asserted; the weak error by its exact formula | `numeric`, `interval` for fitted orders | Comparing against independent exact samples; one order for both error measures |
| Fundamental theorems, completeness, static replication | RN2 | Arbitrage portfolios by linear programme (rule 4); static replication sums on a strike grid converging to the closed form | `numeric`, `matrix` for portfolios | Arbitrage claimed from different expected returns alone; weights without $dK$ |
| SDEs, Feynman-Kac, finite differences, Monte Carlo | RN3 | SymPy checks that each claimed $g(t, x)$ solves its PDE and terminal condition; affine bond prices against simulation of the exact Ornstein-Uhlenbeck transition and its integral; stability limits by the scheme's amplification factor | `numeric`, `expression` | A forward-running PDE; an unstable time step reported as fine; doubling paths to halve the error |
| Exotics, American options, numéraires, rates, volatility models | EX1 to EX3 | Barrier prices by killed-density integration against bridge-corrected simulation; lookbacks by Hull's formula against the maximum's law and simulation; geometric Asians by closed form against simulation; perpetual puts by maximising over levels numerically; American puts by tree against Longstaff-Schwartz; Margrabe and Black against numerical integration; Merton by its series against simulation; implied volatilities by Brent's method; Dupire on a flat surface recovering a constant | `numeric` | Monitoring frequency ignored; the drift compensation of jumps dropped; caplets discounted from the fixing date |
| Counting, symmetry, indicators, conditioning | PZ1 to PZ3, PZ7 | Exhaustive enumeration with `itertools` and `Fraction`s on the actual parameters (up to $10^7$ outcomes), against the closed form | `numeric` with exact fractions | Unordered outcomes treated as equally likely; dependent indicators' variance taken as binomial (and the cases where it happens to agree flagged in the item's notes) |
| Chains, waiting times, races, games | PZ4 | SymPy exact solves of the first-step system against the overlap rule and against simulation | `numeric` | Resetting to the start after a miss; a race decided by expected waits |
| Continuous and geometric | PZ5 | SymPy areas and volumes (by symmetry piece and in full) against $10^6$-point seeded simulation | `numeric`, `region` | Uniform radius for a uniform point; a missing symmetry factor |
| Stochastic processes at interview depth | PZ6 | As BR3 to IC2 | `numeric`, `expression` | As those families |
| Speed and estimation decks | QM1 to QM3 | Generators with exact integer or decimal answers checked by Python's `decimal`; estimation items with a stated tolerance (5%) and Fermi items against a sourced fact with its citation and date, tolerance a factor of 3 | `numeric` with tolerance; deck score | A fact without a source or date; a tolerance that admits a distractor |
| Brainteasers and number puzzles | QM4, QM5 | Exhaustive state-space search for reachability and invariants (as for the board puzzle of lesson 56); timer constructions replayed by a state simulator; knowledge puzzles by model checking over possible worlds; modular answers by direct `pow` | `numeric`, `order` for constructions | A construction that works only by rounding; an invariant claim that a search refutes |
| Calculus and linear algebra recall | QM6 | SymPy limits, integrals and ODE solutions; eigenvalues by NumPy against the structured formula; validity by exact $LDL^\top$ in fractions against eigenvalues | `expression`, `matrix` | Dropping a boundary term; entries in $[-1, 1]$ taken as validity |
| Bets, hedges, replication, Kelly | MG1, MG2 | Expected values exactly; hedges by exact linear solve; replication stakes by backward induction against path checks; Kelly by SymPy derivative and by numerical maximisation of expected log wealth; arbitrage by linear programme (rule 4) | `numeric`, `game` for stakes | Odds read as probabilities; Kelly fractions added across correlated bets |
| Market-making and trading games | MG3, MG4 | Posteriors by enumeration over the hidden quantity (at most $10^6$ outcomes); quote scores by the `mm-game` scoring rule, property-tested (a quote centred on the posterior mean never scores below an off-centre one of equal width); bots deterministic by seed; Figgie posteriors by enumeration over the 12 compositions | `quote`, `predicate: mm-score` | Grading realised P&L; a stale quote scored as current |
| Options at interview speed | MG5 | Black-Scholes numbers as above; arbitrage in quotes by linear programme; the skew-adjusted digital by central differences of the smile-adjusted call price | `numeric`, `choice` | A digital priced at the at-the-money volatility; convexity checked by monotonicity only |
| Statistics, ML and ML-lab items | TR1 to TR3 | Closed forms by SymPy; sample sizes by formula against a simulation of the test's power; AUC by pair counting against a sort-based implementation; gradients by central finite differences; step-size bounds by eigenvalues; entropy and KL by direct sums | `numeric`, `expression` | A one-sided critical value for a two-sided test; ties ignored in AUC; a missing relu mask |
| Coding rounds and C++ | TR4, TR5 | Hidden tests and known-wrong solutions (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 3.9); the order book against a brute-force reference on 10,000 random operations; C++ output items compiled and run in CI with GCC 13 and Clang 17 at `-O0` and `-O2`, and an item is valid only if every configuration prints the stated output | `code`, `predict-output` | Output that depends on an optimisation level or an implementation's growth factor (such items state the implementation) |
| Take-home and long-form items | TR6, every set and paper | Model-answer lines checked by SymPy where they are mathematics; every number recomputed; every rubric point names lines that exist (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 6.8); the take-home's planted defects listed in the generator and checked present | Self-marking against the rubric with Bertie's suggested mark and evidence quote (`AI-TUTOR.md` 2.7) | Rubric points no line evidences |
| Aloud screens, mocks and book-logs | Every 3.3 lesson, IV4, the books | Not machine-verifiable: the communication check and the mock rubric are marked by the learner with Bertie's suggestion and quotes; mocks with humans and book-log entries are self-declared and labelled so (`ASSESSMENT.md` decision 18) | `rubric`, `state` | A mark with no quote; a "cold" log entry for a problem seen before (the "recognised" outcome exists for it) |
| Originality of 3.3 items (decision 8) | All of 3.3 | CI cannot see the books. Each 3.3 item carries `origin: original` and a reviewer field; the owner signs off each level against his copies of Mosteller, Zhou, Crack and Joshi. Optionally, a local script compares item texts with any problem texts the owner loads on his own machine (never committed or shipped) and flags close matches | None | An item that is a book problem with new numbers |
| The papers | `exm.stoch.finals`, `exm.iv.paper`, `exm.iv.zhou-cold` | `ASSESSMENT.md` 3.2's rules on every form: equal marks, the 20/50/20/10 mix within 5 points, coverage of at least 80% of levels, at least 50% of marks from generators, reserve capacity for six fresh sittings; the cold set's section weights checked per form; the formula card checked entry by entry | Exam engine | A form that drifts off the mix |

**What verification cannot prove** (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 6.13): that a hook is engaging, that a trap catches its misconception, that a rubric rewards reasoning rather than vocabulary, that an original problem is truly absent from the books, or that a firm's test still runs the format the configuration assumes. The first three are judged by the owner's review of each level and by item statistics after release (a trap screen that fewer than 30% of learners fall into, a distractor nobody picks, or a rubric line on which Bertie's and the learner's marks disagree by 3 or more on average, is flagged for rewriting). The fourth rests on the owner's sign-off. The fifth is dated configuration (decision 26), reviewed when a firm's pages change.
