## 7. Sample lesson in full: "Quadratic variation: why $dW\,dW = dt$"

**Why this lesson.** Decision 21: every later lesson of 3.2 stands on it. The Itô integral differs from a Riemann sum because of it (lesson 52); Itô's formula keeps a second-order term because of it (57); the Black-Scholes-Merton equation has its $\frac12\sigma^2S^2c_{xx}$ term because of it (64); Girsanov's theorem changes drift but not volatility because quadratic variation is the same under equivalent measures (72); and volatility can be estimated from data while drift cannot because of it (43). It is also where learners first meet the creative step that confuses most of them: in ordinary calculus, squares of small changes are thrown away; here they must be kept. The lesson makes that step feel forced: the learner computes the sum for a smooth path, predicts it for a Brownian path, watches it refuse to vanish, derives its mean and variance, and only then names it.

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Id | `lsn.brownian.quadratic-variation` (course BR, level BR4, lesson 42 of 3.2) |
| Length | 14 screens (1 title, 1 explore, 1 summary, 11 tasks) and a quiz of 4 items; about 11 minutes of screens and 4 of quiz |
| Requires | Lesson 37 (the definition of Brownian motion and its independent Gaussian increments); lesson 41 (first variation: $E\sum\lvert\Delta W\rvert = \sqrt{\frac{2nT}\pi}$); 1.5 level V5 (the normal's moments, including $E[Z^4] = 3$ from the MGF); 1.5 level J2 (variances of sums of independent terms); 1.3 lesson on Taylor's theorem with remainder |
| Introduces | `skl.stoch.quadratic-variation`, `skl.stoch.qv-mean-variance`, `skl.stoch.ito-table`, `skl.stoch.realised-variance`; terms "quadratic variation" and the notation $[W, W](T)$, $dW\,dW = dt$ (embargoed for Bertie until screen 8) |
| Frame (why it exists) | Calculus works because the squares of small changes are negligible: a smooth path's squared increments add up to nothing as the steps shrink. Brownian paths are so rough that their squared increments add up to exactly the elapsed time, however fine the steps. Any calculus for random paths must keep that term, or every formula built on it is wrong |
| Insight (the aha line) | Each squared increment is random, but there are $n$ of them, each with mean $\frac Tn$ and variance $2\left(\frac Tn\right)^2$; their sum has mean $T$ and a variance that vanishes, so the randomness cancels and $(dW)^2$ behaves exactly like $dt$ |
| Misconceptions | `mc.stoch.sum-not-squares`, `mc.stoch.square-of-sum`, `mc.stoch.one-step-only`, `mc.stoch.smooth-squares-persist`, `mc.stoch.qv-vanishes-like-smooth` (the bait), `mc.stoch.qv-scale-of-one-step`, `mc.stoch.qv-grows-like-first-variation`, `mc.stoch.increment-mean-not-square`, `mc.stoch.sd-for-variance`, `mc.stoch.fourth-moment-not-variance`, `mc.stoch.variances-not-added`, `mc.stoch.drop-second-order`, `mc.stoch.half-qv`, `mc.stoch.dwdt-nonzero`, `mc.stoch.drift-changes-qv`, `mc.stoch.scale-not-squared`, `mc.stoch.time-change-ignored`, `mc.stoch.vol-no-square-root`, `mc.stoch.daily-not-annualised` |
| Worked example with a decision point | Screen 10: $W(T)^2$ by telescoping. The naive route drops $\sum(\Delta W_j)^2$ as ordinary calculus would and gets $W(T)^2 = 2\int_0^TW\,dW$, which fails a one-line check ($E[\int W\,dW] = 0$ but $E[W(T)^2] = T$); keeping the term gives $W(T)^2 = 2\int_0^TW\,dW + T$ |
| Trap and edge cases | Trap: screen 4 (bait: "the sum vanishes, as for the smooth path"). Edges: screen 12 (drift does not change quadratic variation; scaling multiplies it by the square; a time change multiplies it by the speed; smooth paths have none) |
| Transfer | Screen 13: estimating a share's volatility from a year of squared daily returns, whatever its drift |
| Sources | Shreve II §3.4 (quadratic variation) [check]; MIT 18.S096 Fall 2013 lecture 17, Stochastic Processes II [searched]; Baxter and Rennie chapter 3 [check] |
| Lesson-wide tutor guidance | Do not say "quadratic variation", "$dt$" or "$[W, W]$" before screen 8's Check; never state "about 1" or "$T$" for the Brownian sum before screen 4's Check; prefer "roughly how big is one increment over a step of $\frac1{1000}$?", "what is the variance of one squared increment?" and "do the variances of independent terms add?" to statements; if the learner asks why this matters, point to screen 10 without giving its answer |

Throughout, the Brownian path is one seeded path built by bridge refinement (calculus catalogue decision 9), so refining the partition never changes the points already drawn; the path is drawn in ink, the partition points in `--manip`, and the running sums in a two-row readout with text labels ("sum of squared steps", "sum of absolute steps"), so nothing depends on colour (design research 11).

---

**Screen 1. Title** (`title`)

- **Heading:** A sum that refuses to vanish
- **Prose:** "Ordinary calculus throws away the squares of small changes. Brownian paths do not let you."
- **Art:** Bertie holding a ruler against a jagged path, his moustache bristling at the corners.
- **Action:** Continue.

---

**Screen 2. The hook** (`problem`, id `smooth`)

- **Prose:** "A price rises steadily along the straight line $f(t) = 3t$ over one year. Cut the year into 4 equal steps and add up the squares of the price's changes over the steps."
- **Widget:** `quadratic-variation` in smooth mode: the path $f(t) = 3t$ on $[0, 1]$, a partition slider fixed at 4, and a table of the four steps with columns "change" and "change squared"; the learner fills the second column and the sum.
- **Question:** "What is $\sum_j(\Delta f_j)^2$ with 4 steps?"
- **Answer input:** `numeric-input` (fractions accepted).
- **Correct:** $\frac94$.

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| 3 | `mc.stoch.sum-not-squares` | "That is the sum of the changes, the total rise. Square each change before adding." |
| 9 | `mc.stoch.square-of-sum` | "That is the square of the total change. Square each step's change, then add the four squares." |
| $\frac9{16}$ | `mc.stoch.one-step-only` | "That is one step's square. There are four steps." |
| anything else | (otherwise) | "Each step rises by $\frac34$. Square that and add four of them." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "How much does the price rise in one quarter of a year?" Point: highlights the first row of the table. Step: "Each change is $\frac34$, its square $\frac9{16}$, and four of them make $\frac94$."
- **Why?** Yours: "$4 \times \left(\frac34\right)^2 = \frac94$." Then: "Keep an eye on what happens to this sum when the steps get smaller."
- **Tutor.** Focus: the difference between the sum of squares and the square of the sum. Notes: the commonest slip is 9. Avoid: any mention of Brownian motion.

---

**Screen 3. Finer steps** (`ladder`, id `smooth-limit`, constraint "Use 1,000 steps")

- **Widget:** the same, with the partition slider now free; the table collapses to its first three rows and a running sum.
- **Question:** "With 1,000 equal steps, what is $\sum_j(\Delta f_j)^2$? And what happens to it as the number of steps grows without limit?"
- **Answer input:** `numeric-input` for the sum; `choice` for the limit (grows without limit; settles at a positive number; tends to 0).
- **Correct:** 0.009; tends to 0.

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| 9 | `mc.stoch.square-of-sum` | "Each of the 1,000 steps rises by $\frac3{1000}$. Square that, then multiply by 1,000." |
| 0.003 or 3 | `mc.stoch.sum-not-squares` | "Square each change first: $\left(\frac3{1000}\right)^2$." |
| "settles at a positive number" | `mc.stoch.smooth-squares-persist` | "With $n$ steps the sum is $n\left(\frac3n\right)^2 = \frac9n$. What does $\frac9n$ do as $n$ grows?" |
| anything else | (otherwise) | "With $n$ steps each change is $\frac3n$; the sum is $\frac9n$." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "Write the sum for $n$ steps." Point: highlights the running sum as the slider moves. Step: "$n \times \left(\frac3n\right)^2 = \frac9n$, which is 0.009 at $n = 1{,}000$ and tends to 0."
- **Why?** Yours: "The sum is $\frac9n$, so it vanishes." Then: "This is why ordinary calculus can drop squared changes: for any smooth path, each change is about the slope times the step, so the squares add up to about (slope squared) × (total time) × (step), which goes to 0 with the step."
- **Tutor.** Focus: why squared changes are negligible for smooth paths. Notes: a learner who sees the general $\frac9n$ at once is ready for screen 4. Avoid: "quadratic variation".

---

**Screen 4. A Brownian path** (`trap`, id `predict-brownian`, trap `mc.stoch.qv-vanishes-like-smooth`, bait option A)

- **Prose:** "Now Bertie's Brownian path on the same year, cut into 1,000 equal steps. Predict the sum of its squared changes before the widget shows it."
- **Widget:** `quadratic-variation` in Brownian mode, prediction first: the path is drawn; the readout is hidden until the learner commits.
- **Question:** "The sum of squared changes over the 1,000 steps is closest to:" (`choice`)

| Option | Correct? | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|---|
| A. 0: with fine steps the squares vanish, as for the straight line (the bait) | no | `mc.stoch.qv-vanishes-like-smooth` | "That was true because each smooth step was about $\frac3n$. How big is a Brownian step over $\frac1{1000}$ of a year? Its variance is $\frac1{1000}$, so its typical size is about $\frac1{\sqrt{1000}}$, much larger than $\frac1{1000}$." |
| B. 0.03 | no | `mc.stoch.qv-scale-of-one-step` | "0.03 is about the size of one step, $\frac1{\sqrt{1000}}$. Square it and add 1,000 of them." |
| C. 1 | yes | | "Yes. Each squared step is about $\frac1{1000}$, and there are 1,000 of them. Unlike the straight line, this sum does not shrink as the steps do." |
| D. 31.6 | no | `mc.stoch.qv-grows-like-first-variation` | "31.6 is $\sqrt{1000}$, about the size of the sum of absolute steps from lesson 41 (25.2). Squaring each step makes it smaller, not larger." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "What is the variance of one Brownian step over a time $\frac1{1000}$?" Point: highlights one step of the path with its length marked. Step: "Each step is $\mathcal N(0, \frac1{1000})$, so its square is about $\frac1{1000}$ on average; 1,000 of them add to about 1."
- **Why?** Shown: "A Brownian step over time $\Delta t$ has size about $\sqrt{\Delta t}$, not $\Delta t$. Its square is about $\Delta t$, and the $n$ squares add to about $n\Delta t = T$." Then: "The readout now shows this path's sum, close to 1 (two standard deviations at 1,000 steps are about 0.09). The next screen lets you refine the steps and watch."
- **Tutor.** Notes: most learners choose A; the screen exists to be failed once. Avoid: "quadratic variation", "$dt$" and "$T$" before Check.

---

**Screen 5. Refine and watch** (`explore`, id `refine`, `continueWhen`: the slider has visited at least three values including one of at least 1,000)

- **Prose:** "Drag the number of steps from 10 to 10,000. The path never changes; only the cuts do. Watch both sums."
- **Widget:** `quadratic-variation` with the partition slider on a log scale (10, 100, 1,000, 10,000), the path refined by bridge midpoints, and the two-row readout: "sum of absolute steps" and "sum of squared steps". For orientation the readout shows each sum's expected value beside it in caption type ($\sqrt{\frac{2n}\pi}$: 2.52, 7.98, 25.2, 79.8; and 1), and a band of one standard deviation either side for the squared sum ($\sqrt{\frac2n}$: 0.45, 0.14, 0.045, 0.014). Seed fixed; CI checks that the path's displayed sums lie within 4 standard deviations of their expectations at every slider value (the sum of absolute steps has standard deviation $\sqrt{1 - \frac2\pi} \approx 0.60$ whatever $n$).
- **No question.** Continue appears when the condition holds.
- **Tutor.** Focus: the two sums moving in opposite directions. Notes: learners often ask why the squared sum wobbles at 10 steps and is steady at 10,000; say the next two screens answer exactly that.

---

**Screen 6. The average of the sum** (`ladder`, id `mean`, constraint "Compute it, do not watch it")

- **Prose:** "Split $[0, T]$ into $n$ equal steps of length $\Delta t = \frac Tn$. Each step's change $\Delta W_j$ is normal with mean 0 and variance $\Delta t$."
- **Widget:** `derivation-steps` in fill mode:
  1. $E[(\Delta W_j)^2] = $ **[slot A]** (the variance of a mean-zero variable is its mean square).
  2. $E\left[\sum_{j=1}^n(\Delta W_j)^2\right] = n \times$ **[slot A]** $= $ **[slot B]**.
- **Question:** "Fill slots A and B."
- **Answer input:** two `expression-input` cells, in $\Delta t$, $T$ and $n$.
- **Correct:** A: $\Delta t$ (or $\frac Tn$); B: $T$.

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| A: 0 | `mc.stoch.increment-mean-not-square` | "The mean of $\Delta W_j$ is 0, but its square is never negative. The mean of the square is the variance." |
| A: $\sqrt{\Delta t}$ | `mc.stoch.sd-for-variance` | "$\sqrt{\Delta t}$ is the standard deviation, the typical size of the step. Its square's mean is the variance." |
| A: $\Delta t^2$ | `mc.stoch.qv-vanishes-like-smooth` | "That is the square of a smooth step of size $\Delta t$. A Brownian step has variance $\Delta t$ itself." |
| B: $n\Delta t^2$ or $\frac{T^2}n$ | `mc.stoch.qv-vanishes-like-smooth` | "Use slot A as you wrote it, $\Delta t$, times $n$ steps." |
| anything else | (otherwise) | "$E[(\Delta W_j)^2] = \mathrm{Var}(\Delta W_j) = \Delta t$, and $n\Delta t = T$." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "For a variable with mean 0, what is $E[X^2]$?" Point: highlights line 1's margin. Step: "$E[(\Delta W_j)^2] = \Delta t$; summing $n$ of them gives $n\Delta t = T$."
- **Why?** Yours: "On average the sum is exactly $T$, for every $n$." Then: "That explains why the readout hovered near 1 at every number of steps. It does not yet explain why the wobble shrank. For that we need the sum's variance."
- **Tutor.** Focus: mean of the square equals the variance. Avoid: "quadratic variation".

---

**Screen 7. Why the wobble shrinks** (`ladder`, id `variance`, constraint "Now the spread of the sum")

- **Prose:** "For a standard normal $Z$, $E[Z^4] = 3$ (1.5, from the MGF). Each $\Delta W_j$ is $\sqrt{\Delta t}\,Z_j$ with independent $Z_j$."
- **Widget:** `derivation-steps` in fill mode:
  1. $E[(\Delta W_j)^4] = \Delta t^2E[Z^4] = 3\Delta t^2$.
  2. $\mathrm{Var}((\Delta W_j)^2) = E[(\Delta W_j)^4] - (E[(\Delta W_j)^2])^2 = $ **[slot A]**.
  3. The steps are independent, so $\mathrm{Var}\left(\sum_j(\Delta W_j)^2\right) = n \times$ **[slot A]** $= $ **[slot B]** in terms of $T$ and $n$.
- **Question:** "Fill slots A and B."
- **Answer input:** two `expression-input` cells.
- **Correct:** A: $2\Delta t^2$; B: $\frac{2T^2}n$.

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| A: $3\Delta t^2$ | `mc.stoch.fourth-moment-not-variance` | "$3\Delta t^2$ is the fourth moment. The variance subtracts the square of the mean, $\Delta t^2$." |
| A: $2\Delta t$ | `mc.stoch.scale-not-squared` | "The square of a step scales like $\Delta t$, so its variance scales like $\Delta t^2$." |
| B: $\frac{2T^2}{n^2}$ | `mc.stoch.variances-not-added` | "Independent terms' variances add: $n$ copies of $2\Delta t^2 = 2\frac{T^2}{n^2}$ make $\frac{2T^2}n$." |
| B: $2T^2$ | `mc.stoch.variances-not-added` | "That forgets that each term's variance carries $\frac1{n^2}$." |
| anything else | (otherwise) | "$3\Delta t^2 - \Delta t^2 = 2\Delta t^2$; times $n$ with $\Delta t = \frac Tn$ gives $\frac{2T^2}n$." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "Variance is the mean of the square minus the square of the mean. Here the 'square' is a fourth power." Point: highlights line 2. Step: "$3\Delta t^2 - \Delta t^2 = 2\Delta t^2$, and $n \cdot 2\left(\frac Tn\right)^2 = \frac{2T^2}n$."
- **Why?** Yours: "The sum has mean $T$ and variance $\frac{2T^2}n$, which tends to 0." Then: "So the sum of squared steps does not just average $T$: it becomes $T$, with less and less randomness. At 10,000 steps over a year its standard deviation is about 0.014, the narrow band you saw."
- **Tutor.** Focus: independence makes variances add, and the $\frac1{n^2}$ in each term beats the $n$ terms. Notes: learners who reach $\frac{2T^2}n$ often ask what "becomes $T$" means precisely; screen 8 states it.

---

**Screen 8. The name** (`generalise`, id `name`)

- **Prose:** "You have shown that $\sum_j(\Delta W_j)^2$ has mean $T$ and variance $\frac{2T^2}n$, so as the steps shrink it converges to $T$ (in mean square; with more work, almost surely along refining partitions). This limit is the **quadratic variation** of Brownian motion up to time $T$, written $[W, W](T)$. It is the same for every path, although each path is random."
- **Rule (displayed):** $[W, W](T) = \lim\limits_{n \to \infty}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\left(W(t_{j+1}) - W(t_j)\right)^2 = T$, which we write as $dW\,dW = dt$: over any short interval, the squared change of $W$ adds up exactly like elapsed time.
- **Widget:** `quadratic-variation` showing the running sum $\sum_{t_j \le t}(\Delta W_j)^2$ against $t$ for the seeded path at 10,000 steps: a line indistinguishable from the diagonal $y = t$ (concreteness fades: the path itself shrinks to a thumbnail).
- **Question:** "How much quadratic variation does $W$ accumulate between $t = 2$ and $t = 5$?"
- **Answer input:** `numeric-input`.
- **Correct:** 3.

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| 5 | `mc.stoch.time-change-ignored` | "That counts from 0. Quadratic variation accumulates at rate 1 per unit of time, so count only the time between 2 and 5." |
| 0 | `mc.stoch.qv-vanishes-like-smooth` | "The running sum in the widget rises like $t$ itself. It never stops growing." |
| $\sqrt3$ | `mc.stoch.sd-for-variance` | "$\sqrt3$ is the standard deviation of $W(5) - W(2)$. Quadratic variation adds squares, so it grows like time, not its root." |
| anything else | (otherwise) | "$[W, W]$ grows by the elapsed time: $5 - 2 = 3$." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "At what rate does the running sum rise?" Point: highlights the diagonal between 2 and 5. Step: "It rises at rate 1, so between 2 and 5 it gains 3."
- **Why?** Shown: "The running sum is the straight line $t$: the roughness of Brownian motion is perfectly regular." Then: "For a path with smooth parts only, this line would be flat at 0."
- **Tutor.** Focus: quadratic variation as a pathwise quantity equal to elapsed time. The embargo on "quadratic variation", "$dt$" and "$[W, W]$" lifts here.

---

**Screen 9. The multiplication table** (`problem`, id `table`)

- **Prose:** "Calculus with Brownian motion needs to know which products of small changes survive when summed. Fill the table: for each product, does its sum over a fine partition tend to $T$ (write $dt$) or to 0?"
- **Widget:** `derivation-steps` in grid layout: a $2 \times 2$ table with rows and columns $dW$ and $dt$; three cells to fill ($dW \cdot dW$, $dW \cdot dt$, $dt \cdot dt$) from the tokens $dt$ and $0$ in a `token-tray`; each filled cell offers a one-line "because" from three choices.
- **Question:** "Fill each cell, and choose the reason for $dW \cdot dt$."
- **Answer input:** three cells and one reason.
- **Correct:** $dW \cdot dW = dt$; $dW \cdot dt = 0$; $dt \cdot dt = 0$; reason for $dW \cdot dt$: "each term is about $\sqrt{\Delta t} \cdot \Delta t$, so $n$ of them add to about $T\sqrt{\Delta t}$, which tends to 0".

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| $dW \cdot dW = 0$ | `mc.stoch.qv-vanishes-like-smooth` | "Screens 6 to 8: the squared steps add up to $T$." |
| $dW \cdot dt = dt$ | `mc.stoch.dwdt-nonzero` | "$\lvert\Delta W\rvert$ is about $\sqrt{\Delta t}$, so $\lvert\Delta W\rvert\Delta t$ is about $\Delta t^{3/2}$; $n$ of them add to about $T\sqrt{\Delta t}$, which vanishes." |
| Reason "because $\Delta W$ has mean 0" for $dW \cdot dt$ | `mc.stoch.increment-mean-not-square` | "Mean 0 shows the expected sum is 0, but not that the sum itself vanishes; the size argument does." |
| anything else | (otherwise) | "Compare the size of each term with $\Delta t$: $\Delta t$ itself survives, anything smaller vanishes." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "Roughly how big is $\lvert\Delta W\rvert$ compared with $\Delta t$?" Point: highlights the $dW \cdot dt$ cell. Step: "$\lvert\Delta W\rvert \approx \sqrt{\Delta t}$. Products of size $\Delta t$ survive when summed $n$ times ($dW \cdot dW$); products of size $\Delta t^{3/2}$ or $\Delta t^2$ vanish."
- **Why?** Yours: "$dW\,dW = dt$, $dW\,dt = 0$, $dt\,dt = 0$." Then: "This table is the whole of the difference between Itô calculus and ordinary calculus. Ordinary calculus has a table too, with $dt \cdot dt = 0$; Brownian motion adds one non-zero entry."
- **Tutor.** Focus: orders of magnitude, $\Delta t^{1/2}$, $\Delta t$, $\Delta t^{3/2}$. Notes: if the learner asks about $(\Delta W)^3$, say homework BR4 shows its sum vanishes too.

---

**Screen 10. Keeping the term** (`derive`, id `square-of-w`; the lesson's worked example with a decision point)

- **Leap.** Naive: expand $W(T)^2$ as a telescoping sum and drop the squared-increment term, as ordinary calculus drops $(\Delta f)^2$, getting $W(T)^2 = 2\int_0^TW\,dW$. Fails: taking expectations, the left side is $T$ while the right side is 0 (an integral of steps taken with the information of their start has mean 0, lesson 53), so the naive formula is wrong by exactly $T$. Points to: the term ordinary calculus discards is the one this lesson found does not vanish; keep it, and replace $\sum(\Delta W_j)^2$ by its limit $T$.
- **Prose:** "Use the algebra $b^2 - a^2 = 2a(b - a) + (b - a)^2$ on every step, add up, and decide what each sum becomes."
- **Widget:** `derivation-steps` in fill mode, each line with its reason in the margin:
  1. $W(T)^2 - W(0)^2 = \sum_j\left[W(t_{j+1})^2 - W(t_j)^2\right]$ (telescoping).
  2. $= \sum_j2W(t_j)\Delta W_j + \sum_j(\Delta W_j)^2$ (the algebra, with $a = W(t_j)$, $b = W(t_{j+1})$).
  3. As the steps shrink, $\sum_j2W(t_j)\Delta W_j \to 2\int_0^TW\,dW$ (left-point sums; lesson 52 builds this integral).
  4. As the steps shrink, $\sum_j(\Delta W_j)^2 \to$ **[slot A]**.
  5. So $W(T)^2 = 2\int_0^TW(t)\,dW(t) +$ **[slot A]**.
  6. Check: $E[W(T)^2] = 0 +$ **[slot A]**, which must equal $T$.
- **Question:** "Fill slot A, the same in lines 4 to 6."
- **Answer input:** `expression-input`.
- **Correct:** $T$.

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | `mc.stoch.drop-second-order` | "That is the naive route: line 6 would then say $E[W(T)^2] = 0$, but $W(T)$ has variance $T$. The squared steps add to $T$ (screen 8)." |
| $\frac T2$ | `mc.stoch.half-qv` | "There is no $\frac12$ in front of $\sum(\Delta W_j)^2$ in line 2; the $\frac12$ appears only when you solve for the integral: $\int W\,dW = \frac12(W(T)^2 - T)$." |
| $2T$ | `mc.stoch.half-qv` | "The 2 belongs to the first sum only. The second sum is the plain sum of squared steps." |
| anything else | (otherwise) | "Line 4 is the quadratic variation of screen 8." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "Line 4 is a sum you met on screens 4 to 8. What does it converge to?" Point: highlights line 4. Step: "$\sum(\Delta W_j)^2 \to T$, so $W(T)^2 = 2\int_0^TW\,dW + T$; the check gives $E[W(T)^2] = T$, as it must."
- **Why?** Yours: "$W(T)^2 = 2\int_0^TW\,dW + T$, so $\int_0^TW\,dW = \frac12\left(W(T)^2 - T\right)$." Then: "Notice what just happened: the term that ordinary calculus throws away contributed exactly $T$. In differential form, $d(W^2) = 2W\,dW + dt$: the first Itô formula, and the $dt$ is $dW\,dW$."
- **Tutor.** Notes: the decision in line 4 is the lesson's point; if the learner hesitates, ask what $\sum(\Delta f_j)^2$ did for the straight line and what it did for Bertie's path. Avoid: "Itô's formula" before Check.

---

**Screen 11. The other sums** (`ladder`, id `ito-preview`, constraint "Apply the table once more")

- **Prose:** "Using the table, compute $d(W(t)^3)$ from a second-order expansion: $d(W^3) = 3W^2\,dW + \frac12 \cdot 6W\,(dW)^2$."
- **Widget:** `ito-lemma-lab` showing the average of $W(t)^3$ over 2,000 seeded paths (flat at 0) and of $W(t)^3 - 3\int_0^tW(s)\,ds$ (also flat), with an `expression-input` for the $dt$ term.
- **Question:** "Replace $(dW)^2$ by the table's entry. What is the $dt$ term of $d(W^3)$?"
- **Answer input:** `expression-input` in $W$ and $dt$.
- **Correct:** $3W\,dt$.

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | `mc.stoch.drop-second-order` | "$(dW)^2$ is $dt$, not 0." |
| $6W\,dt$ | `mc.stoch.half-qv` | "Keep the $\frac12$ from Taylor's second-order term: $\frac12 \cdot 6W = 3W$." |
| $3W^2\,dt$ | slip | "The second derivative of $W^3$ is $6W$, not $6W^2$." |
| anything else | (otherwise) | "$\frac12f''(W)\,(dW)^2$ with $f''(W) = 6W$ and $(dW)^2 = dt$." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "What does the table say $(dW)^2$ becomes?" Point: highlights $(dW)^2$. Step: "$\frac12 \cdot 6W \cdot dt = 3W\,dt$."
- **Why?** Yours: "$d(W^3) = 3W^2\,dW + 3W\,dt$." Then: "So $W^3$ is not a martingale given its past (lesson 44), but $W^3 - 3\int_0^tW\,ds$ is; lesson 57 turns this into Itô's formula for any smooth $f$."
- **Tutor.** Focus: the table used mechanically for the first time. Notes: this screen previews lesson 57; do not derive the general formula here.

---

**Screen 12. Which processes have this roughness?** (`problem`, id `other-processes`; the edge cases)

- **Prose:** "Quadratic variation measures roughness, not position or trend. Decide which of these have quadratic variation exactly $T$ over $[0, T]$."
- **Widget:** `choice` in multi-select mode, five cards, each with a small path picture drawn from the same seed; after Check each card shows its own running sum.
- **Question:** "Select every process with $[X, X](T) = T$."
- **Correct:** (a) and (d).

| Option | Correct? | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|---|
| (a) $X(t) = W(t) + 5t$ | yes | | "A drift is smooth: it adds only $dt$ terms, whose squares and cross products vanish." |
| (b) $X(t) = 2W(t)$ | no | `mc.stoch.scale-not-squared` | "Each step doubles, so each squared step quadruples: $[X, X](T) = 4T$." |
| (c) $X(t) = W(2t)$ | no | `mc.stoch.time-change-ignored` | "Running the clock twice as fast packs twice the time into $[0, T]$: $[X, X](T) = 2T$." |
| (d) $X(t) = -W(t)$ | yes | | "Squaring removes the sign." |
| (e) $X(t) = \sin t$ | no | `mc.stoch.drift-changes-qv` | "A smooth path has quadratic variation 0 (screen 3)." |
| Missing (a) | | `mc.stoch.drift-changes-qv` | "A trend changes where the path goes, not how rough it is." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "For each, what is one small step, and what is its square?" Point: highlights card (b). Step: "(a) $\Delta W + 5\Delta t$, whose square sums to $T$; (b) $2\Delta W$, square $4(\Delta W)^2$; (c) a step of $W$ over $2\Delta t$, square about $2\Delta t$; (d) $-\Delta W$; (e) smooth."
- **Why?** Shown: "$[aW + \text{drift}, aW + \text{drift}](T) = a^2T$, and a time change by a factor $c$ multiplies it by $c$." Then: "This is why changing probability measure (lesson 72), which can add a drift, leaves volatility untouched."
- **Tutor.** Focus: what changes quadratic variation and what does not. Notes: (a) is the one most learners miss.

---

**Screen 13. Transfer: volatility from squared returns** (`problem`, id `realised`, context quant)

- **Prose:** "A share's log price moves like $(\mu - \frac12\sigma^2)t + \sigma W(t)$. Over a year of 252 trading days, its 252 squared daily log returns add up to 0.0628."
- **Widget:** `quadratic-variation` in realised mode: two simulated shares driven by the same noise, with $\sigma = 25\%$ and drifts $+30\%$ and $-10\%$ a year, their daily log returns as bars and their running sums of squares, which end within about 0.001 of each other (the expectations are $\sigma^2 + \frac{(\mu - \sigma^2/2)^2}{252}$, about 0.06279 and 0.06257; one year's sum has a standard deviation of about 0.006 around them).
- **Question:** "Estimate the share's annual volatility $\sigma$."
- **Answer input:** `numeric-input` (percent).
- **Correct:** about 25.1% ($\sqrt{0.0628}$); accepted 24.5% to 25.5%.

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| 6.3% | `mc.stoch.vol-no-square-root` | "0.0628 is the quadratic variation of the log price, $\sigma^2T$. Volatility is its square root." |
| 1.6% | `mc.stoch.daily-not-annualised` | "That is roughly a daily volatility. The sum over a whole year already gives $\sigma^2$ for one year." |
| 0.25% | slip | "Check the units: $\sqrt{0.0628} \approx 0.25$, that is 25%." |
| anything else | (otherwise) | "$[\ln S, \ln S](1) = \sigma^2 \times 1$, so $\sigma \approx \sqrt{0.0628}$." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "What is the quadratic variation of $\sigma W$ over one year?" Point: highlights the running sum's end value. Step: "$\sigma^2 = 0.0628$, so $\sigma \approx 0.251$."
- **Why?** Yours: "$\sigma \approx 25.1\%$." Then: "The two shares in the widget, driven by the same noise, drift in opposite directions, yet their sums of squares differ only in the third decimal place: on average the drift contributes $\frac{(\mu - \sigma^2/2)^2}{252}$, a few hundredths of a percent. This is lesson 43's point in one picture: a year of data pins down volatility and says almost nothing about drift."
- **Tutor.** Focus: realised variance as quadratic variation. Notes: learners who ask about intraday data can be told lesson 43 covers sampling frequency and noise.

---

**Screen 14. Summary** (`summary`)

- **Three ideas.**
  1. For a smooth path, squared changes add up to nothing as the steps shrink. For Brownian motion, $\sum(\Delta W_j)^2$ has mean $T$ and variance $\frac{2T^2}n$, so it converges to $T$: $[W, W](T) = T$.
  2. The multiplication table $dW\,dW = dt$, $dW\,dt = 0$, $dt\,dt = 0$ says which products of small changes survive; it is the one difference between Itô calculus and ordinary calculus.
  3. Keeping the surviving term gives $W(T)^2 = 2\int_0^TW\,dW + T$, the first Itô formula; drift does not change quadratic variation, scaling by $a$ multiplies it by $a^2$, and the quadratic variation of a log price is $\sigma^2T$, which is how volatility is read from data.
- **Formula card (saved to the Notebook).** $[W, W](T) = T$; $E\sum(\Delta W_j)^2 = T$, $\mathrm{Var}\sum(\Delta W_j)^2 = \frac{2T^2}n$; $dW\,dW = dt$, $dW\,dt = dt\,dt = 0$; $\int_0^TW\,dW = \frac12(W(T)^2 - T)$; $[\sigma W + \text{drift}](T) = \sigma^2T$.
- **Common trap.** "Squares of small changes vanish": true for smooth paths, false for Brownian ones, where each squared step is the size of the time step itself.

---

**Quiz** (4 items, difficulty 2 to 3, one attempt each, no hints; "Why?" after each)

| # | Item | Type | Difficulty | Correct | Diagnosed wrong answers | Why? |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Q1 | $[0, 3]$ is cut into 300 equal steps. What is $E\left[\sum_j(\Delta W_j)^2\right]$? | N | 2 | 3 | 0 (`mc.stoch.qv-vanishes-like-smooth`); 0.01 (`mc.stoch.one-step-only`: one step's mean); 0.03 (`mc.stoch.qv-vanishes-like-smooth`: $300 \times 0.01^2$, steps treated as smooth) | "Each squared step has mean $\Delta t = 0.01$; 300 of them make 3, the length of the interval." |
| Q2 | For the same partition, what is $\mathrm{Var}\left(\sum_j(\Delta W_j)^2\right)$? | N | 3 | 0.06 | 0.09 (`mc.stoch.fourth-moment-not-variance`: $300 \times 3 \times 0.01^2$); 0.0002 (`mc.stoch.variances-not-added`: one term); 6 (`mc.stoch.scale-not-squared`: $2\Delta t$ per term, so $2T$) | "Each term has variance $2\Delta t^2 = 0.0002$; 300 independent terms give 0.06, that is $\frac{2T^2}n = \frac{18}{300}$." |
| Q3 | Select every process with quadratic variation $4T$ over $[0, T]$ | M | 3 | $2W(t) + t$; $W(4t)$ | $4W(t)$ (`mc.stoch.scale-not-squared`: it has $16T$); $2W(\frac t4)$ (`mc.stoch.time-change-ignored`: it has $4 \cdot \frac T4 = T$) | "Scaling by $a$ multiplies quadratic variation by $a^2$; running the clock $c$ times as fast multiplies it by $c$; drift adds nothing." |
| Q4 | Match each sum over $n$ equal steps of $[0, 1]$ to its limit as $n \to \infty$: $\sum(\Delta W_j)^2$; $\sum\Delta W_j\,\Delta t$; $\sum(\Delta t)^2$; $\sum\lvert\Delta W_j\rvert$ | S | 3 | 1; 0; 0; $\infty$ | $\sum\lvert\Delta W_j\rvert \to 1$ (`mc.stoch.qv-grows-like-first-variation` reversed); $\sum\Delta W_j\Delta t \to 1$ (`mc.stoch.dwdt-nonzero`); $\sum(\Delta W_j)^2 \to 0$ (`mc.stoch.qv-vanishes-like-smooth`) | "Terms of size $\Delta t$ add to a finite limit; smaller terms vanish; terms of size $\sqrt{\Delta t}$, like $\lvert\Delta W\rvert$, add to infinity (lesson 41)." |

The quiz creates the FSRS cards for `skl.stoch.quadratic-variation`, `skl.stoch.qv-mean-variance` and `skl.stoch.ito-table` (`ASSESSMENT.md` 5.3); 3 of 4 or better on the first completion marks the lesson Mastered, and any wrong item joins the Redo queue as a fresh generated variant at the same level (`gen.stoch.qv-moments` with $T$ and $n$ as parameters, `gen.stoch.qv-of-process` over scalings, drifts and time changes, `gen.stoch.small-sums`).

**Verification of this lesson's numbers** (section 6): the smooth sums $\frac94$ and $\frac9{1{,}000}$ exactly; $E[(\Delta W)^2] = \Delta t$ and $\mathrm{Var}((\Delta W)^2) = 2\Delta t^2$ by SymPy from the normal's moments; the expectations shown on screen 5, $\sqrt{\frac{2n}\pi}$ at $n = 10, 100, 1{,}000, 10{,}000$ (2.52, 7.98, 25.2, 79.8) and the bands $\sqrt{\frac2n}$ (0.45, 0.14, 0.045, 0.014); the seeded path's displayed sums within 4 standard deviations at every slider value; $W(T)^2 = 2\int W\,dW + T$ checked on 2,000 seeded paths by left sums at 10,000 steps (mean residual within 4 standard errors of 0); the realised sums' expectations 0.06279 and 0.06257; $\sqrt{0.0628} \approx 0.2506$; Q1 to Q4 recomputed (3; 0.06; $4T$ for $2W + t$ and $W(4t)$, $16T$ and $T$ for the distractors).

**What this lesson asks of the widget library.** `quadratic-variation` in three modes, smooth (a deterministic $f$ with the step table), Brownian (prediction first, log-scale partition slider, both sums with expectation captions and bands) and realised (simulated daily log returns of two GBMs with a shared seed for the noise) [the realised mode is a mode to add]; bridge refinement so the path is fixed under refinement (calculus catalogue decision 9); `derivation-steps` grid layout with tokens for screen 9; `ito-lemma-lab` with a user-entered $dt$ term for screen 11.

**Answers recomputed** (Python 3.11.15, NumPy 2.4.6, SciPy 1.17.1, SymPy 1.14; scripts in the session scratchpad, not committed): every numeric hook in section 2; every number in the 48 homework sets' drill tests, known-wrong claims and examples A and B; the worked examples of the three papers; every number in section 7. Methods: exact `fractions` enumeration of all paths for the Shreve trees (the lookback 1.376 and $\Delta_0 = \frac{13}{75}$; American puts $\frac{34}{25}$ and $\frac{174}{125}$; the Asian $\frac{608}{375}$; state prices and log-optimal wealth); exact sums for short-rate trees; SciPy `quad` for the killed density (up-and-out 1.17607), the maximum of drifted Brownian motion, the lookback put (14.2906, matching Hull's formula and a 2-million-path simulation with exact bridge maxima, 14.2924 ± 0.0025) and the importance-sampling second moment; seeded simulations for the barrier (1.1752 ± 0.0071), the Asian (arithmetic 5.78; geometric control variate with a 1,333-fold variance reduction), Merton's model (11.638 ± 0.017 against the series 11.6617) and the one-touch (0.4132 ± 0.0008 against 0.4127); SymPy for the Vasicek bond, the square-centre area $\frac{4\sqrt2 - 5}3$, the three-in-a-row chain (43) and the pattern race ($\frac5{12}$); brute force for the PIN count (842,400), the empty-box variance ($\frac{36{,}611{,}855}{60{,}466{,}176}$), the same-rank variance ($\frac{48}{17}$), the naive shuffle's counts (5 and 4 of 27; 8 to 15 of 256), the reachable final numbers of the board puzzle ($\{1, 3, 5, 7, 9\}$) and the Figgie posterior; `linprog` for the linked-market arbitrage (0.5, the only one; superseded by the second pass below, which found a second arbitrage); GCC 13.3 for the copy and move output, with and without `noexcept`.

**Changes made after recomputation.**

1. Lesson 17's hook: the 3-period American put is exercised only after a first T; the price-1 node is never reached alive (draft said it was exercised there too).
2. Lesson 40: the planned follow-up (the variance of same-rank neighbours) gives exactly the binomial value $\frac{48}{17}$, because the pair covariances cancel, so it cannot illustrate the binomial trap; the lesson now uses empty boxes (variance 0.606 against the binomial 1.34) and keeps the deck case as the edge. The colour-change count of lesson 8 also turned out to have the binomial-form variance ($\frac{20}9$); not used as a trap.
3. Lesson 17's edge: 4 aces in an 8-card deck give Ann $\frac{23}{35}$, not the drafted $\frac{43}{70}$; homework PZ3's count is 140,725, not the drafted 140,296.
4. Lesson 56's edge: an exhaustive search confirmed every odd number from 1 to 9 is reachable; the drafted claim was unverified.
5. Lesson 66's trap: ignoring the hour hand's motion gives 150°, not 140°.
6. Lesson 81's edge: with several simultaneous bets the total stake must stay below 100% (10 bets: 10.0% each); the drafted claim that stakes approach the single-bet fraction was wrong.
7. Homework AT1's known-wrong value is $\frac{43}{20}$, not $\frac{1376}{625}$; homework RN1's known-wrong is about 15.5, not 14.2; homework RN3's $A(1)$ is 0.0085108; TR1's known-wrong is about 7,200; MG4's 10-spade test is $\frac{33}{34}$, not above 0.99.
8. Lesson 78's answer: staking all savings on a 1% chance is valued at minus infinity by log utility, and the Kelly stake is about 0.5% of wealth.
9. The Stochastic Lab's importance-sampling target: the variance ratio for the strike-160 digital is about 28, not the drafted 50; the test now asks for more than 20.
10. Quiz types in ten RN, TR and EX lessons were changed from choice to order, sort or expression items, so that every course is at or below 40% choice (RN was 40.3%, TR 40.9%, EX exactly 40%).

Exact enumeration agreed with every tree, card, dice, chain and counting answer; closed forms agreed with quadrature or seeded simulation (lookback 14.2906, arithmetic Asian 5.78 with a variance ratio of about 1,315, Merton 11.6617 with implied volatilities 24.40%, 23.22%, 22.63%, the strike-160 importance-sampling ratio 27.9). No distractor equals its answer. Choice items per course recounted from the tables: they match section 1.0.4. **Fixed.**

1. Homework MG4 example B: the quotes hold two arbitrages, not one. Buying $X + Y$ at 14.5 and selling $X - Y$ at 7.5 makes $2Y$ at 3.5 a unit, below $Y$'s bid of 4 (a riskless 1.0); a linear programme confirms these two are the only extreme arbitrages. The first log's "the only one" is marked superseded.
2. Homework RN3 example B: the exponent used $A = 0.0084786$; it is 0.0085108, which gives the stated $B(0, 1) = 0.96839$.
3. Homework BR5 example B: the claim that reflecting without the drift factor is "too high for short horizons, too low for long ones" was wrong. Both 0.375 (no factor) and 0.317 (driftless) are too high, and both tend to 1 as the horizon grows.
4. Section 4.1: generator slots carry 60 marks, not 58 ($12 + 12 + 14 + 14 + 8$).
5. Section 4.3: the format mix listed 6 marks of choice and 4 of `mm-game` quotes that the slots cannot produce (only C3(a) is a choice item). It now gives the fixed formats (choice 2 marks on every form) and the 18 marks whose format varies by form and track.
6. Sample lesson, screen 5: the band of $\sqrt{\frac2n}$ is one standard deviation either side, not $\pm2$ (screen 7 already calls it one standard deviation, about 0.014 at 10,000 steps).
7. Homework MG2 hidden test: growth at twice Kelly is $-1.377 \times 10^{-4}$, outside the stated "within $10^{-4}$ of 0"; the test now states the value.
8. Lesson 33's edge ("approaches $\frac a\mu = 2$") was muddled; lesson 83's edge said a 3-point error "doubles" the position (it raises it by half); lesson 66's 0.0198 is per trading day at 252 a year, now stated.
9. Smaller corrections: EX1's known-wrong payoff named exactly; PZ7 example B's check bounds; QM2 example B's rounding of 15.875; QM6's drill uses an exact $LDL^\top$ factorisation, since Cholesky needs square roots that `Fraction`s cannot hold; TR4 lesson 121's cancellation explained in digits; TR5 example B gains its `#include` lines and was re-run under both compilers at `-O0` and `-O2`; section 4.5 no longer says 2.3 has its own exit line (the curriculum gives it none; the map line and the item's README, tests and results-note rule stand in).
